#6931. Survey: Organizations Report Positive ROI on AI Investments, Yet Data Readiness and Oversight Lag Behind

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

The company's recent State of Enterprise AI Maturity research surveyed 515 U.S.-based AI decision-makers. Just 5% believed their organizations' data was largely ready for AI.

The findings suggest that gaining value from individual AI applications and preparing to operate them more broadly are separate challenges. Reported returns alone do not establish that an organization has the foundations to sustain or expand those results.

Further found that 37% of respondents' organizations had paused, scaled back, or abandoned an underperforming AI initiative in the previous 12 months. Among that group, data quality or availability was the most frequently cited main reason, at 24%.

Meanwhile, 29% reported completing a formal data audit and remediation plan. The research also placed 69% of organizations in the exploring, experimenting, or implementing stages of adoption.

Further distinguishes data prepared for conventional reporting from data prepared for AI. Its explanation emphasizes data origins and documentation, along with access controls that support ongoing evaluation and governance.

For readers assessing their own readiness, the practical issue is whether data supports the requirements of a particular application. A collection suitable for a dashboard may need additional preparation before it can reliably support an AI system.

Seventy-seven percent reported a positive return on AI investment. However, only 3% said their organizations had a documented AI-audit process.

The findings measure different things: perceived financial benefit and the existence of a repeatable oversight process. Neither substitutes for the other.

"In reality, confidence without a documented process is exposure," said Kierra Dotson, associate director of AI strategy and governance at Further. "Closing that gap doesn't take new technology; it requires an auditable governance process."

For the full report, visit the Further site (registration required).

#6927. Researchers Find AI Is Accelerating Vulnerability Discovery

AI-powered vulnerability research is contributing to a major increase in security updates, raising concerns that IT teams could fall behind as patching demands grow, according to cybersecurity company ThreatDown.

In a recent blog post, the company stated that in September 2026, vendors addressed 998 common vulnerabilities and exposures (CVEs) around Patch Tuesday, compared with 96 during the same period last year. In addition, the three-month rolling average of patched Linux kernel vulnerabilities tripled year over year, while Google's Chrome browser experienced a 36-fold increase.

The researchers point to advanced AI models, including Anthropic's Claude Mythos Preview, as a major factor behind the increase in vulnerability discovery.

Introduced in April 2026, Mythos Preview is being used through Project Glasswing, an initiative involving Microsoft, the Linux Foundation and other organizations to identify vulnerabilities in critical software.

While AI could ultimately make software more secure, the cybersecurity company warns that vulnerability discovery is accelerating faster than organizations can deploy fixes.

IT teams must still evaluate patches, test compatibility and schedule updates without disrupting critical business operations.

The challenge is particularly significant for organizations already struggling with patch management.

The cybersecurity vendor cites Verizon's 2026 Data Breach Investigations Report, which found that 35% of known actively exploited vulnerabilities remained unpatched after 28 days in 2025.

Ryan Powell, vice president of IT and security at ThreatDown, said automated patching systems can help organizations manage the growing workload. However, additional patches still require more testing and validation, increasing operational overhead and the risks associated with frequent system changes.

The report also highlights the risks of vulnerabilities in widely used software components such as OpenSSL, embedded in applications, network devices, and security products.

Unlike standalone applications, these components often depend on downstream vendors to release updates before organizations can deploy fixes.

The security firm's analysis points to CVE-2022-0778, an OpenSSL vulnerability patched in March 2022, as an example. Some affected products did not receive updates until several months later.

As AI accelerates vulnerability discovery, these dependencies could delay remediation and extend organizations' exposure to known security flaws.

ThreatDown's findings illustrate a growing challenge for enterprise cybersecurity: AI is making vulnerabilities easier to discover but fixing them remains a complex and time-consuming process.

For the full report, visit the ThreatDown blog here.

#6922. 8 Takeaways from Design Miami and Art Basel in Paris

Inside the week’s standout moments, from Miu Miu to Madonna.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

There was plenty of ground to cover in Paris last week where the French editions of Design Miami and Art Basel took place alongside a full slate of noteworthy openings and events. (“I have 55 invites this week,” said one harried interiors maven in between stops.) Though the protests in Paris had temporarily subsided by the time the fairs began, they remained top of mind. But still, there was a sense of optimism in the air, which could be chalked up to the unifying power of art and design.

This was the fourth consecutive year that Design Miami, Paris was shown at L’Hôtel de Maisons (the one-time home of Karl Lagerfeld), and the second year that it has been led by Jesse Lee, the proprietor of the luxury resale platform Basic. Space. Below, a few of the best moments from the week.

Jeanne Greenberg Rohatyn’s Salon 94 Design received the award for Best Gallery Presentation at Design Miami Paris. The New York gallerist took over the far end of the first floor and filled it with an eclectic mix of pieces: Next to chairs by Alberto Giacometti, she showed furniture—sleek “Stag Stools” replete with protruding antlers—by Rick Owens and Michèle Lamy for the first time. Celebrated artist (and occasional Acne model) Peter Schlesinger quietly stood by as guests admired his large ceramic swan and geese urns, which were fired in a kiln in the garage of his Long Island home. Overlooking it all was a sculptural chandelier by Rome-based designer F. Taylor Colantonio that looked as if it was sliced from the surface of the moon and embedded with gemstones.

For Apple’s second annual Designers of Tomorrow showcase, a quartet of creators were issued a singular prompt: presence, or rather how to hold people’s attention amid the hurried pace of the modern world. The Brooklyn-based artist Isabel Rower created “Padded Chair, 1860,” which was based on a deep dive into 18th- and 19th-century paper-mâché furniture. Fifteen hundred handmade beads lined the bottom of the piece causing more than one showgoer to comment on the work’s ASMR potential. Other participants included Salem S. Charabi, Yoonjeong Lee, and Genki Matsumura and Sebastián Zorrilla of Parafernalia. “It was easy to identify these four designers and give them this platform,” said Ago Interiors founder Rodman Primack who served as the curator of the project.

At Galerie Anne-Sophie Duval in nearby Saint-Germain-des-Prés, the renowned French architect and designer Sylvain Dubuisson presented a series of ogle-worthy pieces: a sleek black desk shellacked with Japanese lacquer, a gold table with metal legs that was designed to oscillate “just for fun,” and another wooden table that was held together vertically by a peg at its center. When the peg was released, the table went horizontal and became a functional dining piece. Another long marble table could be folded into thirds. Even though she no longer runs the gallery (that honor has been bestowed on her daughter, Julie Blum), Duval was on hand on Thursday night to show off Dubuisson’s playful and imaginative inventions.

The new showroom of ELLE Decor Rising Star Hugo Besnier is an enchanting reminder of all that Paris has to offer. Located in a historic loft off a courtyard in the heart of Saint-Germain-des-Prés, the pedigree of previous inhabitants includes Balthus, Sheila Hicks, and even Vanessa Paradis—David Hockney was once a neighbor. In redesigning the property, Besnier took care to include works of these celebrated artists on the walls. The real function of the space is to not only show off his skills as an interior designer, but also his new collection of furniture, Tour de Mains, which works perfectly in the stylized surroundings.

Shattered prismatic forms by Alteronce Gumby at Jeffrey Deitch and new woven wall hangings by Sheila Hicks at Sikkema Malloy Jenkins were among our favorite pieces that bridged both art and objets. On the Palais’s second level, we encountered Marina Faust’s light sculptures, essentially stripped-down chandeliers hanging from a metal frame. Faust, a former photographer in her seventies, partnered with J. & L. Lobmeyr on these works. They were shown at the booth of Vienna-based gallery Gianni Manhattan.

At Hauser & Wirth Paris, a very specific collection pinpointed a certain passage in Alexander Calder’s life. Curated by Sandy Rower, the artist’s grandson and the founder and president of the Calder Foundation, Calder: François Premier is an examination of 1953–54 when the artist returned to live in the French countryside and had a very productive year both in terms of creating mobiles—Les Granettes (1953), and The Putney Mobile (1954)—trippy ink and gouache paintings, and household bric-a-brac like a homemade metal tea strainer.

French filmmaker Alice Diop, in her second collaboration with Miu Miu, presented Geste de Consolation at the Palais d’Iéna where Miu Miu stages its bi-annual runway shows. The project was the director’s first foray into making an exhibition, and the sampling of works packed a powerful punch. The four interconnected vignettes included a powerful 11-minute video directed by Diop; a purple-hued library and living room space with a curation of Diop’s favorite books and authors such as Chantal Akerman and Christina Sharpe; an ode to an African deity in Simone Leigh’s 16-foot-tall sculpture, Sentinel (Mami Wata); and a poignant photograph by Zanele Muholi. Just beyond the library in the makeshift auditorium, Ivorian choreographer Nadia Beugré arranged for live performances, while concerts were conducted by Angel Bat Dawid and the Sistazz of the Nitty Gritty.

The week even yielded a surprise appearance by Madonna, who was in town to celebrate her son Rocco Ritchie’s new exhibition Back on the Brush—a collection of 11 figurative expressionist paintings and sculptures that were shown at Pierre Augustin Rose. On view until November 7, the richly textured works are “rooted in observation, memory, and lived experience.” Says mom: “I love what he is doing!”

Sarah Cristobal is a writer, editor, and creative consultant. She is currently editor-at-large for 10 Magazine USA, among other freelance projects.

#6920. Ransomware Attacks Move Beyond Enterprise Systems to Theaten Employees' Families

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

In a recent announcement, the company highlighted findings from its 2026 Ransomware Risk Report, which examines AI-related attack tactics, personal intimidation, and recovery preparedness. Censuswide surveyed 1,200 information technology and security professionals across nine countries and eight industry sectors in the first half of 2026.

The sample has an important limit: This year's study included only organizations where at least one ransomware attack caused business disruption. The results describe those victims' experiences, rather than the likelihood that an organization will suffer an attack. The report says that sampling approach changed from previous studies.

Semperis sells identity security, recovery and crisis management products.

The report says 84% of respondents experienced attacks that exploited AI in some way. Its breakdown distinguishes AI-based attacks, reported by 40%, from attacks that manipulated victims' own AI agents or systems, reported by 44%. That distinction matters: The broader figure covers both attackers using AI and attackers exploiting AI deployed inside an organization.

Defensive use of AI also produced mixed responses. Nearly half of respondents, 49%, said they successfully used AI for detection or response, while 23% said their AI tools failed to detect or respond successfully. Those are survey responses about victimized organizations, rather than a controlled comparison of security products.

Chris Inglis, former U.S. national cyber director and a Semperis strategic adviser, described the difficulty of recognizing AI's involvement. "Many attacks are not perceived as being aided and abetted by AI because they're just too slick, too sophisticated," he said in the report.

Data erasure, destruction, or release remained the most frequently reported threat, at 56%. Blackmail, extortion or public shaming followed at 41%, with system lockouts or operational sabotage at 40%. The report also recorded psychological intimidation at 23% and physical threats at 17%.

Network-provider trust concerns are impacting enterprise AI plans, with AI and data-driven projects the most affected initiatives among respondents reporting disruption, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

Video game designers spend an enormous amount of time studying what keeps people engaged when something becomes difficult. They have become very good at helping people persist. Schools should pay attention.

#6918. IDC Report Finds Significant Gap Between AI Spending and Measurable Financial Returns

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

The research firm's 2026 Worldwide AI Economics and Cost of Ownership Survey found that 69% reported such a gap, while only 15% had achieved an AI return on investment exceeding 100%.

In a separate May 2026 survey, 73% of respondents identified excessive AI costs as a significant risk.

IDC highlighted the findings in an October 20 analysis examining what enterprise customers expect from AI technology vendors.

Enterprise customers are placing greater emphasis on measurable business outcomes when evaluating AI products, according to IDC.

The firm identified three priorities for vendors: demonstrating financial value, addressing governance and security requirements, and building support beyond IT departments.

The analysis also highlighted the gap between initial agentic AI deployments and broader adoption. Although 80% of organizations have started deploying agentic AI, only 17% are using it across the enterprise.

IDC said evaluating AI investments can be difficult when the technology changes how employees work and how business processes operate.

The firm expects AI infrastructure capacity to catch up with demand around 2029, potentially easing costs. Until then, vendors face pressure to demonstrate the long-term financial value of their products.

AI funding decisions involve a broad range of executives. IDC's September 2026 C-Suite Tech Survey found that CIOs and CTOs were involved in 63% of those decisions, business-function executives in 62%, and chief financial officers in 61%.

A separate IDC survey found that 42% of CEOs planned to hire a chief AI officer within the next 12 months.

Buyers also reported concerns about the products and terms vendors offer. Limited integration with existing systems, insufficient model and data transparency, and concerns about vendor lock-in were each cited by 34% of respondents.

Overpromising and underdelivering, along with unclear pricing models, were each cited by 31%.

IDC said vendors need to substantiate financial returns and address security and governance requirements while working with decision-makers across IT, finance, and other business departments.

For more information, go to the IDC blog.

#6914. Report: Global Ransomware Attacks Rise 23% in Q3

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

The company's Cyber Threat Intelligence Report Review of Q3 2026 recorded 2,865 ransomware victim claims, up from 2,325 in the previous quarter.

Despite the quarterly increase, attacks declined 26% month over month in September to 821. North America recorded the most attacks that month with 390, followed by Europe with 211.

The industrial sector accounted for 29% of quarterly attacks, while Qilin remained the most active ransomware group for the sixth consecutive quarter, recording 365 victims. The Gentlemen followed with 356, and INC Ransom ranked third with 109.

The report highlighted a July cyber attack against a small UK power generation facility that forced operations to stop for approximately four days.

Although the incident did not affect the national electricity grid, it demonstrated how cyber attacks can disrupt physical infrastructure.

The security researchers said the incident was consistent with techniques previously associated with Iranian-affiliated campaigns, including attacks against internet-exposed industrial controllers.

However, the UK government has not formally attributed the attack, and the techniques used remain unconfirmed.

The report also identified growing security challenges as organizations connect traditional IT networks with operational technology (OT) systems for remote maintenance and centralized management.

The firm recommended network segmentation, stronger privileged-access controls, and continuous monitoring of OT environments.

The research found that more than 40 vulnerabilities affecting enterprise applications, VPN appliances, e-mail gateways, and management platforms were added to CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog during the quarter.

The report examined Gunra, a ransomware-as-a-service operation that has targeted 53 victims across multiple industries as of Q3.

According to the cybersecurity company, Gunra exploited vulnerabilities CVE-2024-55591 and CVE-2025-24472 in internet-facing Fortinet devices to gain access to corporate networks.

The group also used stolen credentials, session hijacking, and administrative tools to move between systems before deploying ransomware.

Other emerging ransomware groups included Deadlock and Storm, with the latter claiming 78 victims by the end of September.

Researchers also observed initial access brokers advertising compromised VPN, Remote Desktop Protocol, and SSH access, alongside access to cloud identities, source code repositories, and security management platforms.

The quarterly review also highlighted BraZetsu, an AI-enhanced Windows malware framework that evolved into a specialized initial access toolkit.

According to the report, BraZetsu uses AI models to automate victim profiling, analyze compromised environments, and identify valuable corporate systems.

The framework searches for enterprise software, financial data, digital certificates, and cloud credentials, helping operators identify compromised access that can be sold to other cyber criminals.

The cybersecurity firm also identified risks involving legitimate AI agents, including prompt injection, excessive permissions, and unintended access to external systems.

The report cited incidents involving U.S. and Australian government systems as examples of agents accessing resources beyond their intended scope.

Researchers warned that these incidents do not necessarily involve malicious intent but can create consequences resembling conventional cyber intrusions.

For the full report, visit the NCC Group site.

#6913. The Skirted Furniture Trend Is Taking Over Homes Again, but Designers Say This Is the Right Way to Do It

Frills and flounce are suddenly all the rage.

Pictured above: This primary suite, designed by Elliott Interiors, features skirted furniture that acts as both a playful design detail and a unifying layout element.

Sofas, sinks, and settees: What do they all have in common? They look great in a skirt. A hallmark of ‘80s and ‘90s decor and the traditional English country-house aesthetic, skirted furniture is back. We’ve been quietly watching the trend evolve, with designers pleating, draping, and dressing everything from bed frames to breakfast banquettes.

“With the rise of traditional design becoming more widely accepted, especially by younger generations, details like skirted sinks, skirted dining chairs, and sofas can be seen everywhere,” says Claire Staszak of Centered by Design. “I think because skirted furniture harkens back to something that feels authentic, aged, and charming, it really appeals to me as an aesthetic choice.”

As design moves out of its minimalist era, skirted furniture is here to bring whimsy back. “I think people are having more fun with their homes right now, and adding a skirt to something is a pretty easy upgrade to accomplish,” says Bridget Tiek, principal designer at the Louisiana-based design firm, TIEK BYDAY. “Existing furniture pieces or even architectural items, sinks, cabinets, can be transformed.”

This feeling of transformation is what interior designer Heather French believes is driving this romantic detail back into the modern-day trend cycle. “I think people are craving softness in their lives. Hard edges and echoing rooms don’t give you that, but fabric does. It quiets a room and makes it feel lived-in and welcoming.”

Before you get a little too skirt-happy, though, make sure you’re using them intentionally—it’s easy to go overboard with the flounce. Here’s how designers recommend using furniture skirts throughout the home.

You may love the idea of a furniture skirt, but the wrong fabric (and the way it’s gathered) can ruin it. Always consider your fabric’s colors, lines, and finish. In the above space, Staszak used a pleated skirt to bring character and French-country flair to the scullery of a revamped 1896 Victorian.

“You should also consider when a gathered skirt, straight skirt, pleated skirt, or ruffled skirt is the right look for your interior,” says Staszak. “For instance, a straight skirt can look very chic in a more contemporary setting, whereas a ruffled skirt is much more appropriate for a French or country aesthetic.”

Consider the rest of your home’s aesthetic to guide the best style of skirt to use.

“I’ve used it in traditional cabins and in contemporary homes, and it’s always the application that makes it work. A crisp box pleat reads very differently from a relaxed gathered skirt,” says French.

“For us, it’s all about casual luxury and being thoughtful about where furniture legs actually belong. I don’t like an overly leggy room,” says French.

French and her husband brought softness to this stone-walled New Mexico bathroom using rounded edges, plush fabrics, and a powdery blue hue.

Sorry, maximalists. This is one furniture style that is best used in a restrained manner.

“You probably only need one skirted item per room! Especially if you are going bold with pattern,” says Tiek.

Even the most delicate details can pack a statement. Tiek and her team used burgundy gingham to add color, contrast, depth, and an air of playfulness to the above breakfast nook.

Ditsy florals, gingham, and tartan have been flooding our feeds lately—and these classic patterns make the perfect choice for furniture skirts.

This laundry room by The Misfit House features a sink skirt in a bold coffee-hued gingham, which nicely contrasts the clean lines of the subway tile walls and complements the hues in the splattered tile.

“With all the options for pattern right now in textiles, the skirt offers a great opportunity to showcase fabric in a different way from traditional drapes or window treatments. Because skirts are often gathered, they look good with fabrics that have smaller repeats and more of an all-over pattern,” Staszak explains.

While we love a good DIY, designers say that furniture skirts look their best with impeccable tailoring—so if you don’t have the know-how to make your own, opt for a professional’s touch.

“Have the skirting and any hardware professionally done,” French advises. “DIY versions can easily look sloppy. The more tailored the skirt, the more intentional it looks, and that’s what makes it feel like a design choice rather than a cover-up.”

For instance, this daybed, tucked into a Martha’s Vineyard dressing room by the team at Sees Design, blends in seamlessly because of its perfect seams.

#6912. The Blue Door House from Notting Hill Just Hit the Market

The fictional London home of Hugh Grant’s bashful bookseller is now available for $9.9 million.

It’s perhaps the most famous house in one of London’s most charming neighborhoods, so beloved that crowds are known to gather around it like the English version of Carrie Bradshaw’s West Village apartment. It’s the blue-doored townhouse where Hugh Grant’s fictional bookseller lived and wooed Julia Roberts’s paparazzi-plagued Hollywood actress in 1999’s Notting Hill.

Despite starring in a blockbuster rom-com, the 2,750-square-foot house in question is very much real—and now it can be yours. That is, for roughly $10 million.

Not coincidentally, the three-bedroom residence also once belonged to Richard Curtis, the screenwriter behind Notting Hill, which was directed by Roger Michell and went on to gross some $364 million worldwide.

Behind the recognizable front door—painted a glossy, rich shade of royal blue—the house unfolds with an entrance vestibule and courtyard before reaching a cavernous great room topped by a vaulted ceiling and a row of skylights. Nearly 50 feet in length, the soaring space was originally an artist’s studio and now does triple duty as a kitchen, dining, and reception area. The room has hardwood floors, a stone fireplace, deep-green cabinetry, and a marble-topped island. Two guest rooms can also be found elsewhere on the floor, each with its own private bath.

Upstairs, the primary suite comes with a dual-sink bathroom equipped with both a freestanding tub and a shower. This floor also features a lofted library and office that overlooks the ground level’s central room, plus a spiral staircase that leads to the roof terrace.

Other outdoor spaces include a small balcony off the second floor and an expansive ground-level courtyard. It might not come with a bumbling Hugh Grant, but the house certainly has charm to spare.

The Blue Door House is listed for £7.5 million with Josh Ratcliff of UK Sotheby’s International Realty.

#6908. Microsoft Expands Protections and Controls for Sovereign Cloud

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

In a recent announcement, Douglas Phillips, president and chief technology officer of Microsoft Specialized Clouds, outlined updates spanning public cloud, private cloud, and partner environments. The changes target governments, regulated industries, and organizations that need tighter control over sensitive workloads without abandoning cloud-based AI capabilities.

"Digital sovereignty should give organizations greater control and security over the technology they depend on, while continuing to innovate," Phillips wrote.

One of the biggest additions is Microsoft's Sovereign Control Panel, which will enter public preview at Microsoft Ignite. Available through the Azure portal, the tool will give IT administrators one place to manage data sovereignty settings, spot compliance issues, and identify potential risks that could disrupt operations.

Microsoft is also tightening controls over who can access sensitive systems. Its Data Guardian feature will require oversight by personnel based in Europe when Microsoft engineers access certain production systems in the European Union and European Free Trade Association. Another tool, Production Touch Ledger, will keep tamper-resistant records of that access, giving organizations a clearer picture of who accessed their systems and what they did.

Microsoft is also giving customers more control over encryption. External key management with Azure Managed HSM is now generally available, letting organizations keep their main encryption keys on hardware they own or manage outside Microsoft's cloud.

The company is also expanding its Sovereign Private Cloud offerings, giving organizations more options to run systems with or without an internet connection. New capabilities include GitHub Enterprise Local, GitHub Copilot with locally hosted AI models, and additional data services running on Azure Local.

The push for greater operational control comes as Microsoft is also reconsidering how enterprises should secure increasingly autonomous AI systems.

As reported by Redmond magazine, Microsoft is expanding Project Perception to address risks posed by what the company calls "agentic insiders," AI agents capable of accessing sensitive information and performing actions without constant human supervision.

That initiative introduces plans for Guardian Agents, specialized security agents designed to monitor other AI systems, evaluate their behavior and intervene when their actions become risky.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," said Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security.

Project Perception is about limiting what AI agents can do. Microsoft's sovereign cloud expansion tackles a different set of questions: where those systems run, who gets to administer them, and how the data stays protected.

Taken together, the two efforts point to a shift in Microsoft's enterprise AI strategy. The company is putting more weight on oversight, not just automation.

Phillips said AI is making these questions more pressing as organizations roll out intelligent systems across sensitive business processes and critical infrastructure.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

#6907. A Jewel-Toned Chicago Home Inspired by Pakistani Culture

Kate Haynes of Noble Studio brought bold personality to her client’s family home.

A room painted in rich eggplant isn’t exactly a subtle introduction. But in this Wilmette, Illinois, home, that’s precisely the point. Just beyond the foyer, a dining room drenched in Farrow & Ball’s Brinjal, complete with a glossy lacquered ceiling, offers the first sign that the designer at the wheel had little interest in playing it safe. And, as Kate Haynes of Noble Studio quickly discovered, neither did her client.

The pair connected over Instagram as construction on the family’s 6,500-square-foot home neared completion. With the architectural details and hard finishes already in place, Haynes was tasked with giving the new build some personality. Luckily, the homeowner, a former graphic designer, had plenty to spare. “We talked about incorporating deeply saturated colors along with a lot of personal touches,” recalls Haynes. “She is one of the warmest, most delightful people, and her home totally reflects her personality.”

What Haynes created for the family is a masterclass in exuberance, informed in part by the homeowner’s Pakistani heritage. Rather than lean into overt cultural references, Haynes borrowed from the richness of Mughal architecture and the bold, jewel-toned palettes of South Asia, weaving both together throughout the five-bedroom home. In the daughter’s bedroom, a scalloped headboard recalls a multifoil arch; elsewhere, vintage rugs lend the new construction a sense of history. “We wanted to have that original depth that can only come from a textile that has seen some life and years,” Haynes explains.

There’s plenty of levity, too: a hanging chair in the blue reading room, a rock wall in the playroom, and a custom circular banquette that rivals the best restaurant in town. Each detail speaks to a family that takes its pleasures seriously, whether that’s gathering with friends for dinner or curling up with a book. “She was just a person who looks for things to be delighted by,” Haynes says of her client. Consider the house a reflection of that philosophy.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Noble StudioBuilder: Sterling Wilson DevelopmentLocation: Wilmette, ILThe Space: A five-bedroom, seven-bathroom home across 6,500 square feet

An expansive crane mural sets a serene, transportive mood in the primary suite, where “subtle eastern influence and massive graphic appeal” establish an earthy palette, says Haynes. The blush pink upholstered bed is flanked by khaki green Lawson-Fenning nightstands, while a rustic vintage Indian teak bench adds a timeworn counterpoint to the room'så more refined furnishings.

Cornflower blue wainscoting and patterned Penny Morrison wallpaper establish a cheerful backdrop in the daughter’s bedroom, where saffron yellow accents tie the space into the rest of the home’s palette. “The homeowner had seen an inspirational image with geometric wainscoting topped with a patterned wallpaper, and it really resonated with her, so she asked us to run with that idea,” adds Haynes.

“We wanted to create serene escapes in each of the two guest rooms that we worked on,” explains Haynes of this enveloping escape, swathed in a taupey pink. “We loved the Penny Morrison block printed graphic fabric that is on the drapes—the coppery brown is so rich against the quieter wall.”

“Our client is an avid reader, and she had wanted to create a dreamy blue space with a hanging chair where she can eventually cultivate plants that will become a living, breathing part of this space,” says Haynes. A Murphy bed is built into the bookshelves for additional sleeping space when the family has a full house, while the graphic sofa plays into the client’s adventurous side.

Designed to keep the couple’s daughter entertained through Chicago’s long winters, the playroom is equal parts imagination and movement. A modular foam sofa can be reconfigured into a fort or gymnastics landing pad, while a custom climbing wall encourages active play. Haynes’s favorite detail? A hand-crocheted saffron yellow swing that brings the home’s recurring signature hue into its most playful space.

Drama was on the menu in the dining room, where walls painted in Farrow & Ball’s Brinjal meet a matching lacquered ceiling. Getting the glossy aubergine finish just right took several attempts, but the result was worth the trouble.

In the home’s soaring living room, Haynes tempered the airy architecture with a collection of tactile, earthy furnishings. Seafoam sofas dressed in block-printed pillows mingle with woven armchairs, a pair of travertine coffee tables, and a vintage African stool. The mix lends visual weight to the expansive space without sacrificing the comfortable, family-friendly atmosphere.

A deep archway visible from the front door offered the perfect opportunity for a little surprise. Haynes tucked a pair of custom banquettes around a circular dining table, upholstering the snug seating in a saffron-and-blue block print sourced from Etsy. The palette echoes the nearby reading room, while the clever configuration makes everyday meals feel wonderfully intimate.

Founded originally in 2018 as Kate Haynes Interiors and reborn in 2023 as Noble Studio, Kate Haynes has been honing a fascination with spaces, architecture, and art since childhood. Her company has always been about beautiful spaces (naturally), but the rebirth as Noble Studio comes with a sense of mature intentionality to create quiet luxury for clients. Kate brings to her team a relentless commitment to the intangible result of joy present in the aesthetics and flow of a place, no matter what style it wears.

#6904. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.

Network-provider trust concerns are impacting enterprise AI plans, with AI and data-driven projects the most affected initiatives among respondents reporting disruption, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

Video game designers spend an enormous amount of time studying what keeps people engaged when something becomes difficult. They have become very good at helping people persist. Schools should pay attention.

#6898. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

Network-provider trust concerns are impacting enterprise AI plans, with AI and data-driven projects the most affected initiatives among respondents reporting disruption, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

At the same time, organizations are putting strict conditions around autonomy, making governance and control the next major challenge for enterprise AI adoption.

#6895. Before & After: How an Artist and Architect Couple Turned the “Worst House” on the Block Into Their Dream Home

In Brooklyn, artist Katherine Keltner and architect Douglas Gauthier transform a crumbling townhouse with equal parts drama and restraint.

Before they stepped foot inside the crumbling brownstone on a landmark block in Brooklyn’s Boerum Hill, artist Katherine Keltner and architect Douglas Gauthier had toured 30 homes and browsed more than 200 online. Neither of them expected to become instantly captivated by the 1869 Italianate townhouse, which was being sold in a stately yet dilapidated condition, having once served as a single-room occupancy (SRO) residence. But it happened nonetheless.

“This was the first one that had the bones that Katherine wanted,” recalls Gauthier, who runs the firm GAuthier Architects. “It follows the classic real estate rule of buying the worst house on the best block.”

The strategy paid off: Once their offer was accepted, the couple embarked on an 18-month-long odyssey that saw them jump through a series of hoops (preservationists; concerned neighbors; skeptical engineers) to completely transform the moldering pile into a creative haven for them to live, work, and raise their daughter.

A true joint-effort, Gauthier oversaw a modest two-story rear expansion and structural alterations, while Keltner took charge of spatial arrangements as well as color, pattern, texture, and artwork—much of it her own.

Once deteriorating, the interiors now have an easy elegance, with plaster walls, arched passageways, and curved corners that give the soaring ceilings an intimate feel. Pink takes an unexpected starring role, appearing throughout the parlor floor and also in the zellige-clad primary bath, but other adventurous hues like mint green and crimson also feature prominently. Capping it off is a plywood-sheathed office for Gauthier’s practice, allowing the family retreat to double as a place of creative inspiration.

Below, Keltner and Gauthier share their biggest lessons (and some “before” pictures) from the painstaking passion project.

When the townhouse was converted to an SRO, it had been chopped up into at least 13 units and two kitchens, and few of the original decorative elements were still intact. A couple had bought it in the 1960s and made it a family home again and added an art gallery space. But by 2021, when Keltner and Gauthier first saw it, the house was in a state of severe neglect, with peeling paint, crumbling floors, ancient appliances—not to mention a 1,500-pound printing press in the studio (that the couple later preserved). Yet, Keltner and Gauthier saw an opportunity, not to recreate the past, but rather forge their own set of rules.

“The house wasn’t burdened with pristine historic details—our moldings were trashed,” says Gauthier. “There were a few things that we kept and celebrated, but to me, it was like a tabula rasa. We can just go in and make it ours.” Katherine agreed: “I feel like sometimes having to preserve things is more expensive than just starting clean,” she says. “And so I thought that was an advantage for us.”

Not having to be too precious with ornamentation allowed the couple to make a bold statement about crown moldings, eschewing tradition and installing curving corners and coved ceilings instead. Nowhere does the effect have more impact than in the parlor, where Gauthier devised a ceiling light in the shape of a giant oval that casts light upward rather than down. “I really like indirect lighting,” he says.

Crafted out of laser-cut plywood, the elliptical fixture is an abstracted homage to the ceiling medallions found in neighboring historic brownstones. “Some of them are incredibly ornate and beautiful, but they don't really have any use—they’re just decoration,” says Gauthier. “Adding ambient lighting to the living space takes that historical reference in pocket.”

Once they were through the process of changing the structure’s zoning from an SRO to a single-family home, Gauthier and Keltner had to contend with its landmarked status, meaning a series of approvals were required for any additions or exterior modifications.

The couple originally planned to construct a three-story extension in the back, but they ended up compromising on two floors. This expansion includes a bump-out for the parlor floor’s kitchen, leading to a rear deck that steps down to the garden. On top, a private terrace connects to the primary bath. “Instead of having that third story, we now have a deck off of our primary suite,” says Keltner. “It’s kind of amazing,” adds Gauthier.

One decision the couple wrestled with mightily was whether to replace the original, 150-year-old pine flooring with ebony-stained white oak. “The floors were part of the reason we liked the house so much,” says Gauthier. But their contractor talked them out of keeping them. “It’s a lot of extra work for them,” says Keltner. “They have to take it up, save it, put something else down, and then put it back. I think we reached a point in time and money where we were like, Okay, fine.”

Another flooring regret was not adding underfoot heating. “I was pushing the mechanical engineers to do radiant heating and radiant cooling,” says Gauthier. “Radiant heat is really lovely—you have warmth under your feet all the time.” But the mechanics behind the system proved complicated and technically redundant, as the home was already configured with a forced-air HVAC system.

“In doing research and other projects, it’s clear that the cost-to-pleasure ratio would have been worth it,” he says now. “We regret not putting the warm floors in.”

While windows might sometimes be an afterthought, Keltner and Gauthier have advice: Go with triple-glazed. “Windows provide heat and cool insulation as well as security,” explains Keltner. “And they can define both the outside and the inside of a space.” The couple went with triple-glazed windows from Austrian-based firm Bewiso that come pre-wired for easy connection to alarm systems. “They’re almost like hurricane windows,” she says.

“You can't break through three panes of glass,” says Gauthier. Due to the building’s landmark status, they had to use historic-style windows on the front facade. “We were able to get these old-fashioned type of windows that when you close them, they feel like you're closing a Mercedes,” says Gauthier. “They completely silence the outdoors.”

The final piece of advice is perhaps the most crucial: “Hire the best contractor you can afford,” says Gauthier.

“The typical advice is to get a few bids and throw out the lowest and the highest,” says Keltner. “People usually take the second lowest.” However, that could be the determining factor between an on-time project and one that drags. “You’ll end up paying the difference both in money and time,” she says. “It’s often hard for people to bite the bullet and go with the slightly higher price, but it really pays off.”

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6892. This California Cottage Puts a Charming Spin on the "Summer Camp Nostalgia" Aesthetic

Nostalgia rules in this 1930s Northern California home.

When Corine Maggio and her husband bought their 1930s cottage in Mill Valley, California, it was meant to be their starter home. Coming in at about 1,500 square feet, with an awkwardly laid-out kitchen, living room, and entryway, it didn’t seem to be an ideal place to raise their two children—that was, until they realized how rare it was to have a view, a yard, and walkability. Before they knew it, they were falling in love with their home—and all the potential it held.

“The house has an energy you can’t just recreate,” Maggio explains. With three bedrooms and two bathrooms, there was technically enough room for their family of four, and the architectural bones could easily meld with the relaxed, “summer camp in Maine” aesthetic Maggio chases in every project. “We needed to get smart about space planning and bring back a significant amount of character that had been stripped away,” she adds. In other words, the layout had to be revamped, and nearly every wall needed a dose of paneling, paint, or wallpaper—or preferably, some combination of the three.

The kitchen was first on the chopping block. Sandwiched between the laundry room and the dining room, the space felt cut off from the rest of the house in a way that wasn’t conducive to their family-focused, indoor-outdoor lifestyle. Maggio’s solution was to remove the laundry room wall, giving the kitchen access to the backyard, and finish the space with a painted beadboard ceiling to lend it a rustic, timeworn feel.

Mixed patterns, layered textures, and natural materials became Maggio’s way of capturing that summer camp nostalgia without creating a space that felt too formal for everyday life. The narrow living room lent itself well to a circular seating arrangement; when paired with a canopy-inspired wallcovering, the layout mirrored the act of sitting around a campfire to tell stories. Throughout, vintage furniture mingles with custom pieces, and personal touches abound, from the butcher block Maggio found on Facebook Marketplace to a chair left behind by a previous owner.

“I want people to come in and feel something familiar,” Maggio says. “I want them to feel comfortable being themselves, knowing nothing in the house is more precious than the people.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Corine MaggioLocation: Mill Valley, CaliforniaThe Space: A 1930 cottage with three bedrooms and two bathrooms.

Located in the center of the action—and boasting the best views of the water—this multifunctional space was designed as a place for family and guests to gather while Maggio and her husband cook.

After reviewing more than 30 wallpaper samples, Maggio finally selected this woodland scene by Bunny Williams Home, her least favorite of the bunch until she held it up in the space and saw the transformation it would make.

To visually lighten the galley kitchen, upper cabinets flanking the Ilve range were fitted with glass, breaking up the run of solid cabinetry and creating a more open feel. The butcher-block island is from the 1940s and weighs over 400 pounds.

Originally, there wasn’t a proper mudroom, only a three-walled entry open to the elements. Maggio enclosed it, painting the new beadboard-covered walls in Swans Mill Cream by Benjamin Moore, a cheery yellow that glows in the sunlight.

Maggio’s home office is a detached Tuff Shed in the backyard, to which she added paneling and Soane Britain wallpaper for an English garden vibe. Pinned-up magazine pages on the wall serve as both inspiration and art.

Maggio’s daughter, June, “personifies joy,” so she wanted her retreat to promote imagination and fun. Filled with vintage pieces and clad in a Pierre Frey wallpaper, it feels nostalgic and youthful.

To accentuate the room’s vaulted ceiling, Maggio switched from vertical nickel groove panels to horizontal ones, carrying them up to the room’s peak.

In a controversial move, the designer reduced the room’s square footage and opted for one sink instead of two. “We benefit significantly more from the counter space and storage,” she says. A vintage dresser gets new life as a vanity.

When Corine Maggio started her eponymous design firm, she knew she wanted to create homes for her clients that felt warm, character-rich, and deeply personal. Her goal isn’t to simply design spaces that look beautiful and collected, but to improve how people feel in their daily lives.

#6887. This Heritage Pattern Is Replacing Flat, Beige Walls—Here’s How Designers Are Using It in 2026

The centuries-old motif also makes lampshades, rugs, and seating feel bespoke.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

In an age of encroaching AI, designers are turning to heritage looks to create bespoke interiors with personality. That may be one reason decorative marbling is having a moment.

"People are tired of flat, safe walls,” says designer Cara Woodhouse. “After years of greige, clients want surfaces with a point of view, and marbling has an artisanal history behind it. It's also flexible: it can read painterly, graphic, or soft depending on the colorway."

Its versatility is part of the appeal. “It doesn't necessarily have to be traditional or even executed on paper,” adds designer Sasha Bikoff. “It's really about the movement of color and the organic, unpredictable patterns that emerge, which can translate beautifully across wallpaper, furniture, textiles, and decorative objects.”

The paper marbling technique has a long history. In 10th-century Japan, artisans created “floating ink” designs by placing ink on water and manipulating it as it moved. By the 15th century, the practice was known in Turkey as “the art of clouds,” before making its way to Venetian and Florentine bookbinders.

The painterly look is appealing because its undulating lines feel expressive and vibrant without harsh edges, says Jo Plant, chief creative officer at Pooky, which is a brand well-known for its marbled lampshades.

Designers are bringing marbling into all kinds of interiors, from murals and upholstery to lampshades and even sink skirts. Analisse Taft-Gersten, cofounder of the 1818 Collective, used billowy marbled silk to elevate a powder room. And studios like Rule of Three are experimenting with the technique in floral fabrics that resemble vines.

Should you need further convincing, Woodhouse puts it best: "Marbling is pattern without repetition. No two passes are the same, so a wall reads more like art than wallpaper. It brings depth and a handmade quality to a room that might otherwise feel too finished."

Here are nine designer-approved ways to bring marbled motifs into your home.

Designer Michelle Fahmy of Haus of Meeshie likes things cheeky and playful. So it was a no-brainer to introduce groovy swirls into a Culver City pied-à-terre via a large-scale mural from Mitchell Black.

“I wanted something dramatic for the stairwell wall which bleeds into the living room and is the first thing you see when you walk in the door,” she says. “When I came across this wallpaper, it stopped me in my tracks! Usually, you see this paper marbling technique done on fabric or in smaller applications, like a lampshade, so this was unusual in scale and exactly the conversation starter I was looking for.”

For Bikoff, interior design should feel like a natural extension of a home’s architectural language. To bring her own sense of color, movement, and personality into a project within a Zaha Hadid building, she opted for Pierre Frey wallpaper with a liquid, dripping marble effect rather than a traditional, tightly swirled pattern.

“I love how the elongated, flowing lines create a sense of movement, almost as though the walls are melting or the colors are cascading down the surface,” Bikoff says. “Traditional marbling can feel very classical, whereas this interpretation feels more contemporary, psychedelic, and expressive.”

The dialogue between the wallpaper and Hadid's architecture is all about challenging conventional forms and embracing organic movement. “I wanted the room to feel immersive, almost like stepping inside a piece of abstract art,” she adds.

It was love at first sight. Marta St. Amour, interior designer and project manager at Braswell Design + Build was enchanted by the beautiful movement and layered tones of amber, soft blue, and sandy beige portrayed in the Lee Jofa Taplow wallpaper.

“The pattern became the inspiration for the bathroom's color palette, tying together the warm veining of Taj Mahal quartzite, rich caramel-colored shower tile, and soft blue tile behind the freestanding tub,” she says.

“We wanted the tub to feel like a statement piece, and the wallpaper's organic, flowing pattern complements its sculptural shape and warm metallic finish.”

To capture the spirit of the marbling technique but in a contemporary way, Bikoff opted for resin chairs with the telltale pattern in the above space.

“The way the colors blend and swirl through the resin gives them this incredible sense of depth and movement, almost like a liquid material that has been frozen in time,” she says.

Juxtaposing materials can challenge the perception of design. Here, the chairs feel like functional sculptures as they balance the visual richness of marble and the playful transparency of resin.

“They bring together color, texture, and fluidity while reinforcing the playful, futuristic spirit of the space."

If a full room of swirls is a bit much for you, settle on something smaller-scale, such as a lampshade. Pooky’s hand-marbled shades show off the dance of acrylic paint.

“Marbling is having a resurgence because we’re all craving craft, texture, and character in our homes,” says Plant. “People want pieces that feel human and handmade rather than mass-produced.” Adding a marbled shade is like decor candy that can add just the right amount of maximalism.

What makes the marbling technique unique is that the patterns are inherently organic, and so they lend themselves to pairing well with other organic shapes. Taft-Gersten anchored this powder room with a silk sink skirt featuring Rule of Three fabric that almost looks like it was kissed by bubbles.

“There’s a rhythm to it, but also an irregularity that keeps your eye moving; it never feels rigid,” Taft-Gersten says. The print also echoes the convex pebble mirrors above.

This girl’s bedroom may look dreamy, but that’s not what Cara Woodhouse was after when she landed on cotton candy-like Merenda Wallpaper by artist Michelle Weinberg.

“I wanted movement,” Woodhouse says. “A kid's room should feel alive, and marbled paper gives the walls energy without a single literal motif. I'd call it soft modern with an edge: rounded forms, blush tones, and a pattern that keeps it from going saccharine. The swirl does the work so the furniture can stay simple and sculptural."

The look of poured paint over a rug is one that will surely raise an eyebrow. Floor_Story designer Bekah Smith was experimenting with duct tape when she stumbled upon unexpected patterns that look like creases and shadows.

“There’s a similar sense of spontaneity to marbling, where patterns emerge through experimentation rather than being entirely predetermined,” Smith says.

For those looking for a ripple effect without the psychedelic swirls, consider ceramics as a way to dabble in the style without being so literal. Henry Holland Studio employs a traditional Japanese ceramic technique, nerikomi, to create irregular earthenware marbled motifs.

The pattern is created by stacking, folding, and rolling colored clay into slabs, then hand-molding them into functional shapes like lamp bases, bowls, and vases.

#6886. The Gilded Age Is Back—Here’s Everything to Know About Season 4

Season four has arrived with familiar faces, new players, and plenty at stake.

With dazzling Newport mansions, an ever-changing social hierarchy, and even a John Singer Sargent appearance, HBO’s The Gilded Age has lasting popularity. It’s a series that knows its strengths—eye candy and society drama—and continues to lean in. The third season, which ended on August 10, 2025, delved further into the conflicts between old and new money, the politics of marriage, and even the era’s technological advances.

But fans want more—and they’re in luck. The Gilded Age returned for its fourth season on November 1, bringing viewers back into the lavish homes and complicated lives of New York’s social elite. With the premiere now streaming and seven more episodes to come, there’s plenty of society drama still ahead. Here’s what to know about the Julian Fellowes show’s fourth season, from the release schedule to the new faces joining the cast.

Season 4 premiered Sunday, November 1 on HBO, with episodes also available to stream on HBO Max. The season consists of eight episodes, released weekly on Sundays through the December 20 finale.

The official season 4 logline reads: “Bertha Russell (Carrie Coon) changed Society at a cost. Now, her family must reckon with the consequences as Agnes van Rhijn (Christine Baranski) seizes an opportunity to regain her position. Meanwhile, Marian (Louisa Jacobson) forges a new path for herself, and Peggy (Denée Benton) works to be accepted by her future in-laws. In this new era, you must be careful what you wish for.”

Horrors! Those of us who grew up in the Commonwealth (God save it!) find the outraged innocence of the governor—and that of the NYT—pretty damn hilarious. Steve M. of No More Mister Nice Blog, also a local lad, compared the ploy to a similar gambit in a Massachusetts governor race. As is the case with the two Dan Sullivans in Alaska, there were two Edward Kings on the ballot differing only in their middle names. It has no effect on the eventual election; the Democratic Ed King won by about 100,000 and was a terrible governor, by the way.

And if you need your shenanigans committed by historical icons, remember that, in 1946, when John F. Kennedy first ran for Congress, two candidates named Joe Russo mysteriously turned up against him in the Democratic primary. One of the Joe Russos was a Boston city councilor and a popular local politician. The other one was a janitor. Local historians long ago revealed that Janitor Joe was paid by the Kennedy campaign to run in the primary and split the Joe Russo vote. JFK won in a landslide anyway. Voters are not as dumb as Alaska Republicans say they are.

One more election item. Up in Minnesota, they’ve rousted a notorious ratfcker. From NBC News:

Oh my, James. Get that pardon payoff in order. Natalie is standing by.

Up until James of Arabia showed up, the voucher system had been perfectly adequate for Minnesotans. It allowed people in the same precinct to vote essentially by proxy for those people who otherwise could not vote because, for example, they’d just moved and don’t yet have acceptable proof of residence. At its best, it was an act of kindness, which to people like James O’Keefe is like garlic to a vampire.

These people demanded a court order, got one, and then appealed it? Does anyone here know how to play this game?

This is your democracy, America. Cherish it.
