#6883. What Happens When You Take a Major GOP Donor and a Liberal Environmentalist Fishing? Something Beautiful, Actually

Doug Deason and Luke Metzger have almost nothing in common politically. Get them outdoors, though, and they’ll teach you a thing or two about bipartisanship. And about angling.

There are few Curtis Creeks in this life, so when you find it, keep its secret well…

—Sheridan Anderson, The Curtis Creek Manifesto

Let me tell you about these two men, Doug Deason and Luke Metzger. Two men who seem like they should be yelling at each other in a Buc-ees. Men who achieved something amazing together.

Where to start? Well, let’s see. Deason is a Trump guy. Actually, more salient: Deason is a Ken Paxton guy. And not just one of his guys but one of his big donors. Ken Paxton, yes, Texas’s embattled attorney general and current Republican candidate for United States Senate. Paxton, charitably, isn’t the best candidate the GOP fielded these midterms, but Trump wanted him in the race, and Trump got what he wanted, and now Paxton is locked in the race of his life, at real risk of losing to a candidate whose party hasn’t won a Senate seat in Texas in thirty-eight years. Earlier in the race, none of the GOP donor establishment wanted to support Paxton. But Deason did, out of a mix of genuine friendship and an affinity for some MAGA priorities, especially the ones that want to see the branches of the federal government pruned back.

There’s also that: Deason is a major Republican donor. You’ll see stories that call him a “megadonor.” Unfair, perhaps. He prefers “activist,” and while his father was a billionaire GOP donor (Darwin Deason sold an IT company to Xerox for $6.4 billion in 2010, providing plenty of funds for his political interests), there are people who give far more. Deason says in a two-year cycle he usually gives between $1.5 million and $2 million; Elon Musk has reportedly put more than $150 million behind Paxton in this race, via super PAC. But in Texas’s vast GOP machine, Deason greases many gears.

Then Metzger. Well. I mean. He’s the executive director of Environment Texas, the state chapter of a nationwide environmental nonprofit. Paxton “routinely sues the federal government to try and block environmental initiatives—for example, suing to undo clean-water protections,” Metzger says. Is that enough? No? Environment Texas describes itself with phrases like “guide our country onto a greener, healthier path” and “grassroots support” and “allow nature to thrive” and “infinite economic growth on a finite planet is neither wise nor possible.”

When I met Metzger, he was driving his college-age son’s Chevy Volt, an early plug-in hybrid.

When I met Deason, he was driving a blacked-out Ford F-150 Raptor, a pickup truck that is a sports car.

Deason is on the board of Galaxy, a data center and digital-infrastructure company that is building Helios, a major data center in Dickens County, near Lubbock.

Metzger? Metzger carefully says that “we’re not saying data centers are inherently bad or we shouldn’t have them but want to set the rules of the road first before it’s too late.”

Or how about this one? Deason thinks wind power—Texas is by far the nation’s most productive state—needs a closer look because of all the birds turbines kill. Metzger thinks the carbon pollution forgone, compared with fossil fuels, is far more valuable to avians (and everyone else). A fight about it on Twitter, in fact, is how they met.

Three years ago, Texans voted for a constitutional amendment to create a $1 billion fund, the Centennial Parks Conservation Fund, to buy and develop land into state parks. Before winning over voters, the legislation made it through both chambers of the state’s legislature and was signed by Governor Greg Abbott. Who masterminded the whole thing? Doug Deason and Luke Metzger. After their meet-cute by the windmill, Metzger decided to reach out privately about raising money for parks. He was savvy enough to understand that, whatever else, Deason was an outdoorsman. Deason agreed. Metzger worked the green lobby. Deason deployed his influence. Two years later: $1 billion. Sounds like a fish story.

That’s the way you might think, anyway. In 2026. When the midterm election is a partisan exercise par excellence, pitting a morally challenged good ol’ boy against a lab-created liberal Christian and attracting more than half a billion dollars in donations while attempting to draw the lines between people that seem to arise as a matter of course these days. Doesn’t it seem as if no one wants to be with the people who disagree with them? Political opponent, acquaintance, friend, or even family member?

But against that backdrop, one of Texas’s greatest and most recent bipartisan successes is starting to pay dividends. In May, the state acquired a ranch of more than fifty thousand acres, which will one day be the second-biggest state park in Texas. And in September, Post Oak Ridge State Park, which was acquired before the Centennial Fund but developed with its monies, opened for day use—the first new Centennial Fund park available to the public.

And anyway, to think that way is to ignore one simple fact: All of these reasons for Deason and Metzger to be enemies are about politics. And there’s so much more to life than politics.

Which is what Deason and Metzger were doing the very day I met up with the two of them and Deason’s communications advisor, Thomas Graham. On September 21—forty-three days before the midterm election—we went fishing at Palo Pinto Mountains State Park, an hour and a half west of Dallas. Fly-fishing, in particular. Metzger had only been a couple times, and the closest I’d gotten to it was a funny numbness in my jaw at “I am haunted by waters.” But Deason had been fly-fishing for fifty-four years, so he took it upon himself to teach us.

We’d left Dallas, where Deason is based, at 5:30 a.m. and arrived at the park to find a perfect morning. I’d flown in from New York girded for 100 degree days, and this might still have become one, but the morning was cool. “Cool”—70 degrees. We parked above the park’s boat ramp and the fishing pier and walked down carrying rods and tackle. The pier arced a couple hundred feet between two points on the shore of Tucker Lake. The water was calm, gently bunched by ripples of wind or rent by the oscillation of a water snake. The lights were on and the sky was beginning to grow big by then, the way the sky does in Texas.

Fly-fishing, it goes without saying, is not bait fishing. I didn’t fully understand that until I saw Deason preparing the fly rods. We had two—one his, one Graham’s. The line on the reel, which an inexperienced fisherman like me could confuse for a lime-green version of the standard stuff my dad picked up at Walmart when I was a kid, is in fact deceptively complex. It’s engineered with four parts—backing, the fly line, the leader, and the tippet—so that an angler can land a fly in the precise pocket of water where hungry fish reside without any but the smartest fish able to connect the whole system back to the guy with the creel at his feet.

Fly-fishing, in other words, is a sport of cunning. Jabbing a nightcrawler onto a hook and dropping it into the water is not unlike bombing an electorate with rage-bait political ads—playing on the most primal, knee-jerk evolutionary instincts. Fly-fishing? It’s a sport of persuasion.

Anyway, this is all very fussy to set up. As Deason got to work, he almost immediately began to apologize for the poor quality of his leader, the lack of appropriately sized line, and so on and so forth. He was working at a wooden bench in the middle of the pier, upon which he’d spread his voluminous fishing tackle—line, clippers, forceps, boxes and boxes of flies—and human tackle—jerky from Buc-ees, a Yeti full of waters. He gave me a spinning reel, a bait-fishing rod, to play with while he worked.

I took up a position near Metzger and practiced a cast. Tall and scruffy at fifty years old, he was dressed in flannel, shorts above the knees, crew socks, and sturdy boots. Which is maybe a caricature of a crunchy environmentalist but is also simply what one wears if they’re expecting to get outdoors. Metzger and Deason had been telling me about their initial meeting, so wind power was on my mind.

“Is there offshore wind in the Gulf?” I asked, metronomically casting out and reeling in.

“Not yet,” Metzger said. “Not anytime soon, either,” he added, seeming rueful.

“I mean, I don’t even know if it makes sense there,” I said. “I’m just curious.”

“To me, the debates about offshore wind are interesting,” I said.

“Yeah,” Deason said, hearing our conversation. He couldn’t help himself. “Well—” he started to jump in.

I finished my thought first. It seemed to me that the real, if at times unspoken, barrier to offshore wind around us, in New York, was NIMBYs who didn’t want blemishes on their horizon line. “But there’s huge shipping traffic coming in and out,” I said. “You don’t have some pristine, unblemished view when you look out on the water anyway.”

“Well, supposedly they mess with whales,” Deason said. “I don’t know if that’s true or not,” he admitted—could be alternative facts.

Metzger picked this right up. “I mean, any offshore disturbance can impact whales, but you know, offshore drilling certainly is, and the boats—that’s one of the big concerns, that boats can drive so fast there’ll be collisions.”

Graham had been preparing his rod, and he got Metzger’s attention. “You want to use this rod to try to fly-fish?” he asked.

“Oh, sure.” Metzger said. And just like that, offshore wind was tabled.

“I wish I had this magazine, Luke. I’m going to send you one. It’s called The Curtis Creek Manifesto.” Deason had already mentioned it once before. It was written by a hippie, he’d said, but it had its insights. Granted, fly-fishing usually happens at water level, and we were up on a pier. And usually you’re working with moving water, building a strategy around the way fish face upstream, waiting for sustenance to come floating down. Still, “it’s just logic,” Deason said of the Manifesto. Still useful. “It’s just common-sense little things. So you know: Control the line.”

The three of them, Graham, Metzger, and Deason, fiddled with the fly rod and adjusted the slack cascading off the reel.

Deason got Metzger set, then came to work with me instead. We got a little lost for a minute, looking at the open water on the lake side of the pier, across to the hills of the other side, monochromatic green under a pale-blue sky painted with wisps of white like an old-timey movie backdrop. The temperature was still in the 70s. “God’s country,” a friend from high school, who’d decamped from California to Texas, used to remind me when I visited. Indeed it was.

Deason held the cork handle of the second fly rod, the one I was going to use, in his right hand and pulled line off the reel with his left until his hand was ringed with a nimbus of fly line. Then he started showing me the motion. He’s sixty-four years old, but Deason has the compact, solid build of someone who has never gone long without physical activity. His movements were efficient, even elegant. Arm more or less straight, maybe cocked a little at the elbow. The rod is what bends. Arm comes up, back just behind the head, then after the slightest second of a pause pendulums forward. Maybe a flick of the wrist. Line shoots.

He let me try. I was not elegant. The line came off the rod for me with no carry, the way a paper airplane thrown carelessly dives as soon as the force of the throw dissipates.

I kept trying, and Deason turned to watch Metzger, who was casting repeatedly. He offered a minor correction to his form.

“I heard someone said, like, it’s a martini glass,” Metzger said. “So like here, to here.” His fly rod precisely described the shape contained by the sloping sides of a cocktail glass.

“Oh yeah!” Deason said. “Yeah, yeah! Looks like a martini. That’s good! I’ve never heard that.”

That morning, on the drive from Dallas, Metzger got to telling Deason about a guy he knew who was trying to buy a ranch up near Amarillo to turn into a bison range, which he hoped to staff with formerly incarcerated people, to give them opportunities on the outside. Deason was interested, doubly so—criminal-justice reform is one of his passions, the one where he cut his teeth on bipartisan politicking.

So the guy asked, Metzger was saying, if he had any idea how to get money from the state. “And I’m like, Ask Doug Deason.”

“I think so,” Metzger said. “Yeah, and you know, if we weren’t just spending the interest on the Centennial Fund…”

There was a digression, because Graham knew a guy with a bison herd. And he was a guy Metzger knew too, because between these three men they seemed to know all 30 million people in the state. And so—

Deason cut back in. “What’d you just say?” He was stuck on something. “Are they still trying to say they’re just gonna spend the interest?”

Yeah, Luke said. It was a billion-dollar fund, but they—the lieutenant governor, maybe, or the state budget commission—were only going to spend the interest. Treat it more like a trust than a spend-down.

Deason didn’t care for that. When he and Metzger had initially strategized on the legislation, the environmental movement was pushing for modesty. Maybe a few hundred thousand dollars. Deason wanted to shoot for $1 billion to spend over three years. Then he wanted to go back and get more.

“That’s absolutely absurd,” Deason said. He stared absently out the windshield as oaks and junipers skipped by. “We should have done $10 billion.”

Metzger let that sit for a beat. “I understand you want to stretch the principal out,” he said, “but if you have a once-in-a-lifetime opportunity, you should be able to dip in—”

“That’s the whole point,” Deason said. “Because these beautiful places are going to get developed.” In an op-ed the two men wrote in 2022 arguing for more parkland, they noted that between 1997 and 2017, rapidly growing Texas lost ten football fields of land per hour to development. More recently, Metzger has cited an updated statistic: a thousand acres per day. “That happened in my hometown,” Deason said. “There was a spring, and now it’s a culvert that runs into a creek. It was a beautiful spring.” He’s a Texan through and through, but Deason is originally from Arkansas, so he speaks less with a twang and more with a lilt, which makes the word BYOO-tih-ful more beautiful. “It had been pooled up for years, so the family that owned it started raising the trout in this deep, cold water. Dallas got a lot of trout from Rogers, Arkansas. But that pool got knocked out, and they built a culvert.”

It was impossible, there on the fishing pier, not to think about whether Texas is changing. Though Texas has always been changing. In the state’s great political novel, Billy Lee Brammer’s The Gay Place, Governor Arthur Fenstemaker—a thinly veiled portrait of Lyndon Baines Johnson—says that “most of us came into town one Saturday a few years ago and stayed. … We’re urban, by God.” That book was published in 1961. Today something like two thirds of the state that prides itself on its open-range ethos lives in the four major metro areas of the Texas Triangle—Dallas–Fort Worth, Houston, San Antonio, and Austin. And now the state might be turning blue—which it once was—at least a little. That too is a tale that’s been told before.

“I was telling Luke yesterday,” I said to Deason, “coming down here from outside, where there’s just constant media reporting about the Senate race, I thought I was going to see yard signs everywhere, and I saw almost nothing.” The day before, I’d driven down to meet Metzger at Post Oak Ridge State Park, outside Austin. I’d covered a few hundred miles’ worth of suburbs and small towns. “When we had lunch yesterday, I had seen one. I saw three more on the way home, and that was it.”

“Well, that’s what Beto did,” he said. The only way Beto got any traction at all is they spent several million dollars on signs, he explained. They had signs everywhere. The Beto folks even asked Deason if he’d put one up. He donates to Democrats occasionally, so he was on their list. Hell, he has a big yard—maybe he could take two, put one at each end, make it look like his neighbors were supporters?

Deason caught sight of Metzger casting, a perfect rhythmic shoot of the line out over the water. He interrupted his own Beto story. “Pretty good there,” he observed.

“You think seeing all the Talarico signs, that’s a strategy too,” I said.

“Yeah,” he said. “But it didn’t work for Beto. The problem is there’s not a lot of enthusiasm for either candidate. Because I mean, I know Talarico, and I know Paxton really well. He was with me last night at the game.” As in, in a box at the Cowboys stadium. In fact, right now two Paxton signs are staked into his yard. Thanks for the idea, Beto. “And nobody knows Talarico, so he’s trying to create this reimagining of himself.” (Deason, by his account, met with Talarico two sessions ago as he was talking to Democrats about school choice. He found Talarico forthright: The state representative told Deason he had long admired his work on criminal-justice reform but also that his parents were teachers and, thank you very much, he was never going to go for school choice.)

“Do you have any predictions for the outcome of the race?” I asked.

“I think Paxton wins by six to eight,” Deason said. “The reinforcements have shown up. We had a big ad buy. About $85 million has rolled in from PACs and big donors. We’ve raised another $5 million over the past twelve or fourteen days.”

All the while I was casting. Pulling in, straightening my arm, casting again. Deason finally had enough, set political strategy aside. “So you want to let it load behind you,” he reminded me.

“Let it come back,” I affirmed. I pulled my arm up. The line was so light I couldn’t possibly feel that it had spanned the space behind me. But I tried to feel it, and convinced myself that I did, and then whipped the line forward. The fly sailed with purpose, then died, dropping into the water a mediocre distance from the pilings holding up the pier.

A few weeks later, I called Deason up and asked if it will change how he operates if Talarico wins. He said it will not—as a senator, Talarico would be dealing more with national issues than Deason’s pet state initiatives, and anyway, he was still certain Paxton is going to win. I asked Metzger the same thing. His answer was different. He said that a Talarico win would put the state’s politicians on notice that Republican victory is not preordained, which would make them more inclined to compromise. Bipartisanship might not win the day, but it could win the long game. After all, Metzger said, that’s what happened after Beto’s near miss.

Post Oak Ridge, about an hour and a half outside Austin, near the small town of Lampasas, wasn’t officially open yet—it would open for day use the week after I visited. Metzger met me there, the day before our fishing trip, and it was the first time he’d been to see the fruits of the Centennial Fund in real life. Former ranchland was becoming a park—the state was running utilities, turning ranch roads into roads suitable for emergency vehicles, breaking trail, adding shade structures and camping areas. Because of its proximity to Austin, it was an especially valuable addition to the park system: Metzger could remember a time, while working on the legislation, when every campsite anywhere near the capital, where he lives, was completely booked.

Superintendent Kacey Sloan hiked with us, taking us to an overlook of the Colorado River, which forms part of the park’s boundary. Metzger, who has been to seventy-eight of Texas’s eighty-nine state parks, debated whether he could count this one.

Metzger took a picture of Post Oak Falls, a Seussian waterfall made of tufa, spongy and bulbous limestone formed as minerals precipitate out of water. At another overlook, we could hear people below us, enjoying the Colorado.

Sloan next offered to take us to a more remote corner of Post Oak Ridge, one that trails might never reach. We parked by Yancey Creek, a broad and shallow water stubbled with brush and riffles and smoothed stone.

“If you can hop from rock to rock, we can get across without getting your feet wet,” Sloan told us. Then, when I was mid-hop, “There could be poisonous snakes. I haven’t seen any poisonous snakes, but there will be snakes. Don’t let them scare you.”

On the other side of the creek, we found ourselves under a small tree with low-hanging branches. There, at its base, was a spring. I’ve been to headwaters before. And I’ve been to hot springs. And springs are not remotely rare, I know. But I couldn’t think of another time I’d seen one like this, water actively bubbling out of a cleft in the rock so you felt like Moses.

There was a ladle hanging from a tree branch. Who knows how long it had been there—it was dirty enough that we didn’t use it—but its invitation was clear. First Metzger, then I, knelt down and scooped up handfuls of water and drank.

Back at the pier, Deason ambled over to Metzger. “So what are you working on?” he asked. “What kind of bill do you want to pass?”

“Oh, a few things,” Metzger said. “Wildlife crossings. Trying to get TxDOT to build some crossings over highways to reduce animal/vehicle collisions. Plastic pollution. Um…”

“That one in California is awesome,” Deason said, referring to the Wallis Annenberg Wildlife Crossing on US 101, just north of Los Angeles, which is opening in December.

“So what highways would you cross?” Deason asked.

Metzger, of course, was ready. “Well, TxDOT actually did a study, or commissioned UT to do a study, back in 2019, and they found there’s one hundred segments in Texas where crossings would more than pay for themselves in terms of avoided property damage and loss of life.”

I was holding Deason’s fly rod, letting it rest on the railing of the pier while watching them talk. They always seemed to know enough about what the other one cared about to keep a conversation live. It was tactical, but it also reminded me of The Living Mountain, a favorite piece of nature writing. “The perfect hill companion is the one whose identity is for the time being merged in that of the mountains, as you feel your own to be,” writes Nan Shepherd. “Then such speech as arises is part of a common life and cannot be alien.”

“So yeah, we would start there,” Metzger said.

“How many people a year die in Texas from collisions?” Deason asked.

“I think it’s around twenty or so, and then hundreds get injured.” Texas is number one for fatalities from animal/vehicle collisions.

Deason was incredulous. And then, almost as if listening in, a spot of color like gunmetal dropped from a perch among the trees beside us, along the shore. “See that kingfisher?!”

Deason took over Metzger’s rod to have a few goes of his own, to demonstrate.

“It’s a simple move,” he said, and in a sudden but controlled motion, he whipped the line up into the air. “A River Runs Through It with Brad Pitt tries to make it look like casting is an art,” Deason said. He brought the rod forward and down and placed the fly near the foot of a spindly tree, just beyond a long, dark shape in the water. “Look at that snake,” he said.

He pulled in some of the slack on the line with his left hand, then, just like that, cast out over the snake again. He pulled in the line. “Oh, you got ’im,” said Graham. No fish, but maybe we could land a serpent. The hook grazed across the snake’s midsection, pulling it briefly into a chevron before slipping free, and the snake sluiced off.

Deason laughed. Maybe it was no coincidence we couldn’t get a bite. Maybe the snake was leveraging our lures. “He’s fishin’,” Deason said.

An hour later, Deason was irritated. He’d wondered aloud a few times whether there was a place in the park to get bait. A ranger came by, and Deason asked. The closest place with bait was a shop in Strawn. We weren’t about to leave and come back. Deason fiddled with his tackle. He put a new fly on the end of the line. “I’d hate for these guys to get skunked,” he said.

He gave me the rod and pointed me to a spot several yards beyond where he’d played with the snake. Right in the pocket under the tree, he said. Fish like that.

His instinct was good, if for no other reason than that the distance from the pier to the target was one even I could manage. I could place my fly pretty much where I wanted to on the calm water. I cast out, then started slowly pulling line in with my left hand, guiding it along the pole with the index and middle fingers of my right, just as I’d seen Deason do.

Metzger was working the other end of the pier. Deason and Graham were still talking to the ranger. Maybe it had broken 80 degrees. I felt like I could be getting a sunburn, a pleasant heat.

I looked down at my fly. A pair of fish had materialized next to it. My breath caught. The fish struck at the lure. I yanked the pole so violently the fly lifted off the water and the nearly weightless line bounced unnaturally, like a half-filled helium balloon that can’t make up its mind whether to float or sink.

I checked to see if anyone had been watching. They hadn’t, so I pulled the line in and cast again, to the same spot.

As soon as the fly was back on the water, the fish returned. I saw one make its move, and this time, with a little more grace—not much, but a little more—I pulled the line.

Instead of using the reel, I pulled the slack in by hand, piling it at my feet on the pier until I could grab the line above the fish and pull it over the railing. “I got one!” I said. It was a bluegill, fantastically small, maybe the size of my palm. I started toward the guys at the other end of the pier, ungainly with the fish on a line in one hand and the fly rod in the other and a pile of slack, which I stumbled on, at my feet.

Metzger and Graham saw me first. “Look at that!” Graham said, coming over. It was a tiny fish, but I had caught it. And now I needed to let it go—catch and release.

“This is the part I’m not so good at yet…” Metzger said.

Graham told me to grab the fish around the body with one hand, then pinch the head of the fly between the thumb and finger of my other hand. “Push down, twist, and pull the hook out,” he said. I tried, but the hook wasn’t coming. I could just see the yellow bead at the top—the head—where the fly connected to the tippet. I kept trying. The fish was uncomfortable, and I could feel Metzger wincing. Graham tried. Nothing. The fish had nearly swallowed the thing whole.

We took it to Deason. His hands failed, so he got a pair of forceps from his tackle. He gripped the head of the fly with the forceps, and then, with great delicacy, pushed the lure deep into the fish’s throat, so that he could turn it and extract it. There was a tenderness in the way he treated the animal. I don’t mean to exaggerate this, but I thought of an uncle of mine, a hunter, and the way he loves the things he kills. He calls himself a conservationist. The lure was free.

Deason tossed the fish unceremoniously off the pier. I saw Metzger wince again.

Metzger caught a fish too, before the heat rose too much. I caught another, a second tiny bluegill. This time, when I held the fish in my hand, I could see the hook protruding from the skin just below its mouth. I grabbed the top, twisted until the point of the hook withdrew, and pulled out the fly. I tossed the fish back to the water. I still couldn’t cast worth a damn, but I felt like I’d learned something.

We stopped in Strawn on the way home, at a famous greasy spoon called Mary’s. Metzger, Graham, and I all ordered the house special, chicken-fried steak. Mary’s offered small, medium, and large. We each chose small, and none of us could finish the meal. Deason unwittingly ordered a two-pound hamburger. He thought he was getting two patties. It was the biggest burger I’d ever seen by a fair margin. He picked at it with a fork and knife while his phone pinged over and over with messages from the Paxton people.

We made the drive back after lunch. It was hardly into the afternoon, but we’d had a full day. We were mostly quiet, except Graham, who was working his phone for a fundraiser the following weekend. “Doug, you’re not going to come on Saturday?” he asked, between tapping out messages.

“No, thank you for the invitation, though,” Deason said, eyes on the road.

“You sure you don’t want to come shooting with Rick Perry?” asked Graham, trying to sweeten the deal.

“Ah,” Deason said, dismissively. “I don’t like shooting clay. I think we should preserve the clay,” he added, for Metzger’s benefit, chuckling at himself.

“You should see what windmills do to clay,” Metzger said, and the chuckles became true, deep belly laughs.

The laughter settled as the truck continued through Fort Worth. Passed a billboard with a massive portrait of the president: TRUMP—“Born in New York, But TEXAN in SPIRIT” A few fishing poles were propped over the center console, and the smallest eyelet on the end of each one tracked imperfections in the road like a phonograph needle. We were all sun-spent. The postmodern skyline of Dallas would soon appear out of the distant haze hanging in heat over the horizon.

“Let me know what you guys are trying to do this session and we’ll find something to work on,” Deason said, quietly. Metzger promised that he would. There was always more to do. Soon after, Deason would start calling legislators about lifting the cap on spending only the Centennial Fund’s interest. Metzger published an op-ed in the Houston Chronicle arguing forcefully against the state’s reported consideration of selling Boca Chica State Park to Elon Musk, whose rocket company launches nearby. Metzger and Deason had worked on it together. “Doug and I were 95 percent agreed on language in the op-ed but couldn’t hammer out some of the final pieces quickly enough” to get Deason’s byline on it, he told me. No word yet on the wildlife overpasses. No word yet on the midterms.

When I got home to New York, I ordered a copy of The Curtis Creek Manifesto. It turned out to be a hand-drawn comic in the irreverent style of comix of the sixties and seventies. (“The Upstream Crawl,” it said, describing a careful tactic for approaching fish, “Originated by the Marquis de Sade in February of 1764…”) The author was a Utahn named Sheridan Andreas Mulholland Anderson, whose author photo shows a longhair with a black cape and mustache, hand on chin in a parody of self-seriousness. I could see immediately why Deason liked it. It was populist, prickly, but also eminently practical.

I read it in one sitting. On the very last page of the book, Anderson offered a plea. “Most of the waters I fished as a kid have been channeled, piped, or dammed,” he wrote, under the heading “Sanity, Anyone?” “Greedy little minds are ever busy converting our landscape into slag-heaps, housing tracts, canals, freeways and shopping malls, a perversion they zealously pursue under the ragged banner of progress.”

As I flipped back to the beginning, I read over the very first page one more time. It explains the basic task of the sport and describes in large type the characteristics of its two principal actors. The fisherman: “deceive — trick — fake — hoodwink — beguile — ensnare — trap — con — bamboozle — inveigle.” The fish: “vigilant — cagey — shy — careful — cautious — alert — wary — guarded — watchful.”

I thought of Deason delicately releasing the hook from the fish’s mouth and Metzger wincing when it hit the water. The key to angling was to recognize that the superior being was the fish. And maybe that’s why Deason and Metzger are successful. I’m not saying they don’t enjoy the organizing, the activism, the political maneuvering. But even more, I think they love the part today’s politics often forgets, which is the part where you and everybody else around you is just trying to live. These guys. They could be screaming at each other on Twitter or in a Buc-ees or I guess even at the statehouse. Instead, they’re a couple of fish, facing upstream, basking in the holding water, seeing when something good comes floating down.

Jared Kushner: Worst American of All Time?

Oh No, Not Pete Hegseth Aiming for the Senate

Don’t Ask Trump How His Revenge Tour Is Going

Tim McGraw Knows How to Make a Marriage Work

#6882. Inside the Lavish Estates Where The Gilded Age Was Filmed

These locations are straight out of 1880s high society.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Why should the Brits have all the fun? The writer of Downton Abbey, Julian Fellowes, went stateside with the HBO series The Gilded Age, which tells the story of New York City high society in the 1880s amid the halcyon days of the industrial boom. The series explores the tension between old-money elites and their escalating feud with the nouveau riche neighbors who threaten the established social order. Meanwhile in real life, powerhouse families like the Vanderbilts and the Carnegies left their opulent footprints on Newport, Rhode Island, in the form of their famously ostentatious and absolutely gargantuan “summer cottages.” Circumstances which lend themselves to set-design magic.

In honor of season four of The Gilded Age, which premiered November 1, we’re revisiting the real-life settings that have helped bring the series to life. The social alliances may shift, but the architecture remains a dependable scene-stealer. Below, tour 21 properties that rank among the East Coast’s architectural crown jewels, from historic house museums to lavish mansions.

Features as: Bertha Russell’s ballroom and George Russell’s bedroom, as well as the billiard room.

These so-called dollar princesses and their families lived in Manhattan and spent their summers in Newport, Rhode Island. Of course, these industry magnates didn’t consider their infrequent visits (some only came a few weeks in a year) as a reason to exercise restraint. The mammoth Breakers mansion is one of four so-called summer “cottages” (read: castles) that were completed just blocks from each other in a building boom between 1892 and 1902. The mansion was designed by Richard Morris Hunt in the Renaissance Revival style, as a less-than-subtle symbol of the Vanderbilt family’s social and financial preeminence in the Gilded Age. It was architectural one-upmanship that is considered the grandest of all the manses, featuring ornate ballrooms, extensive art collections, manicured gardens, and the latest (then) in modern technology, including electric lights. It has 70 rooms, an interior using marble imported from Italy and Africa, and a library mantel purchased from a château in France.

“When you walk into the dining room of The Breakers, with its painting of the goddess Aurora on the ceiling 50 feet above you or see 500,000 cubic feet of marble in a variety of different hues, you can’t help but be awestruck by the incredible wealth of these Gilded Age tycoons,” says Trudy Coxe, the chief executive officer and executive director of The Preservation Society of Newport County, which has under its stewardship six of the grand mansions featured in the series.

The preservation society worked closely with the filming crew to ensure the safety of the objects and spaces. “We had long discussions about the billiard table at The Breakers,” she explains. “It could not be removed, but it was important to the scene being filmed. Rather than moving it, we worked with their props department to put an alternative covering on the table to simulate an actual game of pool.”

In the series, the Breakers’ neo–Italian Renaissance great hall and music room are featured as Bertha Russell’s (played by Carrie Coon) ballroom, which we see in the final ball scene. George Russell (Morgan Spector) also discusses business while playing billiards in the mansion’s massive billiard room.

Features as: Bertha Russell’s bedroom in the Russell residence

Marble House was built from 1888 to 1892 as a summer cottage for Alva and William Kissam Vanderbilt. It was also designed by Richard Morris Hunt, but in the Beaux-Arts style. For an American house, it was unparalleled in opulence, with a temple-front portico resembling that of the White House, which had been completed 92 years earlier in 1800 (lofty, no?).

Mrs. Russell’s bedroom, built on a soundstage in Bethpage, New York, was inspired by real-life socialite Alva Vanderbilt’s bedroom in the Marble House. The French Louis XV bed was painted and reupholstered in salmon-pink velvet, and the custom bedding used 300 yards of fabric that have been draped, cinched, and swagged.

And that’s just the textiles. Most of the room’s furniture is refurbished antique and everything in the room has been painted to match. Sometimes, Shaw notes, it was difficult to paint a lovely wooden antique piece. “The scenic artists would look at us and say, ‘You really want me to paint over the chestnut? Are you sure?’” says Shaw. “Yes.”

Consuelo Vanderbilt’s (Alva and William’s child) monochrome red bedroom in the mansion also serves as George Russell’s bedroom in the show.

Features as: The exterior of Sylvia Chamberlain’s house

Rosecliff was built for Nevada silver heiress Theresa Fair Oelrichs, whose father, James Graham Fair, was one of the four partners in the Comstock Lode. The famed architectural firm McKim, Mead & White was commissioned to design the mansion, which was modeled after the Grand Trianon, the garden retreat of French kings at Versailles. The French Baroque Revival gem was designed for entertaining at a grand scale—and it certainly fulfilled its purpose. Fair Oelrichs would become known as one of the three great hostesses of Newport.

The white front facade of this mansion, which recently got a $7.4 million renovation, is a stand-in for Sylvia Chamberlain’s (played by Jeanne Tripplehorn) house. You’ll see it in Episode 7, when Tom Raikes (Thomas Cocquerel) pays a visit.

Features as: The servant kitchen and the real-life Sarah Berwind’s bedroom as parts of the Russell residence

The Elms is a Classical Revival dwelling that was completed in 1901. Architect Horace Trumbauer designed it for the coal baron Edward Julius Berwind, taking inspiration from the 18th-century d’Asnières French château. It also houses an impressive collection of paintings and tapestries, as well as the latest technology of its age.

In The Gilded Age Seasons 1 and 2, The Elms stands in as part of the Russells’ lavish residence, including scenes in the kitchen and Bertha Russell’s bedroom. But it truly steps into the spotlight in Season 3, when it hosts a dazzling ball. And as a long-awaited confession of love looms (we won’t spoil from whom), the opulent Newport mansion proves to be the perfect setting for high drama and high society.

Each guest is granted a cinematic entrance, illuminated by a golden glow that bathes the grand façade—a new addition this season, as electricity is now becoming available. “The house’s interiors would not have been electrified yet, but people were beginning to use it for parties,” Shaw says. “That would mean they would have to have their own generator installed somewhere on the property to light up those lights.” To accomplish this, the crew went to specialty shops that make reproduction bulbs. “The original bulbs were much bigger than what we have in a household today,” Shaw adds. “Some of the lightbulbs we used were 10 inches in diameter, which is how big they were then.”

Features as: The exterior is used for Caroline Astor’s Beechwood house; the boardinghouse is used for a scene with Oscar van Rhijn; and another room serves as Agnes van Rhijn’s bedroom

Completed in 1852, Château-sur-Mer is one of the first grand Bellevue Avenue mansions of the Gilded Age in Newport, built by architect Seth C. Bradford for William Shepard Wetmore. Its interiors were designed by Ogden Codman Jr., who co-authored Edith Wharton’s Decoration of Houses. The mansion was host to many major parties, including the Fête Champêtre, an elaborate country picnic for more than 2,000 guests in 1857, as well as a debutante ball for Edith Wetmore in 1889. Wetmore’s son, George Peabody Wetmore, went on to have a distinguished political career as the governor of Rhode Island and, later, as a U.S. senator.

In The Gilded Age, the home’s exterior was used for Caroline Astor’s (Donna Murphy) Beechwood House. The hallway, dining room, and ballroom were used during Mamie Fish’s (Ashlie Atkinson) doll tea party. Several bedrooms serve as Agnes van Rhijn’s (Christine Baranski) bedroom, Oscar van Rhijn’s (Blake Ritson) bedroom, Anne Morris’s (Katie Finneran) bedroom, and Patrick Morris’s (Michel Gill) office. There’s also a scene filmed at the boardinghouse with Oscar van Rhijn, according to Patch.

Features as: The office of Brook family lawyer Tom Raikes

Hunter House was constructed in 1748 at the harborfront near the north end of the Newport Historic District at a time when the city was a bustling seaport in the British empire. The house was owned by wealthy merchants and overlooked the wharves that serviced their trading ships. It’s one of the finest examples of Colonial architecture in Newport, according to the Preservation Society. Inside, its resident families furnished the house with decorative arts produced by expert craftsmen, much of which has been preserved today. Such a luxurious lifestyle, however, depended upon the labor of enslaved people of African descent; recent research by the Preservation Society has identified at least 13 enslaved individuals who lived and worked at Hunter House. Today’s Hunter House honors their perseverance and incalculable contributions to Newport’s past.

One of the sitting rooms in Hunter House functions as the office of Brook family lawyer Tom Raikes, and is set fictitiously in Doylestown, Pennsylvania.

Features as: The Astor family summer home

Belcourt was completed in 1894 for Oliver Hazard Perry Belmont, a prominent American socialite and coachman who served in the U.S. House of Representatives. Like many of the Newport mansions, the Château-esque structure was completed by architect Richard Morris Hunt; it was designed to mimic the hunting lodge of Versailles, built for Louis XIII of France, but with a special ask to accommodate Oliver’s collection of horses and automobiles. In 1896, Oliver gave the house to his second wife, the former Alva Vanderbilt, who designed the neighboring Newport mansion Marble House. Alva used Belcourt as a gathering place for the elite of society, hosting lavish parties and balls and attracting notable figures in politics, business, and the arts.

In The Gilded Age Season 1, Belcourt shows up in Episode 8 as Mrs. Astor’s lavish new Newport home, which Mrs. Russell sneaks inside and is forced to flee to avoid a less-than-pleasant meetup.

Features as: The social gathering scene where Caroline Astor and George Russell meet

Built in 1867 by prolific Boston tea and china merchant Robert M. Cushing, the Ledges was originally the summer home of the Cushing family. This majestic oceanfront pile is perched on one of Newport’s most desirable peninsulas, overlooking Bailey’s Beach. It was built by architect John H. Sturgis, who designed a large, Stick-style cottage that was equal parts comfort and elegance. Today it features original furnishings, papered walls, and unique artwork, including a studio that was added to the side of the house for Robert’s artistic children.

Today, the estate remains in the hands of the Cushing descendants, who occasionally open the house up to film crews. It was featured a few years ago in the film Evening, starring Glenn Close and Meryl Streep. “Working at the Ledges was pretty extraordinary,” Gilded Age location manager Lauri Pitkus told The Providence Journal, “because I don't think I’ve ever been in a home with spectacular views out of every window.”

This historic house was built in 1864 for John Noble Alsop Griswold, an Old China Trade merchant and diplomat. The house is the work of Richard Morris Hunt; it was one of the earliest buildings in the American Stick style of architecture. The house has a complex roofline with a main mansarded section that is pierced by numerous gable and dormer sections. It boasts a number of balconies sheltered by deep eaves, with gable ends decorated with woodwork. Today, the house is a gallery location for the Newport Art Museum. In the series, the house features in Season 1 Episode 1, and again in Episode 5.

Features as: Mingling with the other elite scene

The International Tennis Hall of Fame and its perfectly preserved grass courts are in the Newport Casino, a National Historic Landmark property. The historic seven-acre estate was commissioned by New York Herald publisher James Gordon Bennett as a social club for the elite summer residents of Newport. The Newport News boasted three days after it opened in July 1880: “It is doubtful if a livelier place can be found.” Today, the space is recognized as the official hall of fame for the sport of tennis globally. Since 1955, 262 distinguished inductees representing 27 countries have been enshrined in Newport.

In the eighth episode of The Gilded Age Season 1, the characters are seen promenading around the piazza that overlooks what is known today as Horseshoe Court. A few characters are even playing tennis. “It was fitting to have that history depicted here, given The Gilded Age’s look into that time period,” the hall of fame’s Megan Erbes tells ELLE DECOR. “Several of our staff members got to work closely with the production team and actors, teaching everything from the history of our property to how to hit a proper forehand.” RI Monthly reported that crews returned to the courts last summer to film Season 2 of the show, so it’s possible that Newport Casino will make another appearance.

Features as: The parlor, library, entryway, and staircase of the Morris house, located on Fifth Avenue in the show; Mr. and Mrs. Morris’s bedrooms were filmed in the Newport mansions

William Smith Slater hired General William Walker to build a home as a wedding gift for his daughter. The result was this historic home, which was completed in 1877. The Victorian house is filled with treasures—the fireplace surrounds, for example, were carved by Charles Dowler, who came to the United States to produce arms for the Civil War. Today, the house is open to visitors and is known as the Clouds Hill Museum.

The Clouds Hill Museum is the only Rhode Island site outside of Newport that was used in the series. The crew scheduled filming for Season 1 in late February of 2021, when a few hundred people were on site during two days of filming, museum director Wayne Cabral told Warwick Online, noting that the taping finished around midnight on the second day of filming (after five days of work to prep the set). Almost all the parlor’s furniture was removed, and the head set designer brought in items sourced from local antiques stores in East Greenwich and North Kingstown, museum president Anne Holst added. The producers were adamant about removing the existing curtains, which were disintegrating, Holst added, so she negotiated to leave the replacements (which cost $10,000) with the museum after filming.

Features as: Visit to the home of Aurora Fane scene

The Gothic Revival–style Lyndhurst Mansion was designed in 1838 by Alexander Jackson Davis. Lyndhurst’s third owner, Jay Gould, is the real-life inspiration for the “new money” character George Russell. “He was fantastically ruthless in business, to an extent that Mrs. Astor would never let him in, he was never allowed into society, and she didn’t let his children in until he was dead,” creator, writer, and producer Julian Fellowes said in a podcast. “But he was an incredibly affectionate father and a very caring and faithful husband…and his children all had these nostalgic memories of Lyndhurst, their house on the Hudson.”

In Season 1 of The Gilded Age, Lyndhurst’s interiors are the home of characters Charles and Aurora Fane (Ward Horton and Kelli O’Hara), who are related to the van Rhijns and part of the “old money” side of society. The Lyndhurst lawns and grounds also doubled as Central Park and the Sheep’s Meadow in the opening sequence. The home boasts a bowling alley that dates back to 1894, which was re-created to be the ferry terminal that Marian Brook (Louisa Jacobson) and Peggy Scott (Denée Benton) take to Manhattan for the first time in Episode 1. And finally, the house’s carriage house was transformed into The New York Globe newspaper offices.

The Belvedere Estate, located between Lyndhurst Mansion and writer Washington Irving’s Sunnyside home, is a century-old mansion on 25 acres in historic Tarrytown. The estate was originally part of Philipsburg Manor, a 90,000-acre property built for Florence and Casper Whitney. “While the mansion dates from the 1920s, it has a much older European feel that could represent something from decades earlier,” says Belvedere Estate sales director Linda Fiorentino, who recalls the massive legwork required to film at the mansion.

“I will never forget the background actors descending from a yellow school bus, fully dressed in period costumes and felt hats,” she says. The Season 1 scene was filmed at the pond at the back of Belvedere Estate, acting as a stand-in for Central Park in the scene when Marian meets Mr. Raikes at Bethesda Fountain in Episode 1. The pond is a man-made feature, and the water level sometimes sinks to a mere foot or two, Fiorentino explains. “Before filming, they filled the pond with roughly 600,000 gallons of water to fill to capacity, giving the illusion of a much larger body of water and to allow them to have pleasure boats filled with passengers on the water,” she says. They also re-created Central Park’s Shakespeare Bridge from rough-hewn logs and brought in trees, shrubs, and foliage to cover the existing link fence because it was too modern.

Features as: The drawing room of wealthy widow Sylvia Chamberlain

Toward the end of the 19th century, Colonel Elliott Fitch Shepard, a lawyer who founded the New York State Bar Association in 1876, purchased more than 500 acres on a hill high above the Hudson River north of Tarrytown. He engaged the renowned architecture firm McKim, Mead & White to design his family’s manor house here. The result was a 75-room Victorian structure that mimicked 18th-century Italian villa architecture. Think: hand-carved mahogany paneling, a grand stairway near the front entrance, high patterned ceilings, and elaborate cornice work. Unfortunately, Shepard died before it was completed in 1893, and it passed into the hands of William Rockefeller and Frank Vanderlip. These men were part of a group (including John Jacob Astor, who died on the Titanic in 1912) who went on to convert the building to the Sleepy Hollow Country Club, which remains a private members-only club today.

Scenes in the drawing room of wealthy widow Sylvia Chamberlain were shot on location here. “The entire room was redressed by our team in the Rococo style,” set decorator Regina Graves told 1st Dibs.

Features as: Part of Mrs. Astor’s residence

Glenview was built in 1876 by John Bond Trevor, a highly successful stockbroker, who engaged New York City architect Charles W. Clinton to build his estate. The mansion was a paragon of luxury and modern comfort, featuring indoor plumbing, gas illumination, and a coal-burning furnace to circulate heat throughout its 37 rooms. The exterior had elements of Victorian Gothic and French Second Empire design, complete with a mansard roof, a gray stone facade constructed of blocks hewn from a Hastings-on-Hudson quarry, and decorative sandstone elements brought from Indiana.

The mansion makes an appearance as Caroline Astor’s residence in Season 1. “They were all so complimentary about Glenview, and of course we did a lot of polishing and waxing,” Laura Vookles, chair of the museum’s curatorial department, told The Rivertown Enterprise. “They focused on our exquisite interiors and filmed in six rooms, including the dining, library, and sitting rooms, and used all but one painting that is normally on view.”

The John Paine Mansion, also known as the Castle, was built in 1896 and was deemed the grandest house in Troy, New York. It has since served as a private residence, a college administration building, a movie set (see: Martin Scorsese’s 1993 drama The Age of Innocence), and, currently, a fraternity house. In the show, one of the bedrooms in the Castle features as Tom Raikes’s office.

This residence, the home of Troy locals Bill and Sue Comiskey, stands in for the Brooklyn residence of the well-off Scott family. At one point early in the series, Arthur Scott (John Douglas Thompson) and his daughter, Peggy (Denée Benton), sit on carved-oak chairs around a damask-covered dining table. The home’s elaborate details really shine in this historic home, which dates back to the turn of the 19th century. Stained-glass windows and an elegant china cabinet are memorable architectural features that are original to the location and transport the viewers back in time.

While filming what would be the Scott family residence, Bill, the homeowner, told News10 that a few prized family possessions actually made the cut. “My desk was in it, front and center…you [also] saw the whole front and back parlor into the dining room where they were having a meal,” he explained. “[There were] great shots of this room catching the stained glass, the fireplace, [and] this bookcase my grandfather made.”

Features as: The interior is used as George Russell’s workspace

Hempstead House was built for Howard Gould by architects Hunt & Hunt in 1912 on the north shore of Long Island. The mansion was eventually sold to art mogul Daniel Guggenheim (yes, that Guggenheim). Hempstead House is one of four mansions on the grounds of the Guggenheim Estate—which includes Castle Gould, Falaise, and Mille Fleur. The Tudor-style castle was the embodiment of Gold Coast opulence, a place where the esteemed family hosted grand parties, performances, and exhibitions by gatherings of the elite of the time. To say it is massive would be an understatement: 50,000 square feet, with 40 rooms and a 60-foot-tall entry foyer. In The Gilded Age, the mansion’s interior was used as George Russell’s workspace.

Features as: The late General Henry Brook’s house

Rock Hall Museum was initially an 18th-century plantation. The two-and-a-half-story Georgian-style home was built in approximately 1767 and later restored to its original appearance in the 1950s. In Season 1 Episode 1, the house’s white facade is featured within the first 15 minutes as a backdrop for the late General Henry Brook’s house. Marian, his daughter, is seen walking out of the home on her way to the train station.

The Hart Cluett Museum is a work of Federal architecture designed by Martin E. Thompson in 1827. He was hired by William Howard. Thompson brought the design of the Hart Cluett House back to New York City, where many examples of the 1827 Federal-style townhouse were copied throughout Manhattan. This house, a museum open to the public today, was featured in The Gilded Age in Episode 2 as Anne Morris’s home.

Rachel Silva is a freelance writer and editor covering design, architecture, and culture. She previously served as an editor at ELLE Decor, and her work has appeared in Harper’s Bazaar, TIME, and Veranda, among others. A graduate of Columbia Journalism School, she is based in New York City.

#6881. Why Is Everything Suddenly Coming Up Christopher Abbott?

#6880. 15 Paint Colors HGTV Stars Swear By for a Home That Looks Expensive

From sea blue to crisp white, these designer favorites are timeless.

Everyone knows that a quick coat of paint is the fastest way to transform a room. It’s a favorite trick among professional designers and DIYers alike, but choosing the perfect shade isn't always easy. With hundreds of colors to choose from, finding “the one” can quickly become overwhelming. If you’re feeling a little stuck, don’t fret: HGTV’s biggest stars make these tough decisions every day.

Whether they’re renovating an outdated home or putting the finishing touches on a dream build, HGTV personalities are constantly selecting paint colors that make a space feel fresh, spacious, inviting, and beautifully designed. Over the years, they’ve crowned a select few as go-to favorites that are foolproof and versatile enough to work in almost any room. When every renovation comes with deadlines and budgets, dependable paint colors become invaluable.

To help narrow your search, we asked some of our favorite HGTV stars to reveal the paint colors they reach for most often. Their picks include everything from versatile neutrals to powdery pastels that work across a variety of homes and aesthetics. Additional copy by Alyssa Khan.

“We love muted colors because they’re playful, but not over-the-top. Behr Mocha Ice is a perfect example of that balance—it brings the personality you need to take a space from basic and bland to warm and interesting. Try it in a bedroom, dining room, or anywhere that’s missing that ‘wow’ factor.”—Drew Scott, Property Brothers

“This beautiful blue is timeless and versatile. It’s calming, but not boring. Even though Behr's Dragonfly makes a statement, it’s still neutral enough for many spaces, whether it’s on every wall in a bedroom or used on a specific feature like kitchen cabinets.”—Jonathan Scott, Property Brothers

“This never-boring black paint color is dramatic and impactful yet very versatile. Pair with white for a timeless aesthetic. Use it to create a dramatic accent wall or outside for your exterior (like we did) or trim for maximum curb appeal.”—Tarek El Moussa, The Flip Off

“My favorite paint color is Tricorn Black by Sherwin-Williams. It’s bold, clean, and makes every detail pop. Whether it’s a door, a wall, or literally anything else, this black brings that high-end look every single time. Never fails.”—Cole DeBoer, Down Home Fab

“I am currently loving greige, the perfect blend of gray and beige. It’s warm, soft, and timeless—a great option for homeowners who want both cozy and modern. Benjamin Moore's Revere Pewter is a classic and rich greige with warmth. Great for open-concept homes.”—Heather Rae El Moussa, The Flip Off

“What I love most about it is its confidence. Studio Green by Farrow & Ball doesn’t whisper: It commands. It’s deep, moody, and unapologetically rich, the kind of color that instantly elevates a space and gives it a point of view. In daylight, it reads as a classic, almost architectural green, grounded and refined. But at night? It transforms into something velvety and dramatic, wrapping the room in a quiet luxury that feels both intimate and powerful.”—Egypt Sherrod, Married to Real Estate andProperty Virgins

“Benjamin Moore Misty Air OC-44 is my constant when I need a bright neutral. It is a broken-in cream that’s the perfect backdrop for colorful art and upholstery.”—Erin Napier, Home Town

““My current favorite paint color is Black Fox by Sherwin-Williams. It’s the perfect mix of sophisticated and moody, with just enough warmth to make any space feel grounded and timeless. It pairs beautifully with so many other tones and textures. I love it in an office!”—Chelsea DeBoer, Down Home Fab

Magnolia Home by Joanna Gaines Juniper Tree Crafted by KILZ

“Seed & Supply was one of the first additions we made to the Silos back in 2015. Before it served as our garden shop on the grounds, it was used as an additional retail space at the original Magnolia shop, which we lovingly refer to as The Little Shop on Bosque. This year, the team had the idea to evolve the building yet again, but this time to serve as a creative workshop space for our guests. I immediately loved the idea. It has always been my hope that our guests’ experience at the Silos would inspire creativity and connection.

The whole interior of the workshop is refreshed with one color, Juniper Tree Trim, Door, and Cabinetry Paint in satin finish. I love how these nature-forward hues turned out—they feel fresh yet familiar, bold yet comforting.”—Joanna Gaines, Fixer Upper

““White Flour is the perfect soft white backdrop for every style. It accentuates simplicity or maximalist energy. The warm hue is anything but basic.”—Taniya Nayak, Battle on the Beach

“I love Farrow & Ball's Drop Cloth for walls, or for trim and cabinetry with a more true white on the wall. It is a timeless neutral that looks beautiful, particularly in historic homes. This warm neutral evokes calm tranquility without being sterile. It is the perfect first layer for any space: It sets a foundation of simplicity that can easily be layered upon with color on cabinetry, trim detail, furniture, art, and décor.”—Jenny Marrs, Fixer to Fabulous

“I keep going back to Behr White. It’s porcelain-like and a classic white without being too stark or jarring. It also has a very slight undertone of blue, which I prefer over yellow or pink undertones, as a slight hint of blue preserves more of a pure white tone.”—Galey Alix, Home in a Heartbeat With Galey Alix

“My current favorite color is Benjamin Moore Windsor Green. I just painted my bedroom this color! The walls, the doors, the windows, and the trim. It's just so deep and lovely. To me, a deep mossy green is cozy and sophisticated at the same time. It steals the show every time, which is why I went for it in my own bedroom!”—Kristina Crestin, Farmhouse Fixer

In her blog for the Laurel Mercantile, the Home Town Takeover co-host crowned Dover White her all-time favorite hue.

“My all-time favorite white, forever and always, to the point my design team just defaults to it when they aren't able to get a quick decision from me.”— Erin Napier, Home Town Takeover

Fix My Flip star, Page Turner, shared her favorite shade of white with HGTV fans. Boasting warm cream and soft beige undertones, Whisper White is the perfect airy neutral.

“I like how this shade isn’t as stark as other white paints can be.” — Page Turner, Fix My Flip

The Property Brothers named Grenada Villa as one of their go-to shades for their site, drew & jonathan, but fans of Brother vs. Brother will recall the shade being name-dropped much earlier. Drew used it to coat the guest bedroom ceiling in the Season 6 Canyon House in 2018. The serene shade has remained in their palette ever since.

#6879. Meg Ryan’s Former Montecito Estate Sells for $18 Million

The actress oversaw a major renovation and expansion to the house.

#6875. Your Next Signature Scent Should Be Coffee-Inspired

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

These 11 colognes will inspire you to switch up your rotation.

I am not a coffee drinker. Never have been. The taste is too bitter for my liking, and that much caffeine will just keep me up all night, no matter what time of day I drink it. And coffee breath . . . But the smell of an actual cup of coffee? It’s divine. Rich. Sophisticated. And perfect for personal fragrance.

Perfumers include coffee notes in fragrance by using gourmand accords made from roasted coffee beans, espresso extracts, or aromatic molecules that mimic those smells. If you read through the scent notes on your favorite coffee cologne, you might notice something called “Coffee CO2 extract”—that’s a concentrated aromatic and bioactive oil created by passing supercritical carbon dioxide through roasted coffee beans. Pretty cool, right?

But coffee notes don’t always smell the same. Roasted coffee beans or espresso can smell dark and dry with a slightly bitter and earthy quality, whereas sweeter coffee notes like mocha and latte scents can be warm, milky, and rich. It also, of course, depends on what other notes are mixed in, from woody and earthy scents to sweet, gourmand ones. To give you an idea of the range, we rounded up the best coffee-scented colognes out there.

You’ll see plenty of pricey bottles on this list. We will get cerebral about perfume—men’s and women’s is a dumb distinction for scent. We will try to push you toward something interesting. But before all that, we have to pay respects. You might call Polo Red, first released in 2013, a garden-variety mall fragrance, something catering to the everyman. That is not our opinion. For us, considering the price point, it’s a modern classic.

Polo Red is a bit more interesting than your typical guy fragrance. It blends fresh, fruity notes of grapefruit, lemon, and cranberry with warm, rich notes of redwood, amber, and coffee. Add in earthy sage and woods and you get a really interesting combination that goes from bright and airy to deep and sensual thanks to the base notes of coffee, which blend beautifully with the warm woods and amber.

As always, Loewe delivers on its luxury fragrance—even the bottle looks like something you’d find in a museum. Like Polo Red, Loewe’s Wild Coffee brings together fruity notes and coffee, but instead of relying on light citrus fruits, it has notes of ripe mango, bitter orange, guava, and passion fruit, which provide a juicy, tropical backdrop for the rich coffee, amber, and tonka bean to come through.

It’s ridiculous how good Bois Corsé is. It’s a simple fragrance that captures “the textured outer layer of a tree.” Meaning, bark.

The notes are coffee, sandalwood, and tonka bean. The coffee is deep and chocolatey. The sandalwood gives it an earthiness, a bit of air and smoke. The tonka bean grounds it all in a warm, user-friendly base. It’s a masterclass, really. Straight down the middle and sure to get compliments

The coffee note is not as pronounced here, as this is a busy fragrance. You get Davana on top—an herb from the southern portion of the Indian subcontinent, which in perfume form presents green and herby but also a bit boozy, like a nice cordial. Like in most of these other scents, there’s a warm tonka note that pairs with the coffee. The oud is not a boring smoky presentation, but a pretty earthy one.

The overall experience is, as the name might suggest, a busy street corner in a tropical climate. There’s a lot going on, but it’s all working well together—a really captivating scent.

In September, DedCool launched its new viral fragrance, Mocha Magic. It’s a gourmand through and through, but it creates some intrigue by contrasting bittersweet notes of espresso, mocha, and bourbon with more classically sweet notes of cream, biscotti, toffee, and vanilla. In a word: Mmmm.

Speaking of gourmands, Maison Margiela’s Replica collection includes the perfect sweet and spicy take on coffee. It emphasizes the scents of warm coffee, milk mousse, and sweet vanilla at its base, but before we get there, it’s actually quite fresh and uplifting thanks to lemon, lavender, and spearmint. So you get a bit of citrus and herbal freshness before leaning into the rich gourmand of it all.

Dolce & Gabbana’s Devotion for Men will surely turn you on to coffee scents. Warm, sensual, and woody, it highlights herbal lavender, dark coffee, and smooth woods, with an earthy backdrop of oakmoss. It’s a solid, elegant pick for a nice dinner or an evening out.

Now here’s a combination you probably weren’t expecting: refined rose and spicy coffee. As it turns out, light florals go really nicely with black coffee, and it’s a cool contrast you really don’t find anywhere else. And, in classic Tom Ford fashion, there’s a bit of smoky frankincense at the base that gives the fragrance a dark and sexy twist.

Coffee and woods make sense together, and when notes of roasted coffee beans are combined with a variety of different woods, it makes for something smooth and sophisticated. That’s what Roja does here. The nutty side of coffee is brought out by notes of cinnamon, pistachio, and caramelized nuts, while cedarwood, sandalwood, oud, and dry woods emphasize its deeper, drier side.

Inspired by the Italian dessert affogato—hot espresso poured over vanilla ice cream—Mancera’s Amore Caffe blends delicious gourmand notes of amaretto liqueur, vanilla ice cream, speculoos, and brown sugar with black coffee to give it that addictive bittersweet quality.

Sweet, spicy, and woody, Kilian’s Intoxicated is another beloved coffee scent, though it’s definitely a strong one. So if you want something bold, this is a good pick. The unique mix of dark mocha coffee with green cardamom, cinnamon, nutmeg, and vetiver gives it a rich, sexy, and spicy intrigue.

#6874. Experts Say This Is the Best Way to Get Rid of Fruit Flies—and You Probably Have It in Your Pantry

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Few things are less pleasant than finding insects taking over your kitchen. Fruit flies may not be as alarming as cockroaches or stink bugs, but they’re still a nuisance—and they’re a signal that you’re not cleaning as thoroughly as you should be.

Fruit flies often congregate in sticky, sweet, moist spots: around fresh produce, by the sink, or near the trash. With their persistent buzzing and quick breeding cycle, a few occasional flies can become a much larger problem if you don’t deal with them quickly.

Fortunately, fruit flies are pretty manageable. The first step is to find what they’re drawn to, whether it’s overripe fruit, food spills, or a trash can or compost bin that isn’t sealed properly. Once you’ve found the source, a few straightforward steps can help clear them out and keep the problem from spreading. Homemade traps and smarter food storage are among the practical ways to take back your kitchen.

For expert advice on getting rid of fruit flies, we spoke with Diana Ludwiczak, a certified home pest inspector in New York City. She explains how to deal with an infestation effectively and keep the flies from returning.

“Fruit flies are attracted to ripe and rotting fruits and vegetables, so it’s important to keep your kitchen clean and free of any food debris,” says Ludwiczak. “Wipe down counters, sweep or vacuum the floors regularly, and store fruits and vegetables in the refrigerator to reduce their exposure to the air.”

Fruit flies also like fermented items, including beer, liquor, and wine. They breed in dark, damp areas like sink drains, garbage disposals, trash cans, and mop buckets.

“Once they begin reproducing indoors, females are able to lay about 500 eggs, and the eggs will hatch in as little as 24–30 hours after being deposited by the female,” according to Orkin.

To get rid of fruit flies in your home, you can try this expert-recommended method.

“Fill a small jar or cup with apple cider vinegar and a few drops of dish soap, and then cover the container with plastic wrap tightly,” Ludwiczak suggests. Poke a few holes in the wrap and place the jar near the fruit fly infestation. “The fruit flies will be drawn to the smell and will become trapped in the jar. This is my number one tip, that I personally use every summer!” she adds.

Within a day, you should see many fruit fly carcasses floating in the liquid. You'll want to keep changing out the liquid each day until all the flies have been caught.

Getting rid of fruit flies is just the first step. Here's what Ludwiczak wants you to do to prevent them from coming back.

When you bring home fresh produce from the grocery store, wash it off before storing or displaying it.

“Fruit flies are also attracted to standing water, so it’s important to dry out sinks, showers, and other areas where water can accumulate. Make sure to fix any leaky pipes or areas of your home where water tends to accumulate,” says Ludwiczak.

According to Ludwiczak, rinsing produce with a vinegar solution may keep your produce fresher for longer—and this helps stave off a fruit fly infestation. To make it, Ludwiczak mixes around two parts water and one part vinegar and sprays it onto the produce. Make sure to give your fruits and veggies one more final rinse in water before you’re done, though, so the vinegar flavor and scent don’t stick around.

Screening in your windows is a good idea for preventing all kinds of critters from getting in, but they're also effective against fruit flies.

“Make sure to use screens on windows and doors to prevent fruit flies from entering your home,” Ludwiczak says. “This is especially important during the warmer months when windows and doors are more likely to be open.”

Basic housekeeping is enough to keep fruit flies in check. Just make sure to keep your countertops clean, move overripe fruit to the fridge, take out your garbage regularly, and clean out your sink to prevent it from becoming a breeding ground for bugs.

#6873. Van Cleef & Arpels Announces Support for Montecito’s Lotusland

The jewelry house heads to Santa Barbara with a fitting gift from a brand captivated by botanic motifs.

Above: The Rose Garden Terrace at Lotusland in Montecito, CA.

Ganna Walska was a lifelong collector. First, there were the husbands: six of them, from a Russian baron to a yoga instructor. Then came the jewelry: hundreds of carats of emeralds, diamonds, and sapphires. Finally, there were plants, her love of which ultimately led her to create a 37-acre botanical garden in Montecito, California.

Walska, an opera singer born Hanna Puacz in Poland in 1887, purchased the Cuesta Linda estate in 1941. She intended to both live there and house Tibetan monks, but after her final divorce (and after the monks failed to show), Walska devoted herself to collecting rare plants. She renamed the property Lotusland, for the Indian lotus in one of the ponds, and poured her fortune into the project. In 1971 she auctioned off many of her jewels to fund her gardens. One piece was the Oiseau clip from Van Cleef & Arpels, a special order depicting a bird in flight carrying a 96.62-carat yellow diamond known as the “Walska Briolette.”

Following her death, the property was opened to the public, and now Van Cleef is making a return. The maison has announced its support of Lotusland, focusing in particular on the Rose Garden Terrace. It’s a gift that honors both Walska’s love of the brand and the inspiration that Van Cleef has pulled from nature for a century, from countless floral variations to the butterfly motif. Jewels will once again endow Lotusland—just as Ganna would have wanted.

This story appears in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.
