#6872. Get Ready for the Holiday Season With These Timeless, Customizable Cards

Minted makes it easy to send personalized cheer to everyone you love.

Last year I spent a long weekend with a friend I hadn’t seen in a decade. When I got home, I sent her the usual ebullient text about how wonderful it was to see her again at last and how much the time meant to me. Oddly, she didn’t reply; I thought she’d ghosted me.

About two weeks later, I dragged myself begrudgingly to my mailbox to collect the usual bunch of mailers, bills, credit card offers, and insurance solicitations. I’d planned to stop by the recycle bucket and toss the whole stack. Then I saw something amid the circulars: a thick, silvery envelope, addressed with lavishly scrawled panache, stamped with sealing wax and postmarked with a limited edition portrait postage stamp featuring a favorite poet. The return address showed it came from my friend.

I was over the moon. Amid all the usual generic garbage, here was a gem—something written just for me. I rushed into the hall, found an actual letter opener in a drawer, and mindfully—lovingly—slit the envelope open to find an artist-designed card by Minted. Inside was a loving note from my friend, far more profound and personal than my erasable text to her. This card was something almost sacred that I could touch, feel, save, and read over and over again.

In a digital age of AI generated missives, disappearing chats, and emails nobody takes the time to read more than the first two sentences of, a letter or note in the form of a greeting card is an anomaly. Even holiday cards have, in many cases, gone digital, crammed between promotional emails and work obligations in our inboxes, blasé versions of their former snazzy, personalized selves. But aren’t we all craving more? Something we can treasure? Don’t we hanker to send something to friends and loved ones as special as the festive season itself?

Enter Minted, a clever stationery and design brand with a mission to use the designs of independent artists and elevated printing techniques—such as letterpress and foil—to bring back the art of card sending and letter writing. Their high end products provide first class alternatives that infuse any dispatch with gravitas, making communicating a mindful act. Bring on the sealing wax and calligraphy pens!

These stunning cards make a statement anytime of the year—but especially over the holidays. Their soul-felt artistry embodies the Japanese concept of geidō: the way of the arts, where every action is meant to be a ceremonial—artistic—celebration of life. Minted’s 2026 array of holiday options embrace heritage motifs, vintage details, vibrant colours, and candid photography. Minted Concierge offers complimentary one-on-one guidance to help you find holiday cards that fit your style and message, then personalize every detail—from the layout and photo to envelopes, liners, and stamps.

For a more keepsake-worthy finish, Photo Mount™ pairs a semi-gloss photo print, mounted by hand on thick premium cardstock, with a choice of foil, letterpress, or standard printing; Minted’s letterpress option gives soft cotton paper a deep, tactile impression, with ink hand-mixed in small batches and each piece reviewed for color and texture. New this year, both Peel & Seal and Mail It For Me make sending holiday cards easier. For larger mailings, Peel & Seal envelopes make sending simpler: a concealed adhesive strip seals with a press, no moisture needed, and select envelope colors are available. On select eligible card formats, Mail It For Me can handle printing, addressing, assembly, postage, and mailing through USPS for an additional fee. Minted confirms when the cards are handed off and will ship you any extra unaddressed cards and envelopes for last-minute list adds.

Greeting cards weren’t always computer generated, overly edited, and filtered to death; they’ve evolved over two centuries. While the first holiday greeting card was introduced in the UK in 1843 as a festive, shorthand option for Christmas letter writers who lacked the time to pen several missives at once, they were popularized during the Victorian era. For decades afterwards, decorative cards trended, each an elaborate work of art. As printing and personal photography became more sophisticated and widespread, so did the cards. Sending and receiving holiday cards was a de rigueur tradition, their appearance evoking the styles of each decade. But in digital times, greeting cards began to lose favor, often replaced (poorly!) by a quick email or text. The pandemic brought a resurgence—an indication that people sought to connect in old school ways—and new generations have taken a renewed interest in sending unique holiday cards.

Designed by independent artists, more relevant than ever, Minted’s custom cards bring the age-old tradition into the future. Combining modern tools and the human touch, Minted ensures that sending holiday cards personalizes the moment, with artistry and emotion that make opening every card feel like unwrapping a meaningful gift. Offering cards with contemporary flourishes as well as those that are imbued with vintage vibes, Minted’s options allow the senders to express themselves and set a mood.

Consider Starry Wonder by Alston Foster, one of 2026’s most celestial designs. It boasts a retro-inspired border made of glittery stars, designed to frame a favorite photo.

Whimsical Cut Paper Party by Inkblot Design pairs fluid, colorful shapes with a look that feels both sophisticated and playful. Fully customizable, the artist-designed card is available in myriad shapes and formats.

Photomatic by Ani Somi delves into the photo booth realm, presenting a perfect place to display your silliness and party spirit. The foil-pressed accents gleam, catching the light like tinsel on a Christmas tree.

Choose Modjeska by chocomocacino to connect with the past, perhaps your own childhood memories of the season. From the atelier of an independent artist, this design comes in a range of shapes and formats, each crafted to help tell your story.

Customize your cards with distinctive fonts and greetings, plus colorful backers or envelope liners, to make them uniquely yours. With dots that call to mind holiday lights aglow, Delicate Dots by Ekko Studio pairs shimmering colors with foil-pressed accents for a celebratory, bespoke card.

In the same way you might enjoy meticulously wrapping a gift, baking a holiday dish, or trimming a tree, taking the extra time to send a meaningful greeting to families and friends may be as much a gift to yourself as it is to those who receive your brilliant Minted-made card. Minted makes it easy to celebrate the season and foster connection.

#6869. 6 Antique Trends Designers Are Watching for 2027

Above: The kitchen in designer Phillip Thomas’s Bellport retreat.

Do the words “antique” and “trend” really belong in the same sentence? In many ways, a great antique shop feels like the antidote to trend fatigue: a space where the true must-haves are hidden in dusty corners, with part of the fun in the thrill of the hunt. Yet, as we round out 2026, there’s no denying the current design cycle is casting off perfection for patina. Antiques are en vogue, and top designers are keeping a close eye on the periods, pieces, and yes, trends, that are poised to find new life in 2027.

“The return of interest in antiques reflects a trend towards more personal, unique interiors, and away from strict modern rooms which can often be cool and generic,” says ELLE Decor A-List designer Jamie Drake. This shift paves the way for more playful interactions between pieces, unexpected layering, and unabashed personality.

Other experts second the motion for more character. “I’m seeing clients really drawn to antiques with more to them than meets the eye: pieces that have a bit of humor or whimsy, or pieces with great provenance,” says ELLE Decor Rising Star William Cullum. (He lists a “ridiculously giant butterfly light fixture” from New Orleans, rattan chairs from Bing Crosby’s estate, and a disco ball from Cyndi Lauper’s Bonham’s sale in his list of recent acquisitions—professional and personal.)

Luckily for the amateur antiquer, not all trending vintage styles require a substantial space commitment or glamorous provenance—although an interesting backstory helps. “Antiques are always about a story: where you found them, who owned them, and how that story will continue on after us,” Cullum says.

Ahead, we asked top designers and experts to share the most inspiring antique trends of the moment. From neoclassic mirrors to hand-painted chests paired with modern art, their takes are sure to inform your next estate sale rummage, or inspire you to restyle an old heirloom.

We called mirrored surfaces a major trend to watch for fall 2026, and antique trends have followed suit. Unexpected (or better yet, artfully manipulated) reflections can add rich dimension and movement to any space, but in this category, antiques create a window to the past that’s tough to beat.

“One of the easiest ways to add a sense of history to a room is to include an antique mirror,” says Drake. “Whether you choose a sober and strict neo-classic piece or a more flamboyant baroque one, reflections of the past add depth and sparkle.”

“One trend I’m observing is an uptick in antique dowry chests,” antiques dealer Ali Mahon says. From Pennsylvania Dutch to Swedish Folk and Spanish carved, she’s noticed clients gravitating toward more delicately adorned storage over spare. With thoughtfully mixed furniture (from style to period) becoming more desirable than matching sets, Mahon sees more hand-painted antique surfaces in our future: “With their decorative tulips, leaves, birds, and hearts, dowry chests create a colorful splash in any room.”

ELLE Decor Rising Star Reagan Gail Struble agrees—although delicate designs are not a requirement of the trend, in her book: “Something about a playful wash of color on an awesome old piece of furniture is very alluring, especially when you can see the age and texture of the wood peeking through,” she says. “Hand-painted case pieces add a little punch of charm to any room.”

Luckily, these antiques can be just as functional as they are fashionable. Mahon recommends using a painted chest as a coffee table, at the foot of the bed, or in a space that could benefit from a bit more romance (and a few extra drawers). When styling your find in a modern space, Struble says that contrast is key: try a “chippy Swedish chest of drawers” with an unexpected photograph or painting above it.

According to ELLE Decor A-List designer Nick Olsen, Art Deco pieces are setting the tone for 2027 antique trends—although, if you ask him, they were never out. “I'm particularly inspired by the [art deco influence in the] Gujarat palace in India,” he says. “I'm fascinated by how every element seems to have a wide border: marble floors, carpets, walls of veneer and mohair velvet upholstery.”

According to Olsen, using Art Deco design compositions can do as much for a room as actual pieces from the period. “I like the idea of contrasting borders: solid textures punctuated by smaller graphic prints on furniture and more delicate lighting,” he explains. And while borders are easy to create with paint, paneling, and carpet seaming, Olsen challenges clients to push the Art Deco agenda further by bordering with luxe materials.

Whether you’re new to antiquing or simply trying to add more character to a space, lighting is an excellent place to start. “Lighting is the jewelry of the home, so it’s one of my favorite places to bring in something vintage,” says A-Lister Corey Damen Jenkins. “An Art Deco sconce or an antique brass lantern has a warmth to it that’s hard to find in something brand new.”

Adding to an existing space? Jenkins advises starting small: “Swap one overhead fixture or put a pair of vintage lamps on a console, and you’ll be surprised how much the whole room shifts,” he says. “It’s an easy way to add history without starting over.”

“In light of America’s 250th birthday, Americana in general is in vogue,” says Mahon. Among her clients, the trend has manifested in quilts and textiles, portraits of sea captains, trade signs, and ironstone—culminating in an aesthetic she describes as “elevated country—not corny.”

If you’ve noticed that no particular design period seems to reign supreme among designers’ top antique trend picks, it’s not an accident. Heading into 2027, there’s a strong argument to be made that mixed-period design is more influential than any singular reference.

“I love a room that doesn’t belong to any one decade,” Jenkins says. “A nineteenth-century chest with a 1970s lamp on top, or a Lucite chair pulled up to an antique desk. Those are the pairings that make people stop and look twice.” Drake adds that seemingly out-of-place seating is an easy way to add intrigue. “I love including antique chairs as sculptural notes, contrasted against modern upholstery and tables,” he says.

But when it comes to antiquing, there’s really only so much one can plan for. Letting a great find lead the way often turns out to be a better strategy than arriving to an estate sale with a rigid checklist. The best way to keep up with top designers when it comes to antiquing this year? Find a piece that has something to say, place it where it seems to belong, and then try it where it doesn’t.

Grace McCarty is a freelance Associate Digital Editor at ELLE Decor, where she covers design trends, shopping, and culture. She previously held a staff position at SELF Magazine, where she focused on beauty, style, and wellness. Her work has also appeared in Glamour and Parade.

#6865. The Kitchen Trends Designers Expect to See Everywhere in 2027

The National Kitchen & Bath Association’s 2027 forecast has arrived.

Above: the kitchen of an Athens home designed by Nashmita Rajiah.

Partial walls, hidden appliances, and pantries with room for prep are among the kitchen features expected to gain ground next year, according to the National Kitchen & Bath Association’s 2027 Kitchen Trends Report. Based on a survey of 830 kitchen-industry professionals—including designers, manufacturers, remodelers, and architects—the report looks at the layouts, materials, lighting, and technology they expect to be popular over the next three years. Here are the main takeaways.

NKBA reports growing interest in partial walls, movable partitions, and separate kitchen zones—a shift from last year’s forecast, when open layouts were still gaining ground. Dedicated prep spaces and back kitchens are also emerging features, cited by nearly half of designers.

Kitchens are still expected to get bigger, with 52 percent of designers anticipating growth. The kitchen’s surrounding rooms are growing, too. NKBA reports that mudrooms and laundry spaces are increasingly being integrated into kitchen plans. The vast majority of designers expect attached mudrooms to be popular over the next three years, with 45 percent anticipating mudrooms getting larger and 43 percent expecting laundry spaces to grow.

Appliances are increasingly being incorporated into cabinetry rather than left on display. Eighty-five percent of designers agree cabinets are being designed to hide appliances and other functional elements, and roughly two-thirds say open shelving is on its way out in favor of enclosed storage.

Pantries are taking on more work, too. Nearly 90 percent agree they are becoming multifunctional extensions of the kitchen, and three-quarters expect dedicated small-appliance stations to be popular. Separate beverage areas continue to gain support, with 90 percent anticipating their popularity, up from 85 percent in the 2026 report.

Neutrals remain the leading color of choice, followed by greens and blues. For cabinetry, light and medium tones draw equal interest, and white oak is the preferred wood for roughly half of designers. Islands don’t necessarily have to match: a little more than half expect them to feature a different color from the surrounding cabinets. Quartzite and quartz lead countertop preferences, with nearly half of designers favoring honed or matte finishes.

The lighting forecast includes under-cabinet lights, illuminated cabinet interiors, nighttime path lighting, sconces, and pendants. Eighty-four percent of designers agree kitchens will incorporate multiple layers of light, and 78 percent say lighting is increasingly being used as a design feature rather than just a functional necessity. Under-cabinet lighting leads the forecast at 87 percent, followed by interior-cabinet lighting at 76 percent.

NKBA identifies ventilation, water filtration, touchless faucets, and sound-absorbing materials among the report’s wellness features. Designers are also discussing ways to make kitchens more resilient: 65 percent cite leak-detection systems and automatic shut-off valves, 62 percent cite backup power, and a little over half point to ventilation and air filtration.

Julia Cancilla is the social media & news editor at ELLE Decor, where she oversees the brand's socials and covers design, pop culture, and emerging trends. She also authors the monthly ELLE Decoroscope column. Her work has appeared in Inked magazine, House Beautiful, Marie Claire, and more.

#6861. House Party: Angelica Hicks

With a new Paperless Post collection, the illustrator shares her rules for a party worth RSVPing yes to.

Welcome to House Party, where we ask expert hosts for their top entertaining tips, tricks, and tabletop tactics. Come party with us.

Angelica Hicks has a talent for making high fashion out of everyday materials. The British artist, illustrator, and fashion satirist is known for using household supplies to recreate runway and red-carpet looks, capturing couture’s most elaborate details with candy wrappers and duct tape. A sharp eye runs in the family: Her father is the artist and interior designer Ashley Hicks, and her grandfather was the influential decorator David Hicks. But her visual wit is distinctly her own, evident in everything from her illustrative book Tongue in Chic to her collaborations with Gucci.

Her newly launched invitation collection for Paperless Post brings that humor to entertaining, with designs including a shrimp holding a martini. Hicks’s own hosting philosophy is similarly spirited, though she’s quite serious about a well-considered seating chart. For House Party, we asked her about the best parties she’s attended, the merits of an emergency ice delivery, and the extravagance she’d happily import from Japan.

ELLE Decor: What are your three essentials when hosting?

Angelica Hicks: A great invite that sets the tone before the event. A shrimp holding a martini inviting you to a cocktail party perhaps?

What’s your top entertaining/hosting tip?

People love to complain about placement, but I am pro placement. I think telling people where to sit, and actually spending time considering it, will only benefit the flow of conversation—especially when mixing people who might not already know each other. I think it’s a fabulous thing to do when hosting a dinner party.

Who, dead or alive, is coming to your dream dinner party?

Noël Coward, Joan Crawford, Aretha Franklin, Cristóbal Balenciaga.

Announcing dietary restrictions upon arrival.

If you could add one exorbitantly extravagant component to a party, what would it be?

I think I would fly in urchin from Japan if it were the season.

Who threw the best party you’ve ever attended?

Unemployed Magazine always throws an excellent Halloween party. What makes it so great is that they select one iconic person as a theme and everyone dresses up as that person or someone in the universe. Last year was Liz Taylor, and the year before was Princess Diana.

I'm ashamed to say I rarely bring anything. But once I have arrived, if I notice there is something missing, I will order it, which I think is far more helpful. A bag of ice can come in much more handy than a mid bottle of red.

What historic party would you have liked to have attended?

I think I would have had great fun dressing up for the Rothschild Surrealist Ball.

Buy too much wine; you'll probably go through it.

I don’t have a dishwasher, so I always lament the washing up.

Julia Cancilla is the social media & news editor at ELLE Decor, where she oversees the brand's socials and covers design, pop culture, and emerging trends. She also authors the monthly ELLE Decoroscope column. Her work has appeared in Inked magazine, House Beautiful, Marie Claire, and more.

#6860. Is the Fitbit Edge Worth Buying?

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The future form of the wearable tech is here.

It’s been a pretty big year for Fitbit. In May, the Fitbit Air joined the ever-growing list of no-screen fitness trackers alongside Whoop, and now, not even six months later, I have on my wrist the brand-new Fitbit Edge. It is, as the name suggests, a slimmer fitness tracker. It feels slightly like a step forward, slightly like a step back—a return to form. Let’s say. It’s new, but it’s also a throwback to the slim shape of the (not so) old Fitbit devices like the Charge and Inspire. Importantly, despite being Google’s latest wearable with a screen, it is not trying to be a Pixel Watch. Far from it. Some might complain about what it can’t do, but I’d argue the pared-back nature of the Fitbit Edge is its greatest strength.

At its core, this is the state-of-the-art Fitbit bringing a smooth, responsive modern touchscreen experience to a wearable focused on health tracking. It certainly does more than count your steps, monitor heart rate, and detect REM cycles; the Edge has plenty of smartwatch capabilities. Control your music, check the weather, even read and respond (depending on the phone you have) to messages. But the standard inundation of notifications is replaced by a clean interface that clearly does not like being disturbed. This is the casual fitness tracker for those who value a distraction-free workout.

First things first. I’ve been wearing mine 24/7 for the past couple of weeks, and it’s nothing if not supremely comfortable. Bands are simpler to attach and detach than any other watch I’ve tried, and the new magnet bands are easy to make snug.

From the get-go, the band selection has been excellent. My personal favorite is the magnetic silicone band in Mahogany. It’s got a look that comes off as more upscale than the materials described typically do. Paired with the slim bezel and overall slight shape, it’s a handsome wearable that draws a lot of compliments. I won’t repeat what one friend of mine said, but I’m pretty sure it means she liked it.

The completely revamped Fitbit interface is intuitive and has everything you need just a swipe or tap away by default. You can tinker with the home screen, but having heart rate and total steps, alongside the time and a quick workout start button, displayed by default feels instructional. Everything is readable and I genuinely feel like my mother-in-law could understand how to use it. Like I said, it’s not a Pixel Watch, but it’s certainly taking usability and design cues from Google’s other line of wearables.

As a Fitbit, nearly everything about the Edge is an evolution. The only problem is I’m also comparing it to all the smartwatches out on the market too. I can use literally any other smartwatch in my rotation to tap into my office building with a digital badge, and pay for lunch with Apple Pay or whatever equivalent. With the Edge, no luck on any of these NFC-powered interactions. And because I’m using it with my iPhone, I can read my incoming messages but can’t respond to anything.

On paper, these are flaws, but in reality they allow me to interact with my fitness tracker without getting too distracted. The lack of customizability compared to an Apple or Pixel Watch simply equals less time sitting around and setting up your watch, and hopefully more time going outside and living your life. Which isn’t to say I wasn’t bugged by some of the limitations. The viewable data is not quite as in-depth as some others, and looking at your phone to check your reps sorta ruins the whole distraction-free thing.

In that way, it’s more like a fitness band than a smartwatch. It can track a lot of data on its own, but ultimately the only way to see all of that is to eventually open your phone. For me, that trade-off is worth it to not be getting constant buzzes on my wrist every time I go for a run.

When it comes to fitness tracking, the hardcore still have Garmin. For something more casual, the Fitbit Edge has a great look, and as an Apple Watch user, certain aspects of the simplicity are genuinely refreshing.

#6857. On the Couch with Christopher John Rogers

In honor of his new collaboration with The Rug Company, the fashion designer shares his inspirations and style icons.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Christopher John Rogers has an eye for color. The fashion designer, born in Baton Rouge and based in New York, established his self-titled label in 2016. Over the past decade, he’s become known for his use of bold tones and voluminous silhouettes, and for dressing the likes of Michelle Obama and Cardi B. Now, Rogers is taking his style to the floor in a new collaboration with The Rug Company.

The collection, which Rogers designed with Will Cooper, creative director of The Rug Company, feels alive, with vibrant, swooshing marks, multicolored doodles in wool and silk, and wavy threads that appear to dance across the floor. “The scribbles, the brushstrokes, the happy accidents, the lines that don't quite meet,” Rogers tells ELLE Decor. “I love the idea of taking something spontaneous and seemingly incidental and rendering it permanent through this incredibly meticulous, centuries-old craft.”

Launching tomorrow, the collaboration includes six styles across ten colorways. But Rogers has more planned beyond womenswear, “exploring new ways for the brand to exist beyond the wardrobe,” he says. “I'm particularly excited by opportunities that allow us to think about color, proportion, texture, and materiality through different disciplines, whether that's furniture, interiors, or something entirely unexpected.”

Below, Rogers takes ELLE Decor’s On the Couch survey, sharing a few of his favorite pieces, destinations, and entertaining takes.

Favorite Sofa: Pierre Paulin's ABCD sofa for Artifort. The color theory is delicious, and I'd happily sacrifice a little comfort for something that fabulous.

Architect You Admire: Ettore Sottsass. I love the clarity of his vision and his ability to make the simplest shapes feel monumental.

Destination Museum: The Louisiana Museum of Modern Art in Denmark

Go-to Galleries: 52 Walker, Broadway Gallery, and Salon 94 in New York.

Favorite Artist/Artwork: I've recently fallen for Domenica Brockman. We have one of her paintings in the studio, and I'm constantly struck by how much emotion she can communicate through deceptively simple arrangements of color.

Vintage Piece of Your Dreams: An original Verner Panton Living Tower.

Hotels to Check Into: Hotel Saint Vincent in New Orleans, and La Mamounia in Marrakech.

Entertaining Tips: Great lighting, a boozy cocktail, and a playlist with a point of view. Don't overthink it!

Style Icons: Bert and Ernie, and Isaac Mizrahi.

Required Reading: Interaction of Color by Josef Albers and The Bluest Eye by Toni Morrison. Two very different but equally essential ways of learning to see.

Cocktail or Mocktail of Choice: It's a toss-up between a wet Roku martini and a Cynar spritz.

Favorite Restaurant: Cervo’s in the Lower East Side, Rolo's in Ridgewood, Frenchette in Tribeca, and Santa Panza in Bushwick.

Favorite Jeweler: Presley Oldham and Bernard James for contemporary jewelry, and vintage Alexander Calder or Alber Elbaz-era Lanvin for something a little more fantastical.

Annie Goldsmith is the senior editor and digital lead at ELLE Decor, where she covers design, culture, style, and trends. She previously held positions at The Information, covering technology and culture, and Town & Country, writing about news, entertainment, and fashion. Her work has also appeared in Vogue, Rolling Stone, and the SF Standard.

#6853. Why Everyone Is Wearing Suede This Fall—And the Chicest Ways to Style It

When the temperatures drop and fall dressing takes center stage, one fabric always finds its way back into our wardrobes: suede. Suede has been that fall fashion staple ever since it has been around. It brings that cozy, elevated feel to even the simplest outfits, making it the perfect texture to embrace when the seasons change.

While it’s common to see suede generally in the fall, lately it’s absolutely everywhere, and I’m here to remind you that there are so many ways you can wear this texture. From suede mini skirts paired with oversized sweaters, knee-high suede boots, and of course purses.

Suede is such a perfect fall trend because it brings a subtle touch of texture to any outfit. Shades like chocolate brown and deep burgundy make the material feel especially autumnal.

Consider this your sign to give your fall wardrobe a suede refresh. Whether you go all in with a statement skirt or keep things simple with a chic suede bag, this is one trend that makes every outfit feel a little more luxurious. And if you need some inspiration, I’ve got you covered with some trendy looks below.

There is something so effortlessly chic about a suede skirt. It’s a fun piece I’d wear to work, but styled like the above makes for such a cute day or night time look. The addition of the colored tights and shoes elevates the whole moment, so if you’re a maximalist this suede look is for you.

The simplest way to tap into the suede trend is with your footwear. There are so many popular suede shoes meant for the fall and winter season and these boots are the perfect ones.

Even though I am not a big hat girl, a suede hat like this is a fun way to test the waters. It’s easy and adds to any fall outfit you’re building.

Another staple way to tap into suede is with accessories, specifically a good purse. This dark brown suede shoulder bag is so stylish and paired with the all-white outfit is a texture combo I’m obsessing over.

Of course, no fall wardrobe is complete without a statement jacket. Suede blazers, like the one above, add instant personality to your everyday looks. They’re the kind of investment pieces that make getting dressed feel exciting, even on the busiest mornings.

#6852. For His 40th Birthday, Drake Flexed a Custom Rolex and a $250,000 Watch in the Space of 24 Hours

The rapper marked his milestone birthday with two extravagant timepieces, including a one-of-a-kind Daytona worth millions.

Turning 40 is a milestone worth celebrating in style. And to nobody's surprise, Drake did just that.

As you'd expect, the Canadian rapper's birthday outfit was not exactly understated. In fact, his watch rotation featured not one, but two rather extraordinary Rolexes. Why settle for one when you can have two?

The first was a custom, sapphire-covered Daytona, while the second was a colorful Day-Date 36 with a reported six-figure price tag. There are certainly worse ways to welcome a new decade.

First up was the Rolex Cosmograph Daytona, a watch that makes even the most extravagant diamond-set timepieces look positively modest. The one-of-a-kind creation features a diamond-pavé dial, brilliant blue sapphire hour markers, and a bezel set with baguette-cut blue sapphires. But the real showstopper is the bracelet, which is covered in a gradient of blue sapphires that gradually fade into diamonds.

And if all those precious stones weren't enough to make your eyes water, the estimated price tag certainly might. The one-of-a-kind creation has reportedly been valued at anywhere between $2 million and $5 million. Although, given that Rolex made just one, it's difficult to put an exact figure on it.

While you might assume the rapper had commissioned the watch especially for his big birthday, he actually debuted it back in August. Still, when you've got something quite this spectacular sitting in your watch collection, it would be a shame not to give it another outing.

The second Rolex was a rather different kind of extravagance. Drake also showed off a rose-gold Day-Date 36 featuring the brand's famously playful puzzle dial. Instead of traditional hour markers, baguette-cut sapphires surround a dial made up of brightly colored jigsaw pieces. And rather than displaying the usual day and date, the apertures reveal inspirational words and emojis, including a little red heart. It's about as far from your grandfather's Rolex as you can get.

The watch has been valued at around $250,000 which, rather remarkably, makes it the considerably cheaper of Drake's two birthday watches.

Of course, Drake has never been one for doing things by halves, particularly when it comes to watches. And while most people would be more than happy to receive a single Rolex for their 40th, it seems the rapper couldn't quite settle on one.

#6849. Winter Date Ideas to Heat Up Your Love Life This Season

Time to turn those dark, cold nights into hot date nights.

I’m afraid the rumors are true: Winter is, in fact, upon us. But while the colder weather may find you ready to cuddle up with takeout and TV ‘til spring, let this be your reminder that winter does not mean you have to put your dating life on hold. While this may not be the sexiest time of year, it is the coziest—which means that the cooler months ahead are filled with romantic date-night potential. To help you make the most of that potential, allow me to present this list of the best winter date ideas for you and whoever you’re cuddling up to this season.

Whether you’re already happily cuffed and committed, single and seeking, or feeling things out with a new crush, there’s something on this list for every kind of winter date vibe. Prepare to fill your cold-weather calendar with these 18 winter date ideas.

What better way to heat up your winter nights than an evening of laughs with the one you love? And by the way, you don’t have to shell out for a Netflix Special taping (although if you can swing it, by all means). Tons of venues in cities across the country regularly host affordable standup shows where you can grab some food or a drink and enjoy a chill night filled with plenty of laughs.

To be clear, you can support the arts any time. But there’s just something a little fancy-yet-cozy about winter ballet.

In a similar if much sexier vein, nothing beats a burlesque date night. If you’re New York–based (or have an NYC trip coming up this holiday season) I literally can’t recommend Company XIV’s Nutcracker Rouge highly enough. Get tickets here!

Can you technically see a play any time of year? Yes, of course. But you can’t tell me there’s not something about “going to the theater” that just seems to ooze a certain wintery sophistication. Whether it’s a Broadway show or a more intimate performance at your local theater, catching some live entertainment is a perfect way to spend a winter night out on the town.

If you’re down to class things up a bit this winter, nothing will make you feel more sexy and sophisticated than a jazz club date night—just you and your date, sipping cocktails and enjoying some live tunes.

If you and your person are already seasoned slope-hitters, then a ski trip is a given. But if you don’t know your way around the slopes yet, why not take a lesson together? It’s a great opportunity to learn something new, get outside, and make the most of the cold weather—and it comes with a built-in excuse to warm up with a steaming mug of hot chocolate (or your après-ski beverage of choice) afterwards.

Sometimes an intimate evening with a home-cooked meal you prepare together is actually more romantic than dining out. Pick a recipe you both want to try, open a bottle of wine, and channel your inner chef.

Plenty of companies now offer virtual wine tastings you can do from the comfort of your own home. They send the wine to you, and you and your date can follow along as an expert guides you through the various selections in a Zoom-style seminar. What better way to spend a night in than exploring new wines together and refining your sommelier sensibilities?

Alexa, play “Christmas Tree Farm” by Taylor Swift. Whether or not you actually want to perform the manual labor of cutting down a live tree, lugging it home, and setting it up when you could just order a fake from Amazon, spending a cozy day at a Christmas tree farm is an iconic holi-date. It’s outdoorsy without being too physically demanding, it’s brimming with cute selfie potential, and you can basically pretend you’re in the cheesy-yet-addictive holiday rom-com of your dreams. What more could you ask for?

Get into the holiday spirit and cross some seasonal chores off your to-do list by turning holiday decorating into a cute date night. Best of all: You get to stay inside and keep your comfy clothes on.

This one may sound a little less fun, but hear me out. Un-decorating is sad and boring and work—but turning it into date night can make an otherwise tedious chore actually kind of fun! Grab some wine, throw on a movie or set the mood with some music, and un-deck the halls.

While some of your summertime haunts may be closed for the season, plenty of typically summery destinations are actually open year-round. Not to mention, a lot of them are probably less expensive and less crowded during the off-season. Plan a trip to your favorite beach town or seaside locale and see what it’s like in the winter. Chances are it’s probably a lot more peaceful.

Spending a night together in a hotel is a great way to get out of the house and treat yourselves to a romantic mini-getaway. Some hotels even offer special holiday/winter packages for the season.

Nothing says cuffing season like meeting the parents! But even if you’re not ready to take that step, a little trip to your hometown to show your crush all the old haunts is a great way to spend a winter weekend and indulge in a little nostalgia.

Sometimes it can be tough to work up the motivation to actually get up and go out in the winter. It’s dark, it’s cold, your seasonal depression is acting up, etc. But that’s exactly why you deserve to treat yourselves to a luxe night out. You know that fancy restaurant you’ve been saving for a special occasion? Screw the special occasion. Go now, for no reason other than you can.

What better way to unwind and beat the winter blues than a spa date? Treat yourselves to a couple’s massage, a relaxing sauna steam, and/or some beauty treatments that your dry winter skin will definitely thank you for.

Lots of bars offer mixology classes where you and your date can not only enjoy some delicious seasonal cocktails but also learn to make them yourselves. If you’d rather not leave the house, there are also virtual options you can attend online from the comfort of your own home.

Going to the movies is a lost art. Catch a new release (Wuthering Heights, anyone?) and enjoy your date’s company while snacking on popcorn, candy, and slurpees

#6847. Reckoning With Borat, 20 Years Later

Last month, I sat in a packed-to-the-gills movie theater to experience the latest release from a controversial comedian whose work regularly blurs the line between docu-reality and narrative fiction. I am, of course, referring to Ali G: Who Iz I?—the cinematic return of Sacha Baron Cohen’s once-and-still-somewhat-beloved durag-doffing titular character.

Before the film was unceremoniously dumped on Prime Video last week, I managed to snag a ticket to the only New York City screening that wasn’t totally sold-out, booking a seat—last row, tucked in an alcove of the theater—with the intention of isolated note-taking and (gasp!) the occasional phone-glance should the movie prove tedious. The recliners next to me were eventually claimed by a gentleman decked in a very Ali G-esque skull cap and wraparounds, who unleashed a deafening “BOOYAKASHA!” after Nicole Kidman’s AMC intro.

Cohen’s brand of confrontational cringe comedy continues to attract a level of interest, thanks in part to a certain mustached-and-mankini’d Kazakh journalist. The cultural explosion that was Borat! Cultural Learnings of America for Make Benefit Glorious Nation of Kazakhstan (has any movie title so padded the word count?) turns 20 years old this week, and it remains a curious artifact of the 2000s: impossibly dated and slightly better than you might remember, even as its creator’s talent has slowly and unmistakably eroded.

If you were just old enough to purchase a ticket to an R-rated movie in 2006, Borat felt like a truly out-of-nowhere phenomenon in terms of mass impact. But the character, armed with little more than a shabby suit and an aggressive cheer, emerged from Cohen’s cult favorite Da Ali G Show, which ran for one season on BBC’s Channel 4 in 2000 before making the leap to HBO in 2003. Alongside Ali G and the gay Austrian fashion correspondent Brüno, Borat Sagdiyev was just one of the many personalities Cohen regularly deployed in the thorny thickets of gotcha-mentary, trading in culturally oblivious and antisemitic observations supposedly inherited from his Kazakh homeland, often to the shock and dismay of his unsuspecting subjects.

These fool-me-once hijinks weren’t necessarily groundbreaking, even for HBO’s then-provocative late-night programming. Several years prior, The Chris Rock Show engaged in similar man-on-the-street shenanigans (once, he polled residents of NYC’s notoriously hate-crime-prone Howard Beach region about renaming a street after Tupac Shakur). But segments in which Ali G addressed Buzz Aldrin as Buzz Lightyear, or the one where Borat led a bar full of Arizonans in a rousing chorus of “Throw the Jew Down the Well,” were practically reverse-engineered for virality in the internet’s pre-social-media infancy, and the appeal of Cohen’s approach—how far will he go, and will ordinary folk follow him?—was easy to understand, regardless of the visceral discomfort it may have generated.

Straying from the sketch-based format of Da Ali G Show, Borat embraced a loose narrative structure, with its titular inquirer setting out for America to make a documentary on the country’s social peccadilloes with the intent of eventually romancing Pamela Anderson. If you want to get highfalutin about it, the plot itself situates Borat within the legacy of films about Europeans attempting to understand America—a small-but-fascinating subgenre that includes Paul Verhoeven’s fairytale-cum-nightmare Showgirls, the post-hippie pessimism of Michaelangelo Antonioni’s Zabriskie Point, and Kristoffer Borgli’s social-mores rom-dramedy The Drama.

Don’t get it twisted, though: Borat was and still is best enjoyed as a showcase of physical comedy and inflammatory non sequiturs. It’s no less funny in 2026 to watch a grown man wash his face in a toilet bowl, or gesture toward a tortoise while sincerely asking, “What type of dog is this?” The scene in which an etiquette coach attempts to guide Borat through the intricacies of comic timing is itself an excellent study in comic timing; the notorious full-frontal hotel chase sequence between Borat and his producer Azamat (portrayed by steadily-working character actor Ken Davitian) is a marvel for its sheer escalation alone. This stuff killed in a massive Northern New Jersey movie theater when I was 19, and it still kills from the confines of my couch 20 years on.

Unlike much of Cohen’s subsequent work, Borat is not an explicitly political film. Nonetheless, a good deal of its “GREAT SUCCESS!”—$262 million in box receipts off an $18 million budget, with a Best Actor Golden Globe for Cohen himself—is undoubtedly owed to a different sort of comic timing. The film came out in the throes of George W. Bush’s second presidential term, with the US mired in two wars abroad and a hell of its own making. Americans were thirsty for easy targets to shoulder the blame and, if possible, provide some cheap laughs in the process.

Their thirst was quenched in the form of Borat’s many unsuspecting subjects. Some of this was low-hanging fruit: Borat telling a group of feminists to smile more, or acting like a literal bull in a china shop amidst Confederate memorabilia. But the film’s more sensational material depicted rough corners of the American psyche long hidden in plain sight. Most memorably, Borat encounters a group of rowdy frat boys who huff about white pride and express dismay at the abolishment of slavery before engaging in a viewing of Anderson’s infamous leaked sex tape with ex-husband Tommy Lee.

Not everyone was displeased at their portrayal, though. Joe and Miriam Behar, the Jewish couple who offered Borat and Azamat room and board, were good-natured in a 2006 interview with the Boston Globe. For her part, Miriam shrugged off Borat’s naked antisemitism. “To me it's an anti-Muslim movie, not anti-Jewish, because Kazakhstan is mostly Muslim,” she offered.

Behar’s observation underlines the casual bigotry that otherwise stains Borat. The film’s portrayal of Kazakh culture is undoubtedly offensive, and provoked mixed reactions from the country’s government for years to come. For what it’s worth, Cohen pushes back—intentionally or not—on anti-Muslim sentiments during the film’s rodeo sequence, in which Borat tells a particularly racist attendee that he is indeed “not Muslim.”

But given the post-9/11 climate into which Borat was launched, the implications of a foreign correspondent from a majority-Muslim country espousing antisemitic and misogynistic viewpoints are crystal-clear, and Cohen himself seems too smart to sincerely profess a lack of awareness as to how this might be received. It doesn’t help that his subsequent projects resulted in exponential escalations of offense: 2012’s more narratively-focused The Dictator spared no quarter when it came to stereotypes of Arab culture, while the astoundingly homophobic Brüno, from 2009, was loaded with gags about anal sex and resulted in an out-of-court settlement with a Palestinian grocer who was falsely credited as a former terrorist in the film itself.

After 2016’s box-office bomb Grimsby, which is most notable for the allegations of on-set sexual harassment co-star Rebel Wilson later brought toward Cohen, his work went in a more explicitly political direction. There was his 2018 docu-satire series Who Is America?—two episodes of which featured writing and directing contributions from Nathan Fielder—and 2020’s Borat Subsequent Moviefilm, a second-verse-same-as-the-first sequel which earned several Oscar nominations and brought a truly bizarre “October surprise” to that year’s presidential election involving actress Maria Bakalova, Rudy Giuliani, and the former New York City Mayor’s pants zipper.

Both of those films were more explicitly message-y than they were riotously funny. Cohen’s latest, Ali G: Who Iz I?, is neither, carrying the all-too-familiar stink of a comedian insisting that their capacity to offend amounts to protected speech. At 55, Cohen is clearly not as willing to submit himself to the ragdoll physics he once deployed; in its place is an often-sullen-looking Ali G, his dreads now gray, weighing the merits of cultural appropriation and sparring with a DEI coordinator about the origins of the durag. The entire affair is belabored and depressing.

It made me think of the Who Is America? character Dr. Nira Cain-N'Degeocello, a send-up of Rachel Dolezal-flavored liberals in the form of a Berkeley professor who dons dashikis and keffiyehs. Early in the film, he rages against Ali G’s culturally appropriative ways to a majority-Black community meeting in Oakland. “We don’t care!,” shouts one participant. In hindsight, it’s as much an expression of exhaustion with the dictates of capital-D discourse as it is a referendum on Cohen’s confrontational method, which now yields far less interesting insights about the national psyche than it did when Borat arrived on American soil in 2006.

#6845. Designers Say These 6 Paint Colors Are the Secret to a More Vintage-Looking Home

If you have a brand-new home or just bought a builder-grade house, you may find that your walls are whispering—or maybe even begging—for some character. Or perhaps your home already has vintage bones and tapping into its history feels right. Whatever the case, picking a vintage-inspired paint color can help add a sense of heritage and character to the space before you even spend a dime on furniture or accessories.

Some colors have a modern feel, while others are distinctively old-fashioned. For that nostalgic touch, we consulted interior designers about their go-to paint colors that read as vintage, from charming yellow to dramatic burgundy.

Inspired by the English fine china brand, this soft, dusty shade of blue-gray feels like it’s been living in a room for decades. “It’s the kind of color you find in old European farmhouses or well-loved coastal cottages,” says Jessica Risko Smith, interior designer at Santa Barbara firm JRS ID. “It reads differently in every light: almost slate in the morning, almost soft denim by afternoon.” Her team used it throughout this kitchen renovation to ground the space and give brand-new cabinetry an immediate sense of age and permanence.

This creamy yellow hue skyrocketed in popularity over the last few years, but it’s always been a go-to for cozy, lived-in interiors. Andrew Suvalsky, a New York City-based interior designer, says, “Butter yellow is a color that is as strong as it is youthful and playful. It’s a bridge straight to the 1960s and is as relevant today as it was then.” Whether used on a ceiling or in a kitchen, it adds a sun-drenched character you would find in a home that's been loved for decades.

Rich, saturated tones like oxblood or deep plum instantly add a sense of history and depth to a space. This hue “brings a refined, almost heritage-inspired warmth that feels both dramatic and timeless,” says Iona Graham, interior design expert at Graham & Brown. “It’s particularly effective in smaller rooms or on accent walls, where it can create a cocooning, layered atmosphere reminiscent of classic interiors.” With its moody saturation, this vintage-inspired paint color is also perfect for color drenching.

White is one of those hues that comes in a surprising range of shades. Some lean blue and bright, others feel more golden. A warm parchment shade sits somewhere in the middle, offering a timeless, lived-in take on this go-to color family. “Nothing gives a new build an instant sense of history like a genuine warm parchment—not stark white, but something with yellow or blush undertones that softens edges and feels sun-faded in the best possible way,” Smith says.

For an even deeper infusion of warmth, a peach hue with rosy undertones will do the trick. This bold hue has shown up across the decades, with splashier iterations in the 1980s and softer, muted examples found across the midcentury. Suvalsky says, “This color is great because it is both fresh and cozy at the same time. It works in all seasons and across all eras of furnishings, bringing the romance of golden hour and a subtle retro feeling.”

If you’re thinking blue—but make it saturated—a paint inspired by the crisp night sky will do. It’s rooted in nature, and that’s one of the very reasons why it carries so much vintage appeal. “It is regal, historic, and architectural all at the same time,” Suvalsky explains. “It structures a room as though it’s been there forever, and knows why.” In this wet bar project, deep midnight blue contrasts against a gilded ceiling, warm wood tones, and brass fixtures.

#6844. Joseph Zada and Mckenna Grace Have Thoughts About Fans Shipping Them Off-Screen

The co-stars have seen your fancams—and they’re setting the record straight.

Mckenna Grace and Joseph Zada may play acquaintances-turned-District 12 tributes in The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping, but that hasn’t stopped fans from shipping them in real life.

Well, the co-stars have seen all those fancams and social media threads, and they recently addressed those hoping their on-screen chemistry translates to something romantic IRL.

The actors debunked any notion that they’re anything more than good friends, fully bursting into laughter when Refinery29 Australia asked how they felt about people shipping them off-screen.

“It’s pretty weird,” Joseph admitted, before adding, “Mckenna does feel like my younger, younger sister.”

Mckenna also chimed in, saying, “Joe’s like family. I mean…I think that’s the fun of the internet, is that they can ship whatever characters or people or anything that they want, as long as it’s not intruding on people’s own lives.”

“Yeah, I’ve been shipped with Ben [Wang],” Joe jokingly added, referring to another one of their castmates.

The pair have long maintained that they’re just friends—not to mention that, as Joseph says in the video, he and Mckenna have “both got partners” IRL. Who is Joseph’s partner, you ask? That would be Polly Talarico, who he met in a bar in Sydney earlier this year, according to his recent interview with Vanity Fair.

Mckenna is currently linked to her “best friend” and Regretting You co-star, Mason Thames, who she gushed about during her Cosmopolitan cover story when she was asked about her love life off-screen.

“Your girl is happy. I’m feeling very lovey-dovey,” she shared. “I have somebody I can be a complete nerd with. I got my best friend, and so I don’t see the point in not loving loudly.”

She added that she and Mason are hanging out on the sets of each other’s projects so much that “he’s like an honorary member of District 12 at this point.”

“There are moments that are just for you and your person. But I don’t see the point in not celebrating who you’re in love with,” she said of their public relationship. “A lot of people’s hesitation around this stems from the idea that if you post about someone a bunch and then you break up, that’s embarrassing. But why would I even think about that? I’m just going to focus on the present.”

#6842. Designers Say These Are the 10 Best Antiques to Find at Estate Sales

Thrift stores and flea markets are popular spots for finding unusual vintage pieces, but they’re not the only options. Estate sales let you browse antiques in the very homes where they’ve been used and collected over the years. And since many items haven’t passed through a dealer, they may cost less than similar finds at an antique shop.

That combination makes estate sales a favorite among interior designers. They’re a source of characterful pieces for both client projects and personal collections. Krystal Reinhard, founder and principal designer of Old Soul Design Studio, likes being able to see items in the settings where they’ve long belonged. “It makes it easier to spot quality, scale, and patina,” she says.

For Valerie Helgeson, founder and lead designer at Design Directions, the appeal of estate sales is the fast-paced nature of the hunt. “There’s an immediacy to them that I like. You have to make a decision and trust your instincts,” she shares.

Whether you’re after a bargain, an authentic antique, or the fun of finding an unexpected treasure, estate sales are worth exploring. Ahead, interior designers reveal the 10 antiques they always look for—and explain why each belongs on your estate sale wish list.

Paige Loperfido, founder and principal designer of Decor & More Design Studio, is always game to grab hand-painted porcelain, midcentury ceramics, and antique glassware, like ice buckets and goblets.

“These collectibles bring color, pattern, and personality to kitchens and dining rooms,” she says. “Beyond their practical use, they easily transition into art, with vintage plates mounted on walls for a dynamic display, or grouped on open shelves. In dining spaces, glassware and tableware act as statement pieces, highlighting one-of-a-kind treasures that feel timeless, sophisticated, and thoughtfully curated, adding both charm and character to special gatherings.”

Reinhard calls antique candlestick holders “an instant layer,” with their warmth, height, and “a little romance with a surprisingly big impact for a small spend.” She adds that she often styles them as matched pairs on mantels and consoles, or as loose trios at varying heights on dining tables and coffee tables.

Going into estate sales with an open mind, Helgeson typically finds herself buying something she “could have never imagined looking for.” But frequently, one item tops her wish list, and that’s copper kitchenware. She displays these antique pieces in kitchens alongside other estate sale–found items, such as turned-wood bowls, old cutting boards, and bread bowls.

Pottery is a designer darling, since these pieces add texture and even imperfection (in a good way) to spaces, as Reinhard puts it. She tracks down pottery at estate sales, then showcases it on open shelving, sideboards, and tablescapes. She prefers to pair pottery with “something refined,” like brass, stone, or glass, to keep the mix elevated.

Loperfido’s favorite estate sale gems are what she calls “culturally rich pieces” that have been collected by homeowners from around the world. Think: Chinese antiques like foo dogs and ginger jars, and vintage ceramics such as majolica fish candlesticks, figurative pieces, or midcentury glazed pottery.

Oftentimes, she says that these particular pieces “showcase incredible craftsmanship and character,” adding that they’re perfect to layer on surfaces throughout the house, from bookshelves to nightstands to end tables.

At estate sales, Helgeson is scanning the spaces for “furniture with a strong silhouette.” Acting as anchors in rooms, Helgeson believes in accessorizing the rest of these spaces with vintage finds, which she says makes even new pieces appear like collected treasures.

“Vintage rugs bring color, pattern, and age instantly,” Reinhard remarks. She decorates with them “everywhere”: in powder rooms, mudrooms, and under seating areas to anchor the furniture and add depth.

“Original art can feel inaccessible at gallery pricing, but estate sales are one of the best places to find something truly special for a fraction of the cost,” Reinhard points out. She goes for single statement pieces, small collected clusters, or creates a full gallery wall that combines vintage art with modern pieces and personal photos.

Continually on the lookout for leather-bound collections and cocktail books, Reinhard snaps up antique books at estate sales.

“They’re one of the easiest ways to add personality and story—especially art, history, and travel titles that feel timeless and intentional,” she says. “I use them everywhere—stacked on coffee tables, tucked onto consoles, layered into shelves, or used as risers for objects to create height and visual rhythm.”

Loperfido says that these “finely detailed etched artworks” are often overlooked at estate sales, but they can instantly add sophistication and historical texture to any space.

“Small but rich in visual storytelling, they work beautifully against neutral or patterned backgrounds,” she notes.

#6840. Jude Bellingham Stripped to His Skivvies for Calvin Klein’s Latest Campaign

Last week, Calvin Klein dropped a hint of their next underwear campaign star via Instagram. The clip featured a guy gliding his fingers from his boxers up to his chest. Very suggestive. Very sexy. Instagram, obviously, went wild. A teaser of a teaser, you might say.

Now, the New York label has revealed all. The model in question? Jude Bellingham.

Shot by Oliver Hadlee Pearch on a hot Madrid day, the campaign features Bellingham posing against a clean white background—all the better to keep the focus squarely on the England international. In the exclusive photo above (don’t say we don’t spoil you), Bellingham is sporting CK’s Icon Cotton Stretch Boxer Slim.

Bellingham wears the rest of the Icon Cotton Stretch collection (and nothing else) throughout the campaign, which includes the new Stretch Shine Trunk with a shiny Infinity Bond logo waistband, the Boxer Brief, Low Rise Trunk and Hip Brief. In one shot, he’s pictured doing his goal celebration, which means the image may pop into your head the next time he scores on the pitch.

Maybe it’s obvious from the campaign, but the 23-year-old took to the role of model very naturally. He attributes his ease in front of the camera to the underwear.

“I really enjoyed working on this campaign,” Bellingham says via email. “I liked the idea of ‘the icon underneath’—that confidence starts with feeling comfortable in yourself. Calvin Klein is such an iconic brand, so it was great to bring that idea to life in my own way.”

Over the years, CK has linked up with its fair share of footballers. In 2023, the label shot Liverpool captain Virgil Van Dijk. That same year, Tottenham’s Son Heung-Min stripped to his skivvies as well. And at the end of 2025, it was Bellingham’s Real Madrid and England teammate Trent Alexander-Arnold’s turn. Maybe Bellingham went to him for advice—not that it looks like he needed it. Clearly, the guy knows how to nail the shot.

#6838. Hailey Bieber and Zoë Kravitz Are Loving This Low-Maintenance Nail Trend for Fall

Here’s how to recreate the goes-with-anything manicure.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Last night’s CFDA Awards brought all of fashion’s coolest people together. With glitzy designer dresses and a star-studded carpet, the event boasted a guest list with A-listers like Kim Kardashian, Demi Moore, Megan Thee Stallion, and more, at the Museum of Natural History in New York City. But amid the luxe labels and prestigious awards, one beauty trend was the common denominator taking over the carpet: glassy nails.

In a sea of fall nail colors like earthy greens and moody burgundies, glassy manicures offer a welcome break from bolder polishes. Celebrity beauty muses like Hailey Bieber, Zoë Kravitz, and more were spotted rocking the barely-there manicure. And the best part of all? The sheer, glossy finish goes with just about everything, making it an easy look to wear all season (or year) long, no matter what you're wearing.

This sheer manicure is so easy to DIY, and it’s also super low-maintenance. As it starts to grow out, unlike a bright or deep hue, you can barely tell where the new growth begins.

Start by grooming and shaping your nails for a clean base. Trim any loose cuticles, buff away uneven texture, and file them into your desired shape. Whether you like your fingertips short and round, or long and almond, this fresh finish looks good on any length (as showcased by the chic celebrities who have been rocking many versions of the look lately).

Once your canvas is prepped and clean, choose your glass polish. You can lean a little milkier and pinker with a shade like Essie’s Skinny Dip, or opt for a nourishing formula like Manucurist’s Active Bright. Or, go for a completely shiny finish with a clear coat of OPI’s Start to Finish. For a more translucent look, use just one coat of your desired polish, and for more opaque finishes, two layers should do the trick.

#6837. These 7 Storage Solutions for Small Spaces Look Too Good to Hide

Display-worthy baskets, glass-fronted cabinets, and colorful tins can make organization part of your decor.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

From a Brooklyn studio to a narrow Baltimore rowhouse, two decades of small-space living have taught me how to make every square foot count. Add in my Type A Virgo tendencies and an appreciation for good design, and I have strong opinions about storage: Namely, it should be as good-looking as it is hard-working. My first stop when hunting for products that deliver just that is always IKEA. The Swedish retailer has an unmatched selection of storage furniture and decor. Here are seven pieces that offer both function and form.

I have never known the pleasure of a linen closet, but I’ve gotten so many compliments on this metal-and-glass cabinet in my bathroom that I no longer mind. The reeded glass offers an unexpected texture while obscuring what’s inside to reduce visual clutter, and each interior shelf is roomy enough to hold a stack of towels, plus a small bin for toiletries and medicine bottles. I like to use the bamboo UPPDATERA boxes for material contrast; the larger style works great on the lower shelves to tame my mess of serums, makeup, lotions, and the like.

There is no such thing as too many bins or baskets when you live in a small space. They are the unsung organizational heroes, giving you a way to stash all the miscellaneous things that would typically be tucked away in a closet, attic, basement, or even a spare room in a larger home. IKEA sells a huge variety, but the woven styles like this one are particularly lovely to keep on display. I use them on my EKET living room shelves for games, in my bedroom for scarves and bags, and in the kitchen for tea towels and napkins.

When square footage is at a premium, closets tend to get eliminated. A free-standing wardrobe is the solution to creating clothes storage in a small bedroom. This chic new design features sliding doors—a space-saver itself since you won’t need to account for door clearance—adjustable shelves, and two drawers.

If your front door, like mine, opens directly into your living space, you need to fake a foyer as best you can. The key to that is shoe storage—otherwise, guests will be staring at muddy boots the minute they walk into your home. This cabinet hides 14 pairs behind a traditional design, and sticks out from the wall less than a foot. Two drawers make space for keys and sunglasses while four hooks give bags and dog leashes a home.

All kitchens can benefit from more storage, but it’s the small cook spaces where you really need to get creative. A mobile cart offers that utility, and this one features three shelves for anything from spices and baking supplies to serveware or a coffee bar.

Another simple small kitchen storage idea: Keep your dry goods in stackable containers, like the BETTHAJ tins, to maximize your pantry shelf space. They’re far easier to handle than flimsy paper containers or plastic bags, plus, they look great on a countertop or open shelving. They come in five different sizes, from 19 oz. to 3.3 qt., so you can mix and match to suit your needs.

Why choose between extra seating or more storage when you can have both? This sturdy little pouf hides a compartment that I use for an extra throw blanket, but it could also fit a few games, your knitting supplies, or remote controls. The cushioned top is taut enough that it can also function as a side table, making this a small space triple threat.

Head to IKEA to find storage that maximizes space without sacrificing style.

#6835. AO3’s Outage Is Raising Questions About Who Owns the Fics Fans Love So Much

Two to four weeks offline is testing just how much fandom depends on one volunteer-run site.

Archive of Our Own aka AO3 has become the place to spend your time when you’re trying to escape doomscrolling at 1 a.m. and turn to doom-reading instead, as you speed through a fanfiction that would keep you up the rest of the night. But last week, AO3 shocked everyone when it announced it would be down for the next two to four weeks due to a security incident.

For a normal website, this might not be such a big deal. But Archive of Our Own is run by volunteers, so things can’t be fixed as easily. Just as readers yelled out, “Well, what am I supposed to read now?” (A book, maybe?) the community came together to create their own kind of archive of AO3 works, creating folders on Google Docs to share amongst their fellow fans. But there’s a reason as to why this has caused some major issues between the readers who cannot stop consuming these alternative stories and the authors who bring them to life.

For the uninitiated, Archive of Our Own first launched back in 2018 as a site where any and all fanfiction can live. Several other sites made policies to ban certain content—Fanfiction.net has banned some fandoms due to authors’ dislike for fanfiction, Tumblr started to ban explicit content including written works in late 2018, and Wattpad did the same for content that is exclusively “created primarily for sexual arousal”—but AO3 never made rules like that.

For some, this means that there may be content on AO3 that goes against their morals or their ideas of what is okay to consume in media. But, for Archive of Our Own’s founders, it’s elemental to making sure that fandom continues to grow. Fics are tagged based on original IP, relationships, tropes, and content warnings like “Dead Dove: Do Not Eat” that try to warn users that what they might be reading might not be for them.

All this means that the site has grown significantly, becoming a safe space for both creatives and readers to access stories that normally would not be available anywhere else. And it also means that if anything were to go awry, it can come with some serious consequences.

For authors, the Google Drive initiative is seen as their work being reposted or repurposed for something that it was not initially intended for: private consumption. Fans have always been able to download their favorite fanfics from AO3, but only so they can read them offline. It was never meant to be shared around other than the platforms where the author has uploaded the stories themself.

For some readers, however, it’s all about continuing the legacy of AO3 in the time that it’s down.

Authors, however, have called out the behavior: They never gave permission for the work to be uploaded anywhere else and they are concerned with Google Drive’s use of AI, which may scan their work for other uses.

“Sorry folks but I don’t give permission for my Ao3 fics to be shared on a Google Drive or uploaded to literally any other site,” one author wrote on Threads. “I know this is just me shouting into the void but it sure as hell would be nice if people didn’t give AI scrapers easy access to people’s writing.”

Unlike Google and the other sites like Wattpad and Tumblr, AO3 does not have to adhere to certain content guidelines as they are not-for-profit and do not run any ads on their site. AO3 is run by volunteers (hence the longer-than-normal downtime after this security incident) and through donations by paid members. As they are not making a profit on sex content or copyrighted intellectual properties, the site is able to protect itself under fair use.

For some fans, it feels like they’re just sharing the wealth in a time of uncertainty. But there’s no denying that this entire situation has become a bit of a slippery slope, both ethically and legally. Is community more important than that? That feels like an argument that we’re all constantly questioning nowadays even outside of fandom.

One thing is for sure: There’s no denying the impact that AO3 has had on fandom and its ability to be able to create works openly and without worry no matter the content. With it, the community has been able to grow safely and produce a number of works that have even gone on to live other lives through other means. Without it, there’s a chance that our time creating original stories with existing characters might be at risk yet again. The question is: How much are fans willing to bet for two to four weeks of comfort? It looks like they’re going all in, and it’ll be interesting to see who is holding the cards at the end.
