#6831. Why El Clasico Is the Best Grudge Match in All of Sports

Last Saturday, before the biggest grudge match in international soccer, Spanish police officers decked out in riot gear surrounded my Barcelona hotel. Blue lights bounced from the sun-stained stone of the alley as kids scrambled away in the distance. In the skirmish, one had lost a single ASICS sneaker that sat idly in the road until, sometime between night and the light of day, someone snatched it up for themselves. You often hear of the way heated football rivalries and cup finals bring out a kind of mob mentality among fans. The match, however, wasn’t set to kick off for another 24 hours.

Three days prior I’d landed in Barcelona to attend FC Barcelona versus Real Madrid, better known as El Clásico, for which Tyler the Creator’s GOLF brand had partnered with Spotify on Barcelona’s gameday kits. If you care about football in the slightest you know about El Clásico, though it’s worth knowing about even if you don’t.

Since 1902—the year of Real Madrid’s founding, and three years after that of FC Barcelona—the two biggest football clubs in Spain have clashed in a rivalry match dubbed El Clásico (the face-off now happens twice a season, plus various tournament play that varies in schedule year to year). Before last week’s installment, the match had been played 264 times over 122 years. In most high-stakes football rivalries, one club holds a historical edge: Arsenal has a handy 20 to 30 wins over Tottenham Hotspur in the North London Derby, depending on when you start keeping track, while Manchester United edges out Liverpool in total wins. But heading into the first El Clásico of the 2026-27 La Liga season, Barcelona and Real Madrid stood deadlocked with 106 wins each.

American sports rivalries are often the result of proximity, with most pitting divisional neighbors and crosstown rivals against one another, with the exception of teams that share a bitter history of championship bouts (like the Lakers and the Celtics). But if we’re being honest, not even Yankees-Red Sox holds a candle to El Clásico. Apart from both competing in the upper echelon of La Liga’s clubs, FC Barcelona and Real Madrid’s rivalry has stewed in over a century of political turmoil between the Catalan capital and the city that houses the Spanish throne. During the 20th century as Spain looked to enforce a uniform identity across the nation, Catalonia sought to maintain its independence from the crown and, crucially, its right to speak Catalan rather than Spanish. Francisco Franco’s dictatorship brought more enmity, as Madrid became the proxy club for a centralized Spain while Barcelona came to represent Catalonia’s Republican Left. During the Franco years, when both the language and the flag of Catalan were banned, Barcelona’s Camp Nou stadium became a safe haven for the free expression of Catalan identity. Meanwhile, Franco’s foreign minister Fernando Maria de Castiella declared Real Madrid “the best embassy we’ve ever had.” The wounds of that era have hardly subsided: while the war may be over, as recently as 2017 the Catalan people voted on whether or not they’d remain a part of Spain (the vote was declared illegal by the Spanish government, and there was police intervention at polling stations).

Today, the clubs are recognized as polar opposites in every sense, from politics to style of play: Real Madrid stands for conservatism and a unified Spain, while Barcelona remains the club of the rebellious Left. Madrid takes an Evil Empire approach to roster construction, buying up the most exciting free agents on the market for record-breaking fees. (This century alone they’ve rostered Cristiano Ronaldo, David Beckham, and Zinedine Zidane, and the club currently boasts a striker line that includes Jude Bellingham, Kylian Mbappé, and Vinicius Jr.) Barcelona, on the other hand, has the most acclaimed and successful youth academy in all of football. The school, La Masia, has promoted a singular theory of football for decades, prioritizing position play and a dominant possession game perfected by FC Barcelona veterans like Lionel Messi, Xavi, Pep Guardiola, and Andrés Iniesta. When Spain won the World Cup this summer, they did so with eight former students of La Masia on the pitch (one of whom, Marc Cucarella, currently plays for Real Madrid).

To this day, the rivalry is characterized by an unmistakable hostility. In my four days in Barcelona, I didn’t see one person wearing Real Madrid’s crest, though the souvenir shops lining La Rambla were well-stocked with the club’s bootleg gear. At one point, I walked past a wall scrawled with graffiti: “SPANISH GO HOME.” When we finally got to the match, most Madrid fans were sequestered to a supporter section in the upper deck surrounded by protective plexiglass.

“This is the biggest match in club football,” explained Joel Jaggar, a British transplant who’s lived in Barcelona for a decade. Jaggar runs Real Vintage Football, a rare jersey boutique in El Raval (just outside of the city’s Gothic Quarter) that was unsurprisingly overwhelmed with visitors all weekend. He’d stocked up on at least a hundred Messi jerseys, all of which were gone by the time they closed on Friday. “A lot of people come to Barcelona specifically for this game,” he shared. “Big fans, sure, but also people who want to experience the game for the sake of the experience.” Globally, he argued, the word “Barcelona” is as closely associated with the club as it is the city. “You can’t say the same for Madrid,” he quipped.

Jaggar and I met the morning after a swell of student-organized protests regarding the city’s cost-of-living crisis boiled over into vandalism and looting near the neighborhood that houses his store. There was even suspicion El Clásico would have to be postponed or cancelled altogether. Come morning though, those anxieties had faded–as is so often the case, football continued uninterrupted.

Even with capacity capped at around 62,000 during ongoing renovations, Camp Nou is something to behold. Ahead of kickoff, supporters lifted color-coordinated pieces of poster nestled into each seat,forming a massive, stadium-wide tifo in the colors of the club and, in our section, the Catalonian flag. Fireworks exploded, and El Clásico began.

But much of the match’s first half was mired in tension, as Barcelona dominated possession but failed to score. The crowd finally began to pop when Madrid nabbed their first goal, then properly exploded when Barca tied it up in the second half. When Mbappé scored his second goal of the evening at ‘87 minutes after a beautiful assist from Bellingham, he vaulted over to the gate of the Barcelona supporters’ section, taunting them as trash thrown from the crowd rained over him. Madrid held on, gaining the tiniest of advantages in the El Clásico win count: 107 to their rivals’ 106.

As we walked out of Camp Nou, which will hold a whopping 105,000 spectators when its renovation is complete, I thought about the sort of sporting events that tend to grace bucket lists: Super Bowls and World Cup Finals and World Series games. So often the point is the spectacle of a season or tournament ending—the big trophy, the championship ring, the confetti and fireworks. El Clásico is different. There’s no trophy at the end, no ultimate resolution. Like the ideological divide for which it’s become a proxy, El Clásico is in a constant state of ebb and flow. Each time Barcelona and Real Madrid take the pitch, there’s no decisive victory so much as another brick laid in the foundation of football’s greatest rivalry. These clubs have come to blows 265 times. But as long as there are football stadiums in Barcelona and Madrid, El Clásico will endure as a conduit for the enduring rancor between the country's two biggest cities.

#6828. What El Niño Means for Your Lawn and Garden This Year—and How to Protect Your Plants

Most regions will experience different impacts from this phenomenon.

You may have heard that this year’s El Niño is likely going to have a significant impact on weather in the United States.

“This naturally occurring phenomenon, known as El Niño Southern Oscillation or (ENSO), influences climate in the U.S. and other parts of the world,” says Pam Knox, Ph.D., agricultural climatologist at the University of Georgia. “Some indications say that this could be one of the strongest El Niños on record.”

This pattern occurs roughly every two to seven years, though not on a regular schedule. “El Niño starts in the tropical Pacific Ocean. Changes in ocean temperatures can shift weather patterns around the world,” says Eric Hunt, Ph.D., agricultural meteorologist for the University of Nebraska Extension Service. “This year we’re seeing record warmth, about 10 to 12 degrees above average, in water along the equator. We’ve never seen temperatures this warm in parts of the Pacific.”

Although you’ve probably heard of it referred to as a “Super El Niño,” that’s not a real meteorological term. “Predictions are broken down into categories,” says Madelynn Wuestenberg, agricultural climatology extension specialist for Iowa State University Extension and Outreach. “So, sea surface temperatures .5 degrees above historical averages indicate a weak El Niño, for example. Anything greater than 2 degrees above average is categorized as a very strong El Niño.”

Interestingly, the name of this phenomenon dates back a few centuries to when South American fishermen noticed warmer-than-usual Pacific Ocean waters and decreases in fish catch in December. This led to nicknaming the occurrence “El Niño,” Spanish for “little boy,” because it typically occurred around Christmas.

Here’s what else you should know about El Niño and its potential impact on your lawn and garden.

Experts are clear that while a very strong El Niño can affect temperature and precipitation patterns, nothing is 100 percent guaranteed. “We can say certain conditions are more probable, but we can’t say exactly what will happen,” says Hunt.

Typically, El Nino brings drier, warmer air to the northern parts of the U.S., with fewer clouds and less precipitation. Meanwhile, the Gulf Coast and South may experience wetter, cooler, stormier, and more overcast conditions, says Knox.

A strong El Niño also shifts the track of winter storms. By increasing the total amount of heat and water vapor in the Earth’s atmosphere, and by shifting global storm tracks, El Niño may contribute to significant regional drought and flood events in places such as the Southeast and West, says Hunt.

It’s important to remember that a very strong El Niño doesn’t mean impacts will be more extreme—only more likely, says Knox. This also will vary by region. But being aware of potential risks to your region may help you prepare.

Here’s what the experts say may be issues due to El Niño this winter.

All of these tips are useful for any growing season, but they may be particularly important this year with a very strong El Niño likely to impact weather across the U.S.

Long-range forecasts can help you plan ahead for freezes and other significant weather events, says Wuestenberg. Check eight- to 14-day hazards here, and three- to seven-day hazards here.

Mulch will protect roots from freeze/thaw cycles. This will also conserve moisture and may prevent frost heave that occurs when new or shallowly rooted plants are pushed up out of the ground by temperature fluctuations, says Wuestenberg.

Heavy rains may be problematic if you have areas in your yard where runoff is an issue, says Hunt. That means it’s also a good idea to ensure gutters are clear so water is carried away from the foundation of your house.

Look for branches that could be potential issues, such as any that are overhanging your house, driveway, or walkways.

“It’s recommended to have a certified arborist inspect your trees every two to three years for health reasons because trees are such a significant investment in your landscape,” says Blake Watkins, master arborist and director of plant healthcare at Monster Tree Service. “Arborists also can evaluate a tree’s structure for potential safety issues.”

Many companies offer a free consultation or charge a minimal fee for an evaluation; find a certified arborist here.

Evergreens such as boxwood and arborvitae keep their leaves or needles all winter, which means they keep losing moisture, too. Keep watering them until the ground freezes, especially newly planted trees or if you have no rain, says Wuestenberg.

“We still need to do the same things we normally do to keep our lawns healthy,” says Clint Waltz, Ph.D., turfgrass researcher and extension agent at the University of Georgia. “This means following good agronomics, including irrigating and mowing properly, and applying product as needed to help your lawn be better prepared for spring. It’s also helpful to be aware of what the potential impacts on turf could be, such as a wetter-than-normal winter in the Southeast.”

While it’s too late to apply nitrogen to your warm-season grasses, you can add potassium to help with rooting, and phosphorus, if a soil test indicates it is needed. For cool-season grasses, put down an application of nitrogen, keep irrigating if needed, and consider overseeding and applying a fungicide, if needed, says Waltz.

Don’t let fallen leaves sit on your grass all winter. A thick coating of leaves prevents turf from photosynthesizing and could impact turf health in the long run. But don’t cart leaves off to the landfill; compost them or grind them up and use for mulch, says Waltz.

Inspect plants regularly, especially when the growing season kicks off, so you can treat sooner, rather than later. Keep an eye out for signs of pests that overwintered or diseases that are affecting ornamental or edible plants, says Knox.

Drier-than-normal conditions in some regions may mean you’ll need to water young plants more often after planting. In other areas, it may be too wet to plant at the usual time. You may want to use a cold frame or to wait longer than usual to plant if soil temperatures are cooler than normal, says Knox.

“The number one thing to do is to pay attention to forecasts. But take social media with a grain of salt. Many of these dire predictions are made by people who are not trained climatologists,” says Knox. “It’s really about watching how conditions evolve and thinking about how they may impact your garden.”

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6826. Amazon’s Black Friday AirPods Deals Dropped Early

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Shop surprise discounts on the AirPods Pro 3 and AirPods Max 2 long before Thanksgiving.

While the Black Fridays of yore involved a sea of people rushing into a big-box store to score a new TV or gaming console at a record-low price, the biggest shopping holiday of the year looks a bit different these days. Now, the madness is largely confined to the internet, and the sales happen all month long, not in the wee hours when you’re fighting off a turkey-induced nap.

Case in point: Amazon’s current slate of Apple deals let you save an impressive amount on AirPods long before you’ve even finalized your Thanksgiving plans. Every time Apple gadgets, namely AirPods, go on deep discount, they tend to sell out quickly. It’s just like those Black Friday mad dashes, just from the comfort of your own couch.

Right now, both the AirPods Pro 3 and the AirPods Max 2 are on sale, although we suspect that these deals might deepen soon. If you’re looking for the cheapest possible pair, the brand-new AirPods 5 are just $129—a great deal at full price—but we predict they’ll go on sale for the first time in the coming weeks.

We always recommend buying them when they drop under $200, but they’ve retailed for as low as $169 during other sales, so there’s the chance this price could drop more come Black Friday.

Should you prefer over-ear headphones, the AirPods Max 2 are $100 off in select colorways as part of Amazon’s pre-Black Friday deals. Back when the first generation of these dropped in 2022, editor-at-large Dave Holmes called them “Apple’s best product in years.” Four years later, Apple’s second stab has only improved the superb noise cancellation and sound quality we already loved, and added in a slate of beloved features from the AirPods Pro (think Live Translation, Conversation Awareness, and Personalized Volume), without sacrificing the sleek build.

Since the second generation was released in March 2026, the lowest we’ve seen them go is $398. Ahead of Black Friday, a $50 price cut is nothing to scoff at, especially in an age where our favorite tech has fallen prey to price increases, but you don’t need to rush either. We’ll be keeping a close watch on these.

And while the just-released AirPods 5 aren’t on sale yet, they’re still far and away the cheapest model you can get if budget is top of mind—even if you splurge on the version with a wireless charging case.

Unlike Apple’s older generations of entry-level earbuds, these have active noise cancellation, which was previously only available in premium models. They also have Live Translation and improved dust-, sweat-, and water-resistance, making them earbuds with a premium feel—sans the price tag. We’re anticipating that these will go on sale for the first time on Amazon just in time for Black Friday.

Our rule of thumb when it comes to shopping Apple tech for Black Friday? If there’s something you’ve been eyeing at a price you can stomach, it’s worth adding to your cart. When AirPods go on deep discount, they tend to sell out quickly, and then you’ll be stuck with your tired old earbuds until next year.

#6825. F1 Academy Driver Emma Felbermayr and Audi Reveal the Ultimate Way to Experience Race Weekend in Austin

In their first-ever weekend at the United States Grand Prix, the latest F1 team to join the grid celebrated in style.

Before Miami or Las Vegas, the United States Grand Prix in Austin, Texas, was the only race in the U.S. for Formula 1. And it has quite the history! The iconic Circuit of the Americas track was purposefully built for the sport and has become a quick favorite amongst drivers and fans alike thanks to its stunning turns that were inspired by the likes of the Silverstone Circuit in the United Kingdom and Bahrain’s International Circuit and spacing that allows for fun overturns and racing to happen at all parts of the circuit. (Try overtaking during a street race, we dare you!) But as a person who has experienced several races in her lifetime, the vibes of Austin stood out to me as the iconic venue brought the sport back to the Lone Star State once again for another incredible race that sets itself apart by being clear that it’s made by the fans and for the fans. And the mood was only elevated by the introduction of some brand-new teams in the circuit including the Audi Revolut F1 Team.

In a surprise twist, this year they were also joined by F1 Academy after a schedule change moved one of their planned three-race weekends over to Austin. Which also meant that for the first time, second-year driver Emma Felbermayr would be experiencing the track for herself. And as someone who was heading to the United States Grand Prix weekend for the first time alongside Audi, it felt like fate that we were going to have this new adventure together!

To get the epic Audi experience, the team invited us out to the 2026 United States Grand Prix to see race weekend up close. And in true Texas fashion, we got to see firsthand the real meaning of Southern hospitality. Get ready to find out how Audi is not only bringing a new kind of venture to Austin, but also how Emma got ready for an unexpected weekend in the Lone Star State.

(P.S. The writer received free stays, meals, and experiences in order to write this story.)

I was determined to finally get to witness an entire full race weekend for the first time (third time is the charm, after all), so I traveled out the day before so I wouldn’t have to miss a single second. Ironically, this has also been part of Emma’s plan for success this year.

“This year I took myself a bit more time to see the cities. Last year I was still in school. I always arrived the day before in the evening and went back on Sunday,” she revealed to Cosmopolitan after her final race of the weekend. “But this year, I arrive one day earlier before media day starts to do things you have to do in that specific city.”

Emma used the extra time to her advantage, saying that she went to the city to “try cowboy boots, hats, and all that stuff. But also the most important is getting used to the time zone.”

In a special surprise, Audi allowed me to bring a +1 and so I brought my dad out for his first official race. We hadn’t even made it to the track yet when he let out his first, “Oh my god,” as we were greeted by an Audi representative and were shuffled into a brand-new 2027 Audi Q9, our vehicle for the weekend. Our driver, Cody, let us know that the vehicle only had 18 miles on it before he picked it up, meaning we were truly one of the first to step into it.

Unfortunately, since we got in a bit late, we couldn’t really follow in Emma’s footsteps and take in the sights. Instead, our only stop after our flight was our hotel, the newly opened 1 Hotel Austin. The hotel has a strong link to Audi (more on that later) and we automatically felt right at home as we walked in and immediately headed up to our room.

Room service was absolutely necessary and I totally recommend their wagyu burger which also came with some perfectly cooked fries. After a quick shower, we settled in for the night as we got ready for an incredibly fun-filled weekend.

Practice day meant a more chill day at COTA and a later start than the following few days, so I was able to kick things off with a massage at the Bamford Wellness Spa in 1 Hotel Austin. Not only is there a sauna that I wish I had more time to enjoy, but the staff there were one in a million and made me feel so welcomed and relaxed. The deep tissue massage was a welcome reset after a flight and it got me so energized for an exciting first day.

Unlike in Formula 1, where drivers get three official practices before the qualifying session, Formula 1 Academy drivers already come into day one ready to participate in their first of three races of the weekend. While Emma notes that simulator training does help them be fully prepared for packed weekends like this one, nothing relates to the real thing.

“The track time you get isn't a lot. In karting you don't really learn that you have to prepare yourself that much for a weekend, because you've got five test sessions before every weekend. But here you only have one, so that's the only session you can try something on track and just go through different situations before a weekend,” Emma said.

And so, it was important for us to get the lay of the land. As my dad and I got to the track in the middle of the day and it was officially time to take everything in. We did a hot girl walk around the track going from Turn 1—where he would be taking in the race all weekend—before walking backwards the track’s direction until we hit turn 16. It allowed us to get a pretty good idea of what the track was like and also check out some of the incredible spots where other folks who were attending were able to see the races.

It was a shorter day at COTA, but for a good reason. We ran back to the hotel after doing some very important shopping at the F1 merch shops and got in just in time for our special dinner experience, “Driven by the Journey,” a special partnership between Audi, 1 Hotels, and SingleThread—a three-star Michelin restaurant located in Sonoma County in California.

The dinner brought the iconic SingleThread Farms experience to Austin with a special five-course menu created by owner-chef Kyle Connaughton that featured fresh produce picked from the farm and picked out by owner-farmer Katina Connaughton. Each course also came with a wine pairing and it was truly one of the most incredible dining journeys I’ve ever had the pleasure to try. And the views from the hotel’s 14th floor were absolutely gorgeous, especially as we caught the Austin sunset.

As the sun went down, we went straight up to the rooftop to Watr—1 Hotel’s Japanese-inspired restaurant—where the night was just getting started for the ultimate nightcap.

It was a very full day on the track, so we headed out first thing in the morning in order to catch every single minute. Somehow, Audi worked their magic and gave my dad the greatest gifts you can give an F1 fan: a Paddock Club pass. Some lucky fans are able to win the chance to go into the Paddock Club with a team, while a few tickets are also sold to the public, but usually it’s access that’s reserved for those who are close to the team like sponsors or friends and family.

After catching up with the rest of the group, we walked over to the pit lane to get a private tour of the Audi F1 garage. Not only did we get to see the cars up close, but we also got to see the incredible mechanics and engineers get ready for qualifying day.

We also caught Emma’s Q&A back in the Paddock Club, where she shared her experience so far in Austin and her hope for the rest of the weekend as she prepared for her second race.

“The track is so wide, so big, and with our cars being that small compared to F1 cars, it it's even bigger. So I'm enjoying it a lot,” she revealed.

Soon after, we were heading out to the track for the ultimate experience: a Pirelli Hot Lap. Since I was the first one up for our session, I ran straight into the special Audi RS 5 where Audi F1 ambassador and current F1 Academy mentor Carrie Schreiner took me for the ultimate ride around the track that went up to 150 miles per hour. Just when I thought that walking around COTA was the best experience, there’s nothing like actually getting driven around the track.

Then came the day’s biggest main events: the F1 qualifying race as well as F1 Academy’s Reverse Grid Race. Grabbing one of the team radios only available in the Paddock Club and in the garage, we were able to listen to the team’s communications that made for a fun and different experience compared to watching at home.

For dinner, we headed out into the town and checked out Comedor, a spectacular Mexican restaurant located in downtown Austin where we grabbed some guacamole topped with chapulines—or Mexican crickets—and some Crab Luis Totopos that I will be dreaming about for years.

Then it was back to 1 Hotel for a much-needed rest before the final day. Emma notes that relaxing and getting a good night’s sleep before a race day is always key.

“I like to get my nine hours every day. I just like getting into bed early and maybe watch something on Netflix, not doing so much,” she said. What’s she watching right now? Outer Banks and some reality TV, along with Gossip Girl as she prepares for her first trip to NYC with Formula 1 Academy. But no time for an all-nighter! There’s still race day to look forward to!

It was finally race day and things couldn’t be more heated as both Formula 1 and Formula 1 Academy geared up for another wild day.

After grabbing a smoothie at Neighbors Cafe at 1 Hotel, my dad and I got one final look around the track.

Bonus: There’s one new experience at the United States Grand Prix that blew my mind during race day: the Guest House at Turn 20. In partnership with Austin’s Guest House restaurant, the special private hospitality space gives the perfect view of the final turn into the pit lane and includes a gourmet menu, fun performances throughout the day, and a bar with specialty drinks. Believe me, if you want to get as close to the Paddock Club experience, this is it!

We got to the track just in time to see Emma in her final race of the weekend which left her third in the overall Formula 1 Academy standings. There’s only one more race left in the F1A calendar: Las Vegas.

“It's going to be so much colder, so it's going to be different. Since I've raced there already last year, that's at least an advantage for myself compared to this weekend,” she added. “I think I just can focus a bit more on the driving through practice than learning the track.”

Emma is looking forward to resetting herself before then, something she says is key to further success.

“Either after a bad or a good weekend, you need to reset. If it was a good weekend, it doesn't mean the next one is going to be good as well. But if it's a bad one, there is a chance to come back stronger.”

Part of that includes hanging out with friends and family while she’s back home.

“I love seeing my friends. Since we are traveling so much, I don't get to see them really often. But when I do, it's even more nice,” Emma revealed.

She hasn’t revealed her plans for next season, especially as she finishes up her two-year limit in Formula 1 Academy.

“I'd say it's a bit hard to say now. I don't have proper plans for next year, so we are a bit on seeing the options,” she noted.

But, above all, she’s keeping one thing in mind: her love of racing.

“It was always about having fun, enjoying it, and that's also why I do it. I've always done what I really enjoyed doing and what I felt was the right thing to do.”

And so, with one final look at COTA and a last “yee-haw,” we officially said goodbye to the United States Grand Prix and Audi’s first rodeo in Austin. If this is what their first go-around is like, we can’t wait to witness what they have coming up next!

#6824. 31 Best Gifts for Mom in 2026, According to GQ Editors

There's an art to giving a good gift to someone who's been a mother to you, whether that's your actual mom, grandmother, or the person who's always looked out for you. And it has precious little to do with how much you spend. They say what goes around comes around, and when the occasion comes to buy her something special, there's not better time to return a little of the favor. To that end, we've rounded up the best gifts for mom, from modest indulgences to proper splurges, all chosen with an eye toward good taste, a little surprise, and the small triumph of getting it just right.

Still stumped? We'd suggest perusing the rest of our gift guides and signing up for the GQ Recommends newsletter for more gift ideas worth your money.

I have an AYR sweater in this very fabric, and I've been wearing the hell out of it lately. It's extraordinarily soft, with a bouncy, bouclé-like texture that you really have to feel to appreciate. The High has all that going for it, plus a cropped fit and a prim, high-set collar.

We're firm believers that a good tuxedo is one you'd wear any old day. Which is why we're digging this casual cotton tuxedo shirt from Gap's collaboration with Victoria Beckham. It's awfully chic, and a reason for her to get "dressed up" with nowhere special to go.

Breda's Jane Time Charm is a clever little thing. Made from gold-plated stainless steel, the miniature timepiece can be worn as a necklace or clipped to a keychain, but I think it makes for a pretty decoration on a lapel.

Timex's Cavatina Mini looks like something you'd stumble upon at an estate sale, with its petite 17mm case and old-school script logo. The design nods to the brand's '60s-era women's watches, though this one's fresh out of the box.

Thistles is an IYKYK eyewear label whose fashionable milieu stretches from Em Rata to Kim Gordon and any mom worth her vintage Prada. There are plenty of excellent frames to choose from, but the square-shaped Suede is a safe bet.

Coach has a bona fide can't-miss gift on its hands with the Brooklyn, a slouchy, do-it-all carryall. It comes in a range of sizes, but we're partial to the 34, a generously proportioned everyday bag with room for just about everything.

Cashmere is the safest bet you can make at Quince, so it's nice to see the brand putting it to work as the lining of these leather gloves. And at that price, there isn't much to complain about.

If she's made it this far in life without a pair of UGGs, the Zora ballet flat might be the one that finally wins her over. And if she's already living that UGG life, well, she's gonna need these.

A fresher, more spirited take on the original Coco, Coco Mademoiselle pairs sparkling orange and jasmine with rose and patchouli. For a mom with a taste for the classics, but an appreciation for modern flair, this one'll hit.

We recommended Therabody's Depuffing Wand last year, and we're running it back because it's just so dang good at what it does. Hot and cold treatments in one wonderfully unfussy little device make it a welcome addition to her skincare routine.

I like giving silk pillowcases because they're a beauty treatment and a bedroom upgrade in one. Clementine's smooth silk helps reduce friction against hair, minimizing tangles and breakage, while leaving fewer sleep creases.

Dyson's new travel-size Supersonic brings the brand's high-powered drying technology to a more portable package. It's compact enough to tuck into a carry-on without sacrificing the elite airflow of the original, and it's compatible with all the same attachments.

Laneige's Lip Sleeping Mask is a cult favorite, and chances are she already knows it, maybe even swears by it. But the new gingerbread flavor for the season? A stroke of seasonal madness, and a nice little stocking stuffer to boot.

Aesop is about as close to a sure thing as gifting gets. Its new Solias Hand Serum has a light, floral, citrusy, and herbaceous aroma, with a nourishing formula to help keep Mom's hands in fighting shape through winter.

No. 04 Bois de Balincourt has long drawn comparisons to Le Labo's Santal 33, only at a considerably friendlier price and without the burdern of its own ubiquity. Warm sandalwood and cedar, with earthy vetiver and a little spice—a perfect year-round candle.

I'm a coffee drinker through and through, but I'd consider switching allegiances to tea if it meant getting to use this teapot from Balmuda. Just looking at the thing feels therapeutic.

For farmers' market queens, this tote really does tick all the boxes: heavy-duty cotton canvas, six exterior pockets, a clever strap for securing, say, a bouquet of dahlihas, and a structured design that stands upright on its own.

This insulated Dutch oven tote from Staub and Clare V. is just the thing for the potluck monarch arriving with a cassoulet in tow. A highly specific gifting proposition, admittedly, but an excellent one nonetheless.

Inspired by the pleated designs of Issey Miyake, Wooj's Kinoko lamp has a pretty mushroom silhouette and a softly ribbed shade. A charming little thing for that neglected corner that could use some illumination.

There are plenty of newfangled brands making cute, colorful homewares these days, but long live Marimekko. These towels caught my eye for their classic brick pattern, rendered in a perfect shade of red, with all the pedigree of Finnish design behind them.

Apple's new entry-level laptop might be marketed to students, but it's also a very good laptop for anyone who just needs to do regular laptop things. Browsing, emailing, streaming, the occasional spreadsheet—the Neo handles the everyday stuff without demanding a small fortune for the privilege of owning a fruit-embossed gadget.

If you really want to round out the gift, throw in this laptop sleeve from Cuyana. Made from Italian leather, it's chic enough to be carried around on its own, which is more than you can say for most laptop accessories.

Sony's XM6 headphones are already among the best noise-canceling cans you can buy, but now they come in this very, very good matte olive gray colorway. As if we needed another reason to recommend them.

Brooklyn-based designer Virginia Sin made a name for herself with porcelain versions of disposable paper plates, and her talent for making everyday objects feel a little more special extends beyond the dinner table. Case in point: this little number, which would look mighty fine on mom's desk.

If mom's recently been promoted to grandmother, I know you've been thinking about getting her a digital picture frame, and yes, you absolutely should. But not just any digital picture frame: the Aura is the one to beat, and only the best will do when it comes to showing off the new bundle.

Twelve South's Valet gives her phone a proper place to recharge, with a handy catchall for keys, jewelry, and whatever else tends to accumulate around the house.

Prettier humidifiers? There aren't many. Canopy's 2.0 is a welcome antidote to the moisture-sapping misery of winter, and will look great wherever she puts it.

And just like that, aesthetically pleasing pill organizers are a thing. Fine by us. Where else is she supposed to stash her daily rotation of vitamins, supplements, and what have you?

Never underestimate the restorative powers of a little targeted heat. Ostrichpillow's new Embrace is a clever upgrade to the humble heating pad, with a wearable design, three heat settings, and up to three hours of cordless warmth.

Again, never underestimate the restorative powers of a little targeted heat. Or, in this case, heat from head to toe. HigherDose's sauna blanket is easy to set up and use, and delivers one hell of a sweat session without requiring a trip to an actual hot box.

Some peace and quiet might be the greatest gift of all. Loop's Switch 2 earplugs offer three levels of noise reduction, adjustable with a simple dial, for everything from quick naps to concerts to moments when she'd like you, specifically, to pipe down.

For the mom who likes to get her miles in, these open-ear headphones let her soundtrack a morning run without losing track of the world around her. They're lightweight, stay put even when she's picking up the pace, and pack up to 12 hours of battery life.

#6822. The Tragic Story Behind La Bola Negra, One of the Year’s Best Films

Based on an unfinished play by Federico Garcia Lorca, the movie is already garnering Oscar buzz

It is rare to come across uncompromisingly queer work from LGBTQ+ artists from a century ago and beyond. Many writers did not get to see their work published in their own lifetime. E.M. Forster’s groundbreaking novel Maurice, which he began writing in 1913 and featured a rare-for-the-time happy ending, was not published until a year after his death, in 1971; Emily Dickinson’s romantic stanzas, which many critics believe were dedicated to her sister-in-law, Susan Gilbert Dickinson, weren’t even discovered until long after her passing. But even those queer artists who did see their work out in the world didn’t manage so easily. James Baldwin was told by his agent to burn the manuscript for Giovanni’s Room. Jane Rule was rejected more than 20 times in her efforts to find a publisher for Desert of the Heart.

One of this year’s most critically acclaimed films, La Bola Negra (“the black ball” in English), is based on an unfinished play of the same name written by the Spanish poet and playwright Federico Garcia Lorca in 1936. In the four pages of the story that remain, a wealthy young man is trying to gain entrance to an exclusive casino in Granada. In order to determine his admittance, the members turn to a voting system where they choose to roll a white ball in favor or a black ball in opposition. After they vote, he is handed a black ball; later, when his father asks him why he thinks he was rejected, the boy tells him that it’s because he is gay.

Tragically, Lorca never got the chance to finish what may have been a groundbreaking story. (He was shot dead by nationalist forces after being imprisoned during the beginning of the Spanish Civil War, and his remains have never been found.) But he had detailed his ideas for the drama in a letter to fellow playwright Cipriano Rivas Cherif.

That’s where Javier Calvo and Javier Ambrossi, the directing duo endearingly referred to as “Los Javis,” comes in. Their movie rewrites a bit of history, making it so that Lorca (also a character in the film) manages to complete most of his play, save for the last few pages. The film jumps between three different years—1932, 1937, and 2017—to explore the lives of different gay men across a century who are all loosely connected by the writer’s work. (The cast includes stars like Élite’s Miguel Bernardeau, folk-pop singer Guitarricadelafuente, and Oscar nominee Glenn Close.)

“We don’t have that many movies or books about the history that connects us as a community, the queer community,” co-director Ambrossi told NPR earlier this week. “That’s why we wanted to make it big. We wanted to make it epic, and we wanted to make it bold.”

To try to piece together the story, the directors combed through old letters and archives to try to imagine how Lorca might have ended it. “We were like detectives,” co-director Calvo noted. “It was fun to discover that he would like to talk about his projects in parties.” And these pieces are precisely how the story of La Bola Negra came together (but don’t worry, no spoilers here).

Now, Los Javis are bringing light to this untold story in an effort to preserve queer history and maintain the legacy of someone who couldn’t so freely tell his truth.

“We are all connected by this black ball that we all share, that we pass to each other, every generation—a black ball that is silence, pain, shame, but also it’s a spark of life and desire and love and joy,” Calvo said. “I feel like [this project] made me feel proud of my community and of the brave people that fought before me.”

You can watch La Bola Negra in theaters now. It arrives to Netflix on December 2.

The Haley Stevens endorsement fiasco is just the tip of the iceberg.

It’s nice to see that the World's Oldest Democracy is approaching a semi-historic midterm election with its customary dignity.

Not to further harsh the national mellow further, but the real knife fight will occur between November 4 and January 3, when the new Congress is supposed to be sworn in. Mo liked to recall the brief but pithy concession speech of a defeated candidate.

#6820. 13 Things You Should Toss ASAP If You’ve Lived in Your Home for More Than 5 Years

You'll feel a wave of relief once you do it.

It may seem like you just moved into your new home or apartment yesterday. But in a flash, five years have passed and you’re out of storage and feeling overwhelmed. Most of us know it doesn’t take long for our belongings to pile up.

“By year five, the ‘endless storage’ honeymoon is over,” says Ann Lightfoot, cofounder, DDH Home Organizing & Move Management. “Your stuff has multiplied, your square footage hasn’t, and you now know how you actually live in your space.”

But now is the ideal time for a thoughtful edit. “Taking stock after five years gives us a chance to see what's actually taking up space, what we're still using, and what we've simply gotten used to storing,” says Laura Kinsella, founder of Urban OrgaNYze. “Our careers, families, routines, and interests can change dramatically, but the things we've accumulated don't automatically change with us.”

Decluttering also enhances good design. “To me, a beautifully designed home isn't about how much you can fit into it. It's about being intentional with what you choose to keep,” says Kerrie Kelly, NKBA, FASID, CEO and creative director of Kerrie Kelly Studio, and NKBA 2026 National Board Chair. “Editing isn't about deprivation; it's about making room for what you actually love and use.”

If you don’t know where to begin, start small. “Edit by category rather than trying to tackle the entire house in a weekend,” says Kelly. “Start with one drawer, one cabinet, or one closet. Take everything out, clean the space, and put back only what deserves to be there. It's amazing how quickly those small decisions transform the way a home feels. Empty space is a luxury, too!”

Ahead, here’s what design and organizational experts say you should get rid of if you’ve been in your home five years or more.

“Five years of conferences, events, fundraisers, and corporate gifting can leave you with an impressive collection of tote bags, reusable water bottles, and branded merchandise you never actually set out to own,” says Kinsella. Keep what you genuinely like and use, and pass along the extras.

Most manuals live online now, and that tangle of chargers likely powers nothing in your house. Recycle them responsibly, says Lightfoot.

“Threadbare towels, mismatched sheets, and extra bedding you never reach for take up valuable storage space,” says Kelly. “Keep a thoughtfully curated collection of beautiful, functional linens and recycle the worn-out ones where possible.”

“If your spin shoes haven't been touched since lockdown and the exercise bike has become ‘the chair’ in your bedroom, you may be dedicating valuable space to a workout routine that's no longer part of your life,” says Kinsella. Sell or donate equipment you no longer use so you have room for the activities you truly enjoy now.

Craft kits, furniture you planned to refinish, the bread maker and so on: “If someday hasn’t arrived in five years, give yourself permission to let it go, guilt-free,” says Lightfoot.

“Those little objects that have been sitting on a shelf for years can become visual clutter,” says Kelly. “I love a meaningful collection, but every piece should earn its place. Sometimes removing three things makes the one beautiful object remaining look even better.”

“That rug or wall art you swore would work ‘somewhere’ has spent five years leaning against a wall. It’s time to release it to a home where it fits,” says Lightfoot.

Hang on to a small labeled paint sample for touch-ups—and maybe one tile. But the rest, including the dried-up products or the five half-empty cans in the garage or basement, can go to your town’s hazardous waste drop-off, says Lightfoot.

This is a no-judgment zone: “If the avocado slicer, specialty pan, or oversized appliance hasn't made an appearance in years, it probably doesn't deserve prime real estate in your kitchen,” says Kelly. Ditto for duplicate kitchen tools.

“Whether it's a juicer, red light mask, or massage tool, we've all had that new wellness routine we're completely committed to for a few weeks before life gets busy and the habit quietly falls away,” says Kinsella. “The problem is that the gadget doesn't disappear when the enthusiasm does, and five years later, you may have an entire cabinet devoted to routines that lasted a month.” It’s fine to try something new to learn it wasn't for you, but you don't have to keep storing the equipment forever.

Maybe it's an extra accent chair that collects clothes or a side table that's always in the way. Just because something fits physically doesn't mean it belongs. Give your rooms space to breathe and your furniture room to shine, says Kelly.

Purge the old sunscreen, crusty nail polish, expired makeup, shampoo you don’t like, and half-used cleaners that have piled up in your bathroom. Toss them safely and enjoy the instant breathing room, says Lightfoot.

“If you've lived in your home for five years and still have boxes you never unpacked, it's a pretty good indication that you haven't needed most of what's inside on a regular basis,” says Kinsella. “Open the box, decide what's worth keeping, and find those belongings a proper home so you're not continuing to live around a move that’s already happened.”

#6816. It’s Not Too Late to Plant These 5 Perennials for a Gorgeous Fall Garden

Add cheer to gray days with these late-season planting picks.

Consistent wind and rain might not exactly be “gardening weather,” but you can still add color to your garden this autumn. If you have a bare patch to fill, some established perennials can be planted now—as long as the ground isn't waterlogged or frozen.

As autumn deepens and the last of the summer-flowering plants begin to fade, our gardens can start to look a little tired. Add the persistent drizzle and gray skies, and it's easy to feel that the color has gone for the year.

The good news is that there's still time to brighten things up. Established autumn-flowering perennials are a quick and reliable way to bring color and seasonal interest to your garden, and a mild, wet spell can help newly planted roots settle in.

The key is to check the conditions first: avoid planting directly into waterlogged soil, which can suffocate plants, or when the ground is frozen. If the soil is too wet to work, choose pots with good drainage and keep them somewhere sheltered from strong winds.

As Charlotte McGrattan, hardy nursery stock buyer at Hillier Garden Centers, adds, newly planted perennials “require watering well while they establish.” Rain can help with that, but check the soil rather than assuming your plants have had enough: containers, in particular, may still need watering.

Here, McGrattan shares five autumn-flowering perennials you can plant now.

Heleniums are tough, hardy, and easy to grow. If your soil is very wet or compacted, it's best to plant them in a pot positioned in a sheltered spot away from the wind.

“Heleniums add an abundance of color, bursting from mid-summer through into autumn. There are hundreds of varieties to choose from, from hot yellows and reds to vibrant oranges,” McGrattan says.

In these mild temperatures, it's also not too late to plant heliopsis, also known as “false sunflower.” “They not only make impressive, colorful features in borders and beds, attracting an array of pollinators but can also make for lovely cut flowers,” says McGrattan.

“They grow in clumps with branching stems that give a bushy habit, and the flowering period is between six and eight weeks. To encourage buds to form, deadhead any spent flowers.”

While spring is generally the best time to plant penstemons—which are available in shades of purple, pink, white, and blue—you can still plant them in pots now.

“With some varieties being bi-colors combining two shades, they bring welcome color to the garden,” says McGrattan. “Penstemon thrives in well-drained soil and is very tolerant to drought conditions.”

Crocosmias are a great way to bring both height and vibrant color to your autumn borders, as long as you plant established plants with roots instead of growing them from corms (bulbs). “Originating from South Africa, they require fertile, moist, but well-drained soil,” McGrattan adds.

You can also still plant potted Japanese anemones in late autumn, provided the ground is neither frozen nor waterlogged. “Japanese anemones boast cup-shaped pink or white flowers on the top of tall stems and can spread rapidly, so can be a great means of filling borders,” says McGrattan.

#6813. The 11 Best Fragrances Released This October

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The new smoky, spicy scents you need to know about.

I love fragrance. I love fall. Put the two together, and I’m in olfactory heaven. I’ve been excited to put this roundup together since the first new spooky scents started hitting shelves early last month—as we get into November, it’s evident that October will be a tough one to follow up.

There’s plenty of spice and smoke in the air, plus rich gourmands that feel tailor-made for colder weather. There’s even a new fragrance collection in partnership with Robert Eggers’s latest gothic horror film, for anyone who’s ever wanted to smell like they could turn into a werewolf at any moment—or, well, just want to smell like they’ve been at a bonfire. Ready to find your next fall scent? Read on for 11 exciting new fragrances you can get now.

If you’re looking for a new go-to fragrance for fall and winter, let it be Commodity Spice. Like a hot cup of chai, this expressive fragrance is warm and smooth with a rich cocktail of spices including saffron, nutmeg, clove, cardamom, and cinnamon. If I close my eyes, I feel like I just stepped into a cozy little tea and spices shop, and I want to wear that feeling like a sweater.

When Fulton & Roark released Cathedral of Ruins, it quickly became one of our favorite cold-weather smoky scents. Not only does it have a super unique aroma of smoldering frankincense, black pepper, rose, and woods, but it has a heartbreakingly beautiful origin story—the brand’s founders were inspired to create the scent after visiting the abandoned City Methodist Church in Gary, Indiana. The fragrance is an homage to the religious rituals that once took place in that church.

Think of a buttery croissant; a butterscotch candy; a cinnamon roll with sweet, creamy frosting. Those are the vibes of Gap’s newest fragrance. A late addition to the newly (re)launched collection of EDPs, it joins the group as the first gourmand—just in time for fall. Oh, and if you like how Bliss smells, you can complete the collection with the scented hand cream and body lotion.

I was always under the impression that fruity fragrances and woody ones had very little to do with each other and that, for the most part, they should stay separate. That’s until I smelled Maison d’Etto’s Pinehurst. Here, fruity notes of rambutan, blood orange, and ripe pineapple provide a beautiful overlay to base notes of leather, oud, and other woods. It’s a radiant scent that works year-round, day and night.

David Moltz (the “D.S.” of D.S. & Durga) was inspired to create this Resurrected Corpse Flower fragrance after visiting the Brooklyn Botanical Garden during the height of a rare blooming of a corpse flower. What he smelled was, in his words, “like a baby diaper pail in a rotten fish tank full of rubber lily bulbs & olives.” But then he got to thinking, what if that scent could be cleaned of all its rotten odors and reborn into something beautiful? Thus, Resurrected Corpse Flower was born. With green olive, lily, tuberose, freesia, and deep bog cedar, it’s a truly special scent that’s at once floral and swampy.

Because this fragrance is so unlike anything I’ve ever smelled before, it might be a bit polarizing; I’m sure some of you will love it, and others might prefer one of D.S. & Durga’s other unique fragrances. But after leaving this scent on my skin for a bit, I really started to appreciate it.

In contrast to some of the deep, rich gourmands on this list, Lumira’s Soléor offers a lighter, sun-soaked gourmand option. Instead of creamy sweetness, it’s more of a soft, sensual sweetness that isn’t too heavy or overpowering. Smooth amber and candied fruit up top give it a bit of a citrusy kick, which fades into caramel, vanilla, and woods. It works for fall and winter just as well as it does for spring and summer.

If you do want something rich and creamy, Phlur’s Heavy Cream is the way to go. It’s an ambery gourmand with notes of vanilla mousse, white orchid, and creamy musk, which are balanced out by hints of sparkling lemon and orange flower, adding a little bit of lightness to the otherwise heavy fragrance. And, of course, with a name like Heavy Cream, there has to be a coordinating whipped body butter for the full experience.

How do you make decadence refined? You create a rich, vanilla gourmand and add the sophisticated warmth of Peru balsam. It’s definitely a sweet scent, and the combination of Cristal vanilla, bourbon vanilla, tonka bean, and black sugar creates the aroma of a classic crème brûlée. But once you get past the creamy sweetness of it all, you’re left with a sensual, balsamic scent that lingers.

I didn’t know a fragrance could smell so much like Halloween, but Heretic teamed up with Robert Eggers’ new movie Werwulf and did just that. At first, you get spice and smoke from cardamom, smoke, and leather; it then moves into something woody, earthy, and animalic with fir balsam, labdanum, patchouli, moss, and damp earth. The “suggestion of wet fur” is what really makes this scent stand out—and what gives it that spooky edge. Wear with your scariest Halloween costume.

In a departure from the mostly spicy, rich, gourmand scents that hit shelves this October, Drake’s Better World Fragrance House partnered with October’s Very Own for a fresh, citrusy fragrance that gives the impression of a refreshing white wine sangria with notes of passion fruit, rose, white cedarwood, and roasted tonka bean. But in its spherical burgundy glass bottle, it’ll blend in perfectly with your other fall and winter scents.

Roll-on fragrance oils are all the rage right now, and new fragrance house Gamine has already come out with its own collection of fragrance oils called Microdose. With six parfum oils, you can wear each individually or combine two or three for your own unique scent. My favorite of the bunch is DMT, a metallic fragrance built on notes of burnt latex, aerated foam, and hot steel. I wasn’t sure I would like it based on the description—and the fact that I’d never heard of a metallic scent before—but I’m actually obsessed with it.

#6812. I Went to the World's Biggest Furniture Trade Show—These 6 Trends Are About to Be Everywhere

The furniture profiles, patterns, and palettes you’re about to see everywhere.

Twice a year, High Point, North Carolina, becomes the home industry’s giant showroom, where furniture and design brands unveil new collections for retailers, interior designers, and buyers to see and source. At House Beautiful, we’re your boots on the ground, looking for the ideas that may travel from showroom to store—and into your home next.

This fall, Market made a case for decorating with more character and muddier hues: green moved from the forest floor to the showroom floor, florals claimed entire sofas, and upholstery played with everything from a flash of leg to a full fabric wrap. Add wood-backed furniture as an accent, garden-inspired lighting, and a generous swish of fringe, and the message was clear: The details are ready to take center stage.

This year, green was the unofficial color of Market. Benjamin Moore and Behr had already put the hue on the radar with their 2027 Colors of the Year: Webster Green, a confident mid-tone balancing blue’s coolness with yellow’s warmth, and Grounded, a mossy olive softened by earthy brown undertones. Then came the showroom echoes. Versions of green, from earthy vegetable shades to deeper, moodier tones, appeared nearly everywhere. Kathy Kuo even created a “Music Room” (shown in our opening image) for Rowe Furniture inspired by the verdant shade. The appeal is easy to understand: Green brings the natural world indoors, lending a sense of calm and grounding a space without draining it of personality.

The design industry’s obsession with prints is now a love letter to botanicals. Florals erupted across Market in a myriad of scales, from small, almost sun-bleached botanicals to overscaled, tapestry-adjacent blooms. Even the tiniest prints made a surprisingly bold statement when carried across an entire sofa; the larger blooms brought the feel of a faded heirloom into full view. Both have that grandmotherly, another-era quality, but the impact is anything but timid. Yes, it’s a major design commitment, but why play it safe with a solid, barely-there pattern? It may not be for the faint of design, but we are all in a sofa fully dressed in flowers.

Here’s a furniture detail that changes the whole silhouette: Brands are letting wood take over the outside back—or sides—of upholstered seating. Baker’s sofa makes the move hard to miss, with a broad wood shell framing the piece. It’s a little like “inside-out” upholstery, where the outside gets its own treatment, but here the wood gives the piece architectural shape and presence. As floor plans open up and sofas float in family and living rooms, the back is no longer hidden against a wall. It’s part of the view, and part of how the seating area takes shape, making it all the more important that it feels worth looking at.

Upholstery continues to take its cues from fashion, with a focus on mixing silhouettes: Mini skirts that let those gams show; full, ballooning skirts with all the best parts of an ’80s prom dress; and what we lovingly call body-con upholstery, where the entire chair is wrapped head to toe in one fabric.

The appeal goes beyond a good fashion metaphor, though. Each treatment changes the chair’s proportions: a flash of leg keeps it light, a generous skirt adds volume, and an all-over wrap turns both seat and base into one continuous form, with no leg finish to choose or worry about. Each has its own distinct style POV, making your shopping choices that much more complicated, but in the best way.

If garden motifs aren’t your thing, don’t look up. The floral revival isn’t limited to upholstery and wallpaper; it’s reaching the ceiling, too. Visual Comfort & Co.’s Fairoaks collection, designed by Fisher Weisman, includes a chandelier, sconce, and mini flush mount. Sculptural branches and twigs cleverly disguise the chandelier’s ceiling cord, preserving the romance of the design. At Hudson Valley Lighting, a tulip-inspired chandelier brings a more literal bloom overhead. Both offer a way to bring the garden indoors through shape and silhouette, without committing an entire room to pattern.

Sometimes, all it takes is a little bullion fringe. The thick, tightly twisted cords fall in a dense, weighty row, adding plush texture and a satisfying swish to a skirted chair or sofa. It nods to the Victorian parlor, but stops short of period-drama excess; against a cleaner silhouette or in a spare room, the old-world trim feels playful and unexpectedly fresh. Consider it the design equivalent of a great pair of earrings: a little extra, in exactly the right place.

“Some weeks will be better than others, and that’s okay. I’m only human,” she wrote, in part.

Maura Higgins is opening up after tackling her Disney Night choreography as Tinker Bell on Dancing With the Stars. The Love Island alum got candid in a lengthy statement she penned alongside a carousel of behind-the-scenes photos from Monday night’s episode after fans criticized her performance in Team Slaytopia’s group number.

“This week was definitely one of the toughest I’ve had so far. I was really unwell, running on very little energy and trying to learn two dances, but I gave it absolutely everything I had,” she started her message, adding that she had no previous dance experience before joining DWTS.“I wanted to challenge myself, step outside my comfort zone and give something completely new my absolute best & that’s exactly what I’m trying to do every single week. I’m learning, I’m making mistakes, I’m pushing myself and I’m growing along the way!! Some weeks will be better than others, and that’s okay,” she continued, adding, “I’m only human.”She insisted she wasn’t sharing the message for “sympathy or to make excuses, but simply to share a little of the reality behind the scenes.”

The Traitors star uplifted her pro partner and “biggest support throughout this journey,” Mark Ballas, before seemingly addressing harsh feedback from viewers who criticized her “low energy” performances in both of her numbers this week.

“Genuinely what is up with maura tonight…she was the clear weak link of that team dance,” one person wrote, while another added, “i'm going to be very honest with you i genuinely think maura did by far the worst of any celebrity tonight and the judges were just distracted by how good of a show mark is able to put on that they didn't notice lol.”

Per her message, Maura seems to be focusing on those who continue to uplift and support her throughout the competition.

“To everyone who continues to support me, encourage me and cheer me on, please know how much it means,” she wrote toward the end of her statement. “You genuinely give me the strength to keep going, especially on the harder days. Your kindness never goes unnoticed.”

She then closed her post by expressing how “grateful” she is to still be in the running for the iconic Mirrorball trophy.

Based on that alone, I tink she’ll be just fine either way!
